State of the Market 2025 

x 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

1  EXECUTIVE SUMMARY AND RECOMMENDATIONS 

Two of the themes that have been identified in previous years – especially resource adequacy 

challenges and escalating load growth – persist in 2025. Growing energy needs and changing 

load patterns related to data centers supporting AI technology and crypto mines continue to 

impact markets. Market storage resources and variable energy resources continue to play an 

increasing role in the Southwest Power Pool (SPP) markets. Natural gas prices have climbed into 

the $3/MMBtu range for 2025 after being around $2/MMBtu in the prior two years. Overall, the 

MMU continues to find the SPP market to be competitive in 2025. 

Some key observations in the SPP Integrated Marketplace during 2025: 

•  Energy consumption increased. Monthly average system energy consumption was up 

3% compared to 2024 levels. Most of this increase can be attributed to load growth 

rather than factors such as weather. 

•  The interconnection queue is adjusting to changing dynamics. At the end of 2025, 

generation capacity from renewable, storage, and hybrid resources accounts for the vast 

majority of proposed generation interconnection queue, at nearly 114 GW (76%) of the 

nearly 149 GW in the queue. These resource types accounted for 89% of the queue at 

the end of 2024. However, an increase in gas generation interconnection requests was 

experienced in 2025, rising from 14 GW at the end of 2024 to 35 GW at the end of 2025. 

A significant portion, almost 9 GW, of this increase in interconnection requests for gas 

resources can be attributed to submittals into the expedited resource adequacy study 

(ERAS). 

•  The generation mix continued to evolve. For the fourth straight year, and five years out 

of the past six, wind generation produced the highest percentage of total generation, at 

37% in 2025, down from 38% in 2024. Coal generation climbed from 25% of total 

generation in 2024 to 29% in 2025, while gas generation dropped over the same period. 

•  Addition of wind resources has slowed. In 2025, 1,126 MW of wind generation capacity 

was added to the market, ending 2025 with 35,934 MW of nameplate wind capacity. 

Wind generation capacity now accounts for 34% of installed nameplate capacity in the 

SPP market. However, the generation interconnection queue still contains about 30 GW 

of new wind resources to potentially be added to the market. 

•  Addition of solar resources has accelerated. Total registered solar nameplate capacity at 

the end of 2025 was 2,114 MW, an increase from 986 MW at the end of 2024 and 484 

MW at the end of 2023. 

State of the Market 2025 

1 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

•  Demand response capacity increased. At the end of 2025, there was 987 MW of 

dispatchable demand response resources of installed nameplate capacity representing 

130 resources. This is an increase of 19 MW nameplate capacity, and a net gain of nine 

resources. 

•  Market-to-market payments from MISO were down. Net market-to-market payments 

from MISO to SPP were $86 million in 2025 compared to $112 million in 2024. 

•  Virtual profits increased. Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was 

$1.29/MW in 2025, up from $1.25/MW in 2024. 

•  Resource adequacy concerns remain a top priority. While the availability and 

performance of our accredited resources are likely improving during winter weather 

events, SPP must continue to focus on addressing capacity risk during these events. In 

addition, sometimes SPP must declare resource advisories or conservative operations 

during periods of mild weather when a combination of outages and a lower wind output 

have led to tight conditions, and the outage availability process needs refinement.  

•  Capacity on outage increased. Total outages and derates for capacity taken out-of-

service decreased by 20% from 2024 to 2025, with forced outages and derates increasing 

by 31% and maintenance outages and derates decreasing by 4%. 

•  Self-committed capacity remained flat. The average percentage of day-ahead market 

capacity by commitment status shows that self-commitment stayed flat at 15% of 

capacity, while market status dropped slightly from 55% of committed capacity in 2024 

to 54% in 2025. Capacity on outage dropped as well, from 21% in 2024 to 20% in 2025. 

Capacity in not-participating status was the only category to increase from 2024 to 2025, 

from 7% to 9%. 

•  Gas prices increased. The average gas price for 2025 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was 

$2.97/MMBtu, an increase of 50% from 2024 when the average price was $1.98/MMBtu. 

•  Day-ahead market and real-time market prices were up. The average hourly day-ahead 

price increased by 13% from $30.43/MWh in 2024 to $34.40/MWh in 2025 and the real-

time price increased by 16% from $28.34/MWh in 2024 to $32.78/MWh in 2025. 

•  Negative price intervals decreased. The frequency of negative price intervals dropped in 

the day-ahead market from 9.9% of intervals in 2024 to 7.6% in 2025. In the real-time 

market negative price intervals decreased from 15.2% in 2024 to 12.7% in 2025. Most of 

the decrease can be attributed to decreased congestion year-over-year. 

State of the Market 2025 

2 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

•  Make-whole payments for the day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment 

were essentially unchanged. Although the breakdown of make-whole payments by 

technology type changed from 2024 to 2025, the totals for both day-ahead and 

reliability unit commitment make-whole payments were only $200,000 less in 2025 

compared to 2024. Overall, reliability unit commitment make-whole payments accounted 

for 54% of total make-whole payments.  

•  Revenue neutrality uplift increased. Revenue neutrality uplift climbed from $163 million 

in 2024, to $290 million in 2025. Most of the increase can be attributed to a rise in the 

real-time congestion component of RNU and a decrease in the joint operating 

agreement component of RNU. 

•  Regional congestion patterns remained the same. The lowest average day-ahead 

marginal congestion costs occurred around western Kansas, western Oklahoma, and 

Nebraska. The highest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs exist in two spots in 

Oklahoma, one near the Kansas border and one near the Texas border, with other areas 

of high costs in southeast North Dakota, eastern New Mexico, and southwest Missouri. 

•  Congestion payments decreased. Total congestion payments for 2025 were over $1.6 

billion, down from $1.9 billion in 2024 but up from $1.4 billion in 2023. 

•  Transmission congestion rights funding fell outside of the targeted range. 

Transmission congestion rights (TCR) funding in 2025 once again fell outside the target 

range of 90% to 100%, coming in at 85%, up from the 83% seen in 2024 but down from 

the 88% seen in 2023.  

•  Overall, hedging covered congestion costs. In aggregate, load-serving entities were 

over-hedged at 158% of their congestion cost. 

• 

Individual congesting hedging ranged in performance. Individual market participants 

hedged congestion with varying degrees of effectiveness. Overall, 79% of load-serving 

entities recovered all of their congestion costs through the auction revenue right and 

transmission congestion right auctions. 

•  Auction revenue right funding increased. Auction revenue right (ARR) funding increased 

to 128%, up from 125% in 2024 and 118% in 2023. Relatedly, the ARR surplus increased 

by just over $58 million year-over-year. 

•  SPP markets remain competitive. Structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP 

markets have been competitive over the last several years. The market share, Herfindahl-

State of the Market 2025 

3 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal supplier analyses all indicate minimal to moderate 

potential structural market power in SPP markets outside of frequently constrained areas. 

•  Offer mitigation frequency in the day-ahead market dropped for operating reserves 

and no-load and were essentially unchanged for energy. Overall, levels remain very low 

at less than 0.75% across energy, no-load, and operating reserve intervals. In 2025, the 

application of mitigation in the day-ahead market occurred at the following frequencies: 

no-load (0.52%), operating reserves (0.02%), and energy (0.04%). The low level of 

mitigated resource hours relative to market activity remains a hallmark of the day-ahead 

market.  

•  Mitigation frequency for incremental energy in the real-time market remains low. For 

the real-time market, the annual mitigation frequency of incremental energy remains 

low, at 0.55% in 2025, down from 0.61% in both 2023 and 2024.  

•  The exercise of market power remains rare. Behavioral metrics suggest that attempts to 

actually exercise market power by manipulating the price (economic withholding) or 

quantity (physical withholding) of generation are rare. The output gap, an inference of 

economically withheld generation, dropped by about 0.1 percentage point in 2025. 

State of the Market 2025 

4 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

1.1  EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 

  OVERVIEW 

Executive summary and recommendations 

During 2025, there were three themes that dominated the SPP market: rising gas prices, 

increasing load growth, and (relatedly) continued resource adequacy concerns.  

The average gas cost at the Panhandle hub increased by 50% from 2024 to 2025, while the day-

ahead average price increased by 13% and the real-time average price increased by 16%. 

Typically, gas costs and electricity prices are highly correlated over time, however, the 

correlation is not 1-to-1, and we have observed this correlation weakening slightly in recent 

years. In most years, the gas cost and the electricity price move in the same direction, however, 

the magnitude of change can differ. This can be due to changing congestion patterns, increasing 

load, and other market impacts.  

As with previous years, energy costs remain to be the largest component of total wholesale 

costs, which represented 97% of total costs in 2025. Historically, the percentage has remained 
around 96% since the start of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014.1  

The annual five-minute peak demand of 54,850 MW in 2025 was just 0.2% higher than 2024, 

however, total electricity consumption was up by 3%. Of the 4,048 MW new nameplate 

generation capacity added in 2025, 1,368 MW was from wind resources, 1,129 MW from solar 

resources, 713 MW from gas resources, 510 MW from dispatchable demand response, and 328 
MW from market storage resources.2  

After climbing from 2023 to 2024, wind generation, as a percentage of total generation, had a 

slight dip, decreasing from 38% of total generation in 2024 to 37% in 2025. However, total wind 

generation was virtually unchanged from 2024 to 2025. After a decreasing trend from 2021, coal 

generation increased in 2025, representing 29% of total generation, up from 25% in 2024, likely 

due to the rise in gas prices. 

  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME MARKET PERFORMANCE 

Overall, both day-ahead and real-time energy prices were up in 2025 compared to 2024. The 

average day-ahead price for 2025 was $34.40/MWh, an increase of 13% from $30.43/MWh in 

2024. The average real-time price was $32.78/MWh for 2025, an increase of 16% from 

$28.34/MWh in 2024. Much of this increase can be attributed to the higher gas costs during 

1 The 2021 winter weather event was an outlier; make-whole payments reached over $1 billion, and the 
percentage decreased to 85%. 
2 The sum of the nameplate capacity of added resources in 2025 is higher than the net amount of 
nameplate capacity added due to retirements and rating changes of other resources in the market. 

State of the Market 2025 

5 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

2025. As mentioned in the key observations, the average gas price for 2025 at the Panhandle 

Eastern hub was $2.97/MMBtu, an increase of 50% from 2024 when the average price was 

$1.98/MMBtu. 

Load participation in the day-ahead market rose slightly in 2025. The average monthly level of 

participation for the load assets was between 97% and 99% of the actual real-time load, with the 

annual average of 98.0%, up slightly from 97.6% in 2024. 

On average for 2025, wind generation was 2,945 MW higher in the real-time market compared 

to the amount scheduled in the day-ahead market on an hourly basis. This disconnect 

represents a persistent and escalating challenge to the market as wind generation continues its 

expansion. Virtual bids and offers may theoretically offset the under-scheduling of renewable 

supply in the day-ahead market. However, in net they did not, as they averaged 1,635 MW of 

net virtual supply. Furthermore, it is important to recognize that even if virtual transactions were 

to match the quantity of under-scheduled renewables, the prices associated with the virtual 

offers are not likely to fully represent the expected offer prices of renewable resources. 

In general, virtual transactions were profitable in the SPP market. Gross profit before fees was 

$261 million in 2025, up from $209 million in 2024. When charges and transaction fees are 

included, the net profit for virtual transactions was $123 million in 2025, up from $99 million in 

2024. 

Generation offered in the day-ahead market averaged just over 54% in market commitment 

status followed by self-commitment status at 15% of the total capacity commitments for 2025. 

While self-commitments were unchanged from 2024, market commitments dropped slightly 

from 55% in 2024. Capacity on outage also dropped, from 21% in 2024 to 20% in 2023. 

  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION AND HEDGING  

Locational marginal prices reflect the sum of the marginal cost of energy, the marginal cost of 

congestion, and the marginal cost of losses for each pricing interval at any given pricing location 

in the market. Certain locations of the footprint experience significant price movements 

resulting from congestion caused by high wind generation, external flows, and transmission 

limitations. 

The lowest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs occurred around western Kansas, 

western Oklahoma, and Nebraska. The highest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs 

exist in two spots in Oklahoma, one near the Kansas border and one near the Texas border, with 

other areas of high costs in southeast North Dakota, eastern New Mexico, and southwest 

Missouri. 

State of the Market 2025 

6 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

The 2025 frequently constrained area (FCA) analysis identified Oklahoma City, Tulsa, Lubbock, 

and Kansas City as FCAs. The Williston, North Dakota area was recommended for removal as an 

FCA. The period covered by the 2025 FCA analysis is March 1, 2024, through February 28. 2025. 

Total congestion payments for 2025 were over $1.6 billion, down from $1.9 billion in 2024 but 

up from $1.4 billion in 2023. 

While most load-serving entities were able to successfully hedge their congestion exposure with 

auction revenue rights (ARRs) and transmission congestion rights (TCRs), a handful of 

participants were under-hedged. The largest individual amount over-hedged was $89 million, 

while the largest amount under-hedged was $67 million. 

  UPLIFT COSTS 

Generators receive make-whole payments to ensure that they receive sufficient revenue to cover 

energy, start-up, no-load, and operating reserve costs for both market and local reliability 

commitments. A make-whole payment is an additional market payment that supplements a 

resource’s revenue if prices fall below its cleared offer. These payments are intended to make 

resources whole to energy, commitment, and operating reserve costs. 

For 2025, combined day-ahead and reliability unit commitment (RUC) make-whole payments 

totaled $257 million. RUC make-whole payments represented 54% of the total, with day-ahead 

make-whole payments accounting for the other 46%. 

Day-ahead make-whole payments were nearly $119 million in 2025, only slightly lower than the 

amount in 2024 – a decrease of 0.35%. Conversely, reliability unit commitment make-whole 

payments in 2025 were almost $138 million, up just over $200,000 from the value in 2024, 

representing a 0.16% increase. 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU) ensures settlement payments and receipts for each real-time 

hourly settlement interval equals zero. Positive RNU indicates that SPP received insufficient 

revenue, initiating additional collections from market participants. Inversely, negative RNU 

indicates that SPP received excess revenue, which must then be credited back to market 

participants. 

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2025 was $290 million, up 78% from $163 million in 

2024. The main components of revenue neutrality uplift are revenue inadequacy (which includes 

congestion), and real-time joint operating agreement (also known as market-to-market). The 

joint operating agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue neutrality uplift, while 

the revenue inadequacy components generally act to increase revenue neutrality uplift. Real-

time joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality uplift was just $86 million in 2025, 

State of the Market 2025 

7 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

a decrease of 24% from $113 million in 2024. The revenue inadequacy components of revenue 

neutrality uplift totaled $360 million in 2025, an increase of 40% from $257 million in 2024. 

  COMPETITIVE ASSESSMENT 

Overall, structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP markets have been competitive 

over the last several years. The market share, Herfindahl–Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal 

supplier analysis indicate minimal to moderate potential structural market power in SPP markets, 

outside of frequently congested areas.  

In 19% of the hours in 2025, the market share of the largest on-line supplier in terms of real-

time energy output exceeded 20%, up from 14% in 2024. This disrupts the trend of decreasing 

concentration observed since the June 2018 formation of Evergy, Inc. as SPP’s largest market 

participant. Meanwhile, another general measure of structural market power, the Herfindahl-

Hirschman Index (HHI) calculation for supplier concentration, shows that 2% of hours were 

considered moderately concentrated in 2025, the same amount as in 2024. The SPP market 

remained mostly unconcentrated—with 90% of all intervals since 2019 considered 

unconcentrated. No intervals were considered highly concentrated in 2025, and, like 2024, the 

percentage of intervals considered unconcentrated (98%) was at its highest level since 2017. 

Structural market power in the SPP footprint creates the potential for market manipulation. The 

MMU maintains its belief that the existing local market power mitigation measures are 

sufficiently robust to moderate the impact of an actual exercise of that potential, should it occur. 

The MMU will continue to evaluate structural market power concerns going forward. 

Any exercise of market power is most likely to be profitable in transmission-constrained areas 

where concerns regarding potential local market power are highest. MMU analysis and 

continued scrutiny of potential areas under frequent constraints confirm that existing mitigation 

measures are effective to mitigate the exercise of local market power. 

Behavioral indicators were also assessed by analyzing the conduct of market participants and 

the impact of that conduct on market prices, to detect the exercise of market power. The MMU 

examined offer price markups, offer quantities, mitigation frequency, and measures of implied 

economic and physical withholding by evaluating market behavior. The MMU noted overall 

improvements in the convergence of market price and imputed costs but still observes negative 

prices at a level and frequency that warrants continued note. In particular, wind units had 

exceptionally low markups – a concern that the MMU has raised in prior Annual State of the 

Market reports. Off-peak and on-peak annual average markups continued to be in negative 

territory in 2025, albeit with some increasing trends, with the off-peak period being more 

pronounced.  

State of the Market 2025 

8 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

Positive markups were observed for coal generation resources at slightly increasing levels 

relative to the positive markups seen in 2024, but lower than 2023. The annual average coal 

markup levels were $5.81 in 2025, $4.29 in 2024, and $6.88/MWh in 2023, respectively. Negative 

on-peak and off-peak markups were observed in most summer months for natural gas 

resources in 2025, showing a clear correlation to the prevailing price of gas within the SPP 

footprint, particularly related to instances in which gas prices are negative. 

Nonetheless, all months saw negative markups for wind resources, with markup levels 

continuing to stay low throughout the year, to levels generally higher than those seen in 2024. 

The average markups for wind resources were −$37.81 in 2025, −$37.99/MWh in 2024, and 

−$41.50/MWh in 2023, respectively.  

Compared with the previous year, 2025’s output gap dropped to 0.05% from 0.14% in 2024. 

Overall, the output gap remains at very low levels, averaging less than 0.1% in all months of 

2025 and reflects a high level of participation in the market overall. 

Incremental energy offer mitigation in 2025 slightly increased in frequency in the real-time 

market however, incremental, energy offer mitigation continues to remain very rare, at 0.04% 

and 0.05% of resource hours in the day-ahead and real-time markets, respectively. Overall, 

mitigation frequency levels remain very low at less than 0.75% across energy, no-load, and 

operating reserve intervals.  

Overall, the SPP Integrated Marketplace provides effective market incentives and mitigation 

measures to produce competitive market outcomes, particularly during market intervals where 

the exercise of local market power is a concern. While some behavioral indicators slightly 

increased relative to low prior-year levels, these largely reflected conditions external to the 

market, and structural indicators showed continued levels of diverse and healthy competition. 

Market behaviors and results remained workably competitive overall, only infrequently requiring 

mitigation of local market power to achieve those outcomes. Nonetheless, mitigation of 

economic withholding remains an essential tool in ensuring that market results are competitive 

during periods when such market conditions offer suppliers the potential to abuse local market 

power. 

  RESOURCE ADEQUACY CONCERNS 

Addressing resource adequacy is one of the most critical issues facing the SPP region. While it 

has improved significantly over the last few years, SPP’s supply adequacy approach does not 

sufficiently address supply-related risks. The weaknesses in SPP’s current resource adequacy 

construct are most apparent during the winter storms over the last several years, but resource 

adequacy issues are not confined to winter. The region still faces low margins between 

State of the Market 2025 

9 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

accredited capacity and load obligation during periods in the summer, and resource advisories 

and conservative operations have been declared during periods of mild weather when a 

combination of outages and lower wind output have led to tight conditions.  

The MMU also sees a continued need for better integration between the outage coordination 

process and resource adequacy. The MMU has recently observed times during the shoulder 

months when capacity was tight as resources took maintenance outages. Additionally, with the 

implementation of performance based accreditation, the MMU has started to see a decrease in 

forced outages during resource adequacy seasons. While this is a boon for availability, the MMU 

is concerned that the current resource adequacy construct does not sufficiently address the 

need for resources to have ample time to undergo maintenance. The MMU believes that any 

next step in bolstering resource adequacy must include improving the outage coordination 

process to maximize resources’ ability to take necessary outages.  

The MMU continues to see fuel supply issues drive limited availability during more extreme 

weather. While almost all fuel types experienced some issues during the winter weather events, 

by far some of the most impactful are natural gas resources in the SPP region experiencing a 

high level of outages related to fuel supply limitations. Though SPP may not experience adverse 

weather events every year, correlations in forced outages due to fuel supply shocks need to be 

incorporated into any resource adequacy approach to ensure a more accurate accounting of the 

expected availability of resources. Correlated forced outages were partially addressed in the fuel 

assurance methodology approved last year but it only addressed fuel-related forced outages 

that are incremental to the assumed level of forced outages. Additionally, fuel assurance only 

applies to fuel-related forced outages during the winter season. The LOLE study does not 

address the fact that correlated outages in conventional resources are not accounted for in 

accreditation and, as a result, the reliability cost of these outages will continue to be socialized 

to the region. 

1.2  RECOMMENDATIONS 

One of the primary responsibilities of a market monitoring unit is evaluating market rules and 

design features to improve market efficiency and effectiveness. When an issue is identified with 

the market, the MMU will study the issue and its potential causes and make recommendations 

to remedy the issue. The MMU is making three new recommendations for 2025, as well as 

highlighting three existing recommendations for issues that have not yet been fully addressed.in 

an effort to promote the continued need for these issues to be addressed. 

State of the Market 2025 

10 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

  NEW RECOMMENDATIONS FOR 2025 

2025.1 

Improve generation capacity availability 

Generation capacity availability is a prerequisite for sustaining a competitive market as it 

provides the necessary liquidity to support market transactions and system reliability, helps 

sustain efficient price formation, and sends correct price signals to avoid/defer additional need 

for investment in generation and transmission capacity. When installed generation capacity is 

unoffered or otherwise becomes unavailable, it can unnecessarily push market clearing prices 

upward by creating artificial scarcity and increased congestion.  

The MMU’s 2025 white paper “Unavailable Generation Capacity in SPP Markets: Causes and 
Impacts”3 concludes that most of the unavailable capacity is unavailable due to behavioral 

choices—on how much to offer or make available— made by market participants. The study 

recommended market rules should be examined to review the causes of unavailability and 

identify mechanisms to incentivize or require at least some of this capacity to be available.  

The study found that on average, roughly 10% (6,000 MW) of rated conventional capacity is not 

available for economic commitment. Additionally, the report estimates that in total the SPP 

region may have between 8,000 and 9,000 MW of capacity that exists but is not available to the 

market at any given time.  

Based on that study, the MMU recommends that SPP:  

•  Address inefficiencies in the market due to all available generation capacity not being 

made available. 

•  Consider modifications to tariff language that requires resources to economically offer all 

available capacity into the market, reserving emergency operating limits to that capacity 

which is truly only available during a reliability event. 

•  Review the intended purpose of reliability commitment status to determine a limited list 

of circumstances under which a reliability commitment status is acceptable and explicitly 

disallow market participants from using it when a resource cannot be operated due to 

malfunction, lack of staffing, or lack of fuel. Resources declaring reliability status should 

be required to justify the use of it and demonstrate steps taken to ensure they are 

operable and will be available if called on. 

•  Add explicit language in the appropriate governing documents that net generating 

capability represents the physical capabilities of a resource and is not impacted by 

3 Unavailable Generation Capacity in SPP Markets: Causes and Impacts  

State of the Market 2025 

11 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

economic considerations. The outage coordination methodology should use this value 
from which to measure derates.4 

2025.2  Enhance incentives for load forecasting accuracy and ensure non-conforming 

loads are properly registered 

High Impact Large Loads (HILLs) and non-conforming loads are required to submit projections 

of their hourly energy consumption on a rolling seven-day basis. These forecasts are to be used 

by SPP in developing the aggregated short-term and mid-term load forecasts, which are inputs 

into the reliability unit commitment (RUC) processes. However, currently there are no adequate 

incentives for these large loads to submit load forecasts that are accurate, nor for other existing 

non-conforming loads to register as such and submit adequate forecasts. SPP should ensure 

that existing and future loads that exhibit non-conforming behavior are properly registered as 

non-conforming load.  

Inaccurate load forecasts in RUC studies can be detrimental to proper cost causation and market 

efficiency. For example, consider a HILL that cleared 100 MW of demand in the day-ahead 

market but subsequently submits a load forecast of 200 MW for use in a RUC study. The RUC 

process then commits additional generation to serve this incremental projected load. In the real-

time market, however, the load forecast proves to be inaccurate: its real-time load is only 100 

MW and not the 200 MW forecast it had submitted. In this case, the load forecast caused a RUC 

commitment and potentially created make-whole payments, but the load does not incur make-

whole charges because it did not deviate from its day-ahead position. This is inconsistent with 

cost causation and creates inefficient commitments that harm real-time price formation.   

SPP should create strong incentives for both existing non-conforming loads and new loads, such 

as large loads, to provide accurate load forecasts for RUC studies. There are non-conforming 

loads currently on the SPP system and these loads should be similarly incentivized to submit 

accurate forecasts and update their projections when real-time load deviates from their forecast. 

As HILLs begin to interconnect to the grid, this issue will only become increasingly important to 

address. 

2025.3  Limit self-commitment by demand response resources 

Demand response resources currently have the flexibility to use any commitment or control 

status available to all resources, including the use of a self-commitment status or manual control 

status. When a self-commitment status is used, the market participant is committing the 

resource and SPP will assume that commitment in the relevant study. A manual control status is 

4 See Ch. 5 Conclusions and Recommendations of the December 19, 2025, study for more comprehensive 
list at Unavailable generation capacity in SPP markets: causes and impacts.pdf. 

State of the Market 2025 

12 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

used to indicate that a resource is online but not capable of following a dispatch instruction. 

Self-committing or operating in a manual control status could allow a demand response 

resource to effectively minimize actual opportunity cost while still receiving the full, calculated 

opportunity cost benefit for curtailing. This may result in payments for opportunity costs that are 

not actually incurred, creating a misalignment between calculated and realized economic 

outcomes and reducing market efficiency.  

The MMU recommends that SPP remove the ability for demand response resources to use a 

self-commitment status in the market and clarify when demand response resources can use 

manual control status. These enhancements will provide a higher level of assurance that the 

calculated costs and actual costs are aligned, and improper payments are not made. 

  HIGHLIGHTED PREVIOUS RECOMMENDATIONS  

2024.3 

Identification of affiliates in the market (high) 

The lack of transparency of the affiliates in the SPP markets leads to less efficient market 

surveillance and challenges in detecting potential gaming and market manipulation. The MMU 

recommends SPP implement a design similar to what was in the WEIS market design, and is in 

the Markets+ market design, in which a market participant must identify all of its affiliates within 

the market at the time of registration. Existing participants must timely update their registration 

to reflect current affiliations. The MMU recommends the following definition for affiliate.  

Affiliate: With respect to a corporation, partnership or other entity, each such other corporation, 

partnership or other entity that directly or indirectly, through one or more intermediaries, controls, 

is controlled by, or is under common control with, such corporation, partnership or other entity as 

defined under 18 CFR 35.36(a)(9). 

The MMU filed RR766 to address this recommendation and it is currently moving through the 
stakeholder process.5 The MMU considers this a high priority issue. 

2017.5  Address inefficiency when forecasted resources are under-scheduled day-ahead 

(high) 

The MMU noted in its 2017 annual report that the systematic under-scheduling of wind 

resources in the day-ahead market can contribute to distorted price signals, suppressing real-
time prices and affecting revenue adequacy for all resources.6 The MMU has included this 

recommendation in each annual state of the market report since 2017 and included the 

recommendation in a special section of the 2022 annual report reemphasizing

recommendations from previous reports that have not been addressed.7 Additional details 

regarding the recommendation can be found in those corresponding reports. The MMU

2014.4  Address issues with the day-ahead must offer requirement (high) 

The MMU continues to recommend that SPP and stakeholders eliminate the limited day-ahead 

must-offer provision and revise the physical withholding rules to include a penalty for non-

  OPEN PREVIOUS RECOMMENDATIONS 

The MMU has provided recommendations to improve market design in each of our previous 

annual state of the market reports since the launch of the Integrated Marketplace in 2014. While 

SPP and its stakeholders have found ways to effectively address some of the MMU’s concerns, 

there are still many outstanding recommendations. The MMU would prefer to see more action 

with regard to these outstanding recommendations. 

2024.1  Market-based incentive to ensure daily availability of adequate accredited 

capacity to meet daily load (high) 

From 2021 to 2024, the SPP region has experienced four major winter weather events. During 

those events, SPP spent a total of 335 hours in conservative operations, 44 hours in an Energy 

Emergency Alert (EEA) level 1, 43 hours in EEA level 2, and 9 hours in EEA level 3.  

SPP has made a number of tariff revisions since 2021 to address reliability, including establishing 

a winter season resource adequacy requirement and a separate winter season planning reserve 

margin, which will be implemented for the 2026 winter season. SPP has also filed revisions to 

attachment AA of the SPP Open Access Transmission Tariff (Tariff) with the Federal Energy 

Regulatory Commission (FERC) to better align resource accreditation with past performance. In 

addition to tariff revisions, the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (NERC) has made 

updates to their reliability requirements. In response to the new NERC standards and the more 

frequent severe weather events, resource owners have made material improvements to try to 

ensure increased availability and reliability of their resources. 

Since 2021, there has been a marginal increase in overall resource performance, particularly 

during severe weather events, but the overall reliability in the region remains challenged due to 

load growth. Additionally, there is no immediate incentive, through the resource adequacy 

72022 SPP Annual State of the Market Report, Chapter 7, 7.2 Empasis on Previous Recommendations  

State of the Market 2025 

14 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

construct, for a resource to be available to serve load on any given day. Therefore, to ensure 

reliability and an efficient, competitive market, the MMU recommends the development of a 

market incentive for resources to meet regional load obligations on a daily basis. This incentive 

should focus on accredited resources but be structured to benefit any resource when there is a 

shortage of available accredited resources. The MMU prefers a market-based solution to capture 

the efficiency gains from a competitive market. Alternatively, a non-market solution could be 

used to incentivize capacity to be available to serve load obligations on a daily basis. A non-

market solution would need to mirror the accreditation process with an ex-post review of the 

availability of accredited resources across the season with emphasis on the tightest periods. The 

MMU considers this a high priority issue. 

2024.2  Sufficiency valuation curve transparency (medium) 

A critical piece of the supply adequacy process is an effective incentive structure to ensure LREs 

meet their individual Resource Adequacy Requirements (RARs). The MMU, in collaboration with 

SPP and stakeholders, designed the Sufficiency Valuation Curve (SVC) as an alternative to the 

standard deficiency payment when a deficiency can be attributed to a sudden and significant 

change in the Planning Reserve Margin (PRM). While the standard deficiency payment charges a 

multiple of the Cost of New Entry (CONE) to incentivize new entry, the SVC instead estimates the 

value of excess accredited capacity in the region based on CONE, net CONE, and the amount of 

total accredited capacity available to the region relative to the region’s non-coincident peak 

load and PRM. Payments collected via the SVC are distributed to excess accredited capacity as 

compensation for standing in for deficient LREs. 

The MMU is concerned that the effectiveness of the SVC as an incentive for resource adequacy 

is at risk due to a lack of transparency and a need for process improvements. As such, we first 

recommend that SPP recalculate the value of net CONE annually. In addition, this value and the 

clearing price of the SVC should be published publicly once available to provide transparent and 

symmetric information to all LREs and market participants in order to allow them to make more 

informed decisions related to capacity in the future. The MMU considers this a medium priority 

issue. 

2024.4  Cost of New Entry (CONE) update (medium) 

SPP's gross Cost of New Entry (CONE) study assumptions are outdated and require reevaluation. 

CONE is used to indicate the current, annualized, capital cost of a new hypothetical resource and 

estimate the breakeven revenue required for investment. In SPP's construct, the CONE value is 

used to determine the payment that a deficient load responsible entity is charged if they lack 

sufficient accredited capacity to cover their forecasted peak load and planning reserve margin. 

State of the Market 2025 

15 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Executive summary and recommendations 

While the CONE value was updated in 2025 based on the current assumptions in the tariff, an 

effort is underway to reevaluate these assumptions and the process used to keep the CONE 
value reasonably up to date (RR743).8 The MMU considers this a medium priority issue. 

2023.1  Enhance uncertainty product design to ensure rampable capacity is procured 

and compensated (medium) 

In its 2023 annual report, the MMU included a recommendation to enhance the uncertainty 
product design to address deficiencies in the implemented product.9 There were two issues 

noted by the MMU: backing out other reserve product requirements from the uncertainty 

product’s requirement and how soon-to-start resources are treated in the optimization. The 

MMU recommended that SPP look critically at the practice of reducing the uncertainty 

requirement with other reserve products’ requirements as well as reviewing the use of soon-to-

start resources in meeting the requirement for uncertainty. 

In October 2024, SPP implemented an enhancement to the uncertainty product design to back 

out only the the ramp capability-up product’s requirement from the flexibility requirement in 

alignment with one part of the MMU recommendaton. This has led to some improvement in the 
clearing for the uncertainty product. 10 SPP has noted it will review the second part of the MMU 

recommendation, i.e. the treatment of soon-to-start resources, after having time to assess the 

results of the October 2024 enhancement. The MMU continues to recommend SPP assess the 

treatment of soon-to-start resources in meeting the uncertainty requirement. The MMU 

considers this a medium priority issue. 

2023.2  Ensure planning, markets, and operational processes appropriately consider 

integration of large loads (high) 

The number of large loads, such as large data centers and crypto mines, continue to rise in the 
SPP footprint, with many of them registering as demand response resources.11 Owing to their 

size and location, these loads have the potential to cause, and have caused, high prices and 

significant levels of congestion in both the day-ahead and real-time markets. Alignment of 

planning, markets, and operational processes to accommodate the integration of these large 

loads is necessary to ensure appropriate price signals in the market and address unsolved 

congestion impacts on the system. Furthermore, alignment also helps prevent operational issues 

that ultimately impact the market and other market participants. The MMU made

2022.2  Address limitations with the ramp capability product (medium) 

In fall 2022, the MMU performed a review of the ramp capability product’s effectiveness and 
documented the results in its quarterly report.13 In its 2022 annual report, the MMU 

recommended that SPP and stakeholders evaluate options to address issues with the ramp 

capability product, including issues with the demand curve values and ramp being stranded 
behind congested constraints.14  

The MMU continues to recommend that SPP staff evaluate the effectiveness of the demand 

curves when assessing solutions to the stranded ramping issue. The MMU considers this a 

medium priority issue. 

2021.1  Expand multi-configuration combined cycle resource model to include 

additional resource types (medium) 

In the 2021 annual report,15 the MMU recommended SPP expand the multi-configuration 

combined cycle resource model or create a new multi-configuration model to include additional 

resource types that have multiple operating modes, or configurations. This recommendation 

comes in response to observed inefficiencies from participants attempting to optimize their 

plant’s schedule without the benefit of such logic. The MMU noted that SPP’s multi-

configuration combined cycle resource model provides the market several efficiency gains by 

optimizing the schedule of configurations, improving participant abilities to offer different 

parameters for each configuration, and providing real-time operational awareness. 

Additional information, including further potential applications of the multi-configuration 

combined cycle resource model, is documented in the 2021 report. The MMU considers this a 

medium priority issue.

2020.1  Update market and outage requirements to improve funding for transmission 

congestion rights (high) 

The MMU made recommendations in the 2020 annual report to update outage requirements 

and develop market rules and market incentives associated with outages in order to better align 

the network models used by the transmission congestion rights auctions and the day-ahead 
market.16 This recommendation was a result of the MMU observation of a continued downward 

trend in the overall funding of transmission congestion rights from day-ahead market 

congestion rents.  

In January 2024, the Operating Reliability Working Group (ORWG) approved outage 

coordination methodology language changes requiring outages on transmission lines that are 

100kV and above and five days or longer in duration be submitted to SPP 45 days ahead of the 

calendar month. The MMU noted in its 2024 annual report that after that change was 

implemented, the MMU would monitor the impact of the change on TCR underfunding and 

determine whether this recommendation could be considered completed. However, after the 

time of the report, additional changes were made to the outage coordination methodology 

language that nullified the benefits of the new requirement. These changes including allowing 

for “late” planned outages that are not required to meet the 45-day deadline as previously 

approved by the ORWG. While these late outages will have a lower priority, the issue remains 

that there can be planned discrepancies between the outages in the transmission congestion 

rights models and the day-ahead market. As such, the MMU continues to recommend 

enhancements to address the misalignment of planned outages in the transmission congestion 

rights and day-ahead market models. The MMU considers this a high priority issue.  

2020.2  Enhance market-to-market efficiencies through collaboration with MISO 

(medium) 

In 2019 and 2020, the MMU worked with the MISO market monitor on a series of 

recommendations for the Joint Regional State Committee / Organization of MISO States Seams 

Liaison Committee. Based on the results of the joint study, the MMU recommended in the 2020 

annual report to evaluate the processes and mechanisms used to effectuate the market-to-
market agreement between SPP and MISO.17 Several items identified by the market monitors 

have been addressed by SPP and MISO including activation of MISO market-to-market 

constraints in the SPP day-ahead market and increasing available relief of MISO resources. The

MMU continues to recommend that SPP and its stakeholders address inefficiencies in the 

market-to-market agreement between SPP and MISO. The MMU considers this a medium 

priority issue. 

2019.1 

Improve price formation (medium) 

In the 2019 report, the MMU identified circumstances where market prices provided neither 

short-term nor a long-term economic incentive to ensure reliability and recommended SPP and 

stakeholders review price formation during scarcity events and establish graduated demand 

curves that incentivize proper price formation.  

In the MMU report on the February 2021 winter weather event, the MMU made 

recommendations related to improving price formation during emergency and scarcity 

conditions. In that report, the MMU highlighted situations where price signals did not accurately 

reflect underlying conditions. These recommendations, which were aimed at improving pricing 

outcomes, closely aligned with the 2019 recommendation to improve price formation.  

In 2024, SPP proposed a revision to attachment AE of the tariff to address price formation 
during load shed events and emergency imports.18 This proposal was filed with FERC, accepted 
by FERC, and implemented by SPP on April 15, 2025.19 SPP also filed RR703 (Demand Response 

and Peak Demand Assessment) with FERC in April 2026, and it contains price formation-

enhancing changes for when behind-the-meter demand response resources are called on to 

reduce load. 

Both of these revisions are reasonable efforts to help ensure proper price formation during very 

specific events. While the MMU supports these efforts, the MMU continues to recommend that 

enhancements to improve price formation during emergency and near emergency events 

continue to include addressing increased resource commitments at the beginning of 

conservative operations. The additional capacity brought on-line in the form of increased 

headroom and early commitments in preparation for emergency conditions represents an 

excess of capacity that degrades market efficiency in the way of depressed market prices and 

increased uplift. Additionally, MISO’s updated operating reserve demand curve based on an 

increased value of lost load will create a strong disincentive for economic imports in the SPP 

region during emergency and near emergency events without improved price formation in the 

SPP market. The MMU considers this a medium priority issue.

2019.3  Update and improve outage coordination methodology (high) 

The MMU continues to recommend enhancements to SPP’s outage coordination methodology 

and to add language in its tariff that clearly describes the process and requirements. In the 2019 

annual report, the MMU initially recommended improvements. In 2022, the MMU highlighted its 
outstanding concerns regarding the outage coordination methodology.20 This recommendation 

has not yet been fully addressed and the MMU continues to recommend SPP make needed 

improvements to its outage coordination methodology, particularly in light of the central role 

outages play in the newly implemented accreditation methodologies. With the recommendation 

remaining incomplete, the MMU took the opportunity in the 2023 annual report to re-

emphasize the importance of this recommendation and broaden its scope. In particular, the 

MMU recommends focusing on how the outage study and approval process interacts with all 

aspects of reliability, transmission hedging, and the market, including energy adequacy. The 

MMU continues to believe the current outage coordination methodology is not sufficient to 

ensure energy adequacy or reliability and needs to be, at least in part, in the tariff to ensure 

consistent application and enforceability. The MMU provided additional information and 

examples of why the current language is not sufficient. The MMU considers this a high priority 

issue, made even more so by the current discussions around the seasonal resource adequacy 

construct, which will first require an overhaul of the outage coordination process.  

2017.2  Enhance commitment of resources (medium) 

In 2017, the MMU recommended that SPP and its stakeholders address the issue of inadequate 

ramping flexibility by modifying its market rules to enhance the commitment of resources and 

increase ramping flexibility. The MMU noted potential options to address the commitment 

concerns and recommended SPP and its stakeholders explore options to enhance commitment 

of resources and increase flexibility. Some of the original concern around ramping flexibility has 

been addressed through implementation of the ramp and uncertainty products, but issues 

remain around commitment decisions, notably over-commitment of resources in real-time 

reducing flexibility, suppressing prices, and increasing make-whole payments. We recommend 

that SPP explore both the decommitment of unneeded resources as well as improve how and 

when resources are committed. The MMU considers this a medium priority issue. 

2017.3  Enhance energy storage design (medium) 

SPP implemented its design for energy storage for compliance with FERC Order No. 841 in 2021. 

While the MMU filed supportive comments for the implementation of energy storage in the SPP

  CLOSED RECOMMENDATIONS FOR 2025  

The following recommendations from previous reports were closed in 2025. 

2022.3 

Improve situational awareness of transmission upgrade status and improve 
process to reassign transmission upgrade projects (medium) 

Delays in transmission upgrades can significantly contribute to congestion. This can have 

significant ramifications for market outcomes, and costs paid by ratepayers. Moreover, resource 

decisions, including generator interconnection, are made based on expectations and 

information on the status of transmission upgrades.  

In the 2022 Annual Report, the MMU noted a significant number of transmission projects and 

upgrades that were behind expected relevant deadlines and made recommendations regarding 

the lack of transparency into the actual expected in-service dates and delays and also 

recommended that SPP improve tariff clarity to assist in determining if transmission projects 
should be reassigned.21 Due to work completed since the 2022 report, the MMU considers the 

portion of the recommendation regarding improved situational awareness of transmission 

upgrades to have been addressed through changes in regular in-service date delay reporting 
and processes to reassign projects outlined in changes to BP7060 from SPP RR574.22 The SPP 

MMU considers this recommendation closed. 

2019.2

capacity performance and suggested potential options. In 2021, as part of its February 2021 

winter weather event report, the MMU also made recommendations regarding capacity 

adequacy and performance, many of which align with this recommendation. Specifically, the 

MMU noted there should be meaningful incentives for availability. Many of the MMU concerns 

have been noted in its comments in the filings for Performance-Based Accreditation (PBA) and 
Effective Load Carrying Capability (ELCC) accreditation for wind, solar and storage23 and the PBA

and the potential impact to SPP and its market participants. SPP stakeholders proposed a two-

MMU filed comments originally supporting the SPP filing. After later identifying language in the 

filing that the MMU believed to be inconsistent with the FERC order, the MMU filed 

supplemental comments and limited protest of the filing. FERC accepted the SPP Tariff revisions 

in March 2025. However, FERC did not agree that the show cause requirement has been 

addressed. The MMU considers this recommendation closed.

  WITHDRAWN PREVIOUS RECOMMENDATIONS  

The following recommendations from previous reports were withdrawn in 2025. 

2022.1  Consider limitations on virtual trading during emergency conditions (low) 

In 2022, the MMU published a paper examining the impact of virtual trading during the 
February 2021 winter weather event.28 Based on the results of the analysis performed, the MMU 

submitted an initiative on the SPP Roadmap seeking to study the effectiveness of virtual 

transactions during the winter weather event and identify any potential lessons learned or 

recommendations going forward. The MMU also included this as a recommendation in its 2022 
annual report.29 This initiative was removed from the SPP Roadmap and added to Market 

Working Group’s list of action items to consider. As SPP and the Market Monitoring Unit both 

monitor virtual activity and impacts and, at this time, do not have enough data to inform the 

development of criteria under which it is appropriate and economically sound to limit virtual 

trading during emergency conditions, this recommendation is being withdrawn. 

2020.3  Raise offer floor to −$100/MWh (low) 

In the 2020 report, the MMU recommended raising the energy offer floor to −$100/MWh. This 

recommendation was the result of analysis performed by the MMU which observed resources 

offering at the current offer floor of −$500/MWh and setting price. These offers did not 

represent cost and were harmful to nearby resources. As noted in the 2020 report, the MMU 

believes that raising the offer floor is a simple and cost-effective solution that avoids any 

limitation of what costs can be included in a market offer. The MMU considers this a low priority 

issue and given the lack of movement on this issue is withdrawing the recommendation. 

2017.4  Reduce self-scheduling in the market (low) 

The MMU recommended in the 2017 report that SPP and its stakeholders address the 

inefficiencies caused when market participants self-commit their resources in the market. The 

MMU then conducted an in-depth study of self-commitment practices and associated 
inefficiencies in 2019.30  

The MMU continues to recommend that SPP and its stakeholders explore and develop ways to 

reduce instances of self-commitment of resources outside of the market solution, including 

considering adding an additional day to the optimization process. SPP submitted RR612 (multi-

2  LOAD AND RESOURCES 

Load and resources 

This chapter reviews load and resources in the SPP market for 2025. Key points from this chapter 

include: 

•  Monthly average system energy consumption was up 3% compared to 2024 levels. Most 

of this increase can be attributed to load growth rather than factors such as weather.  

• 

In 2025, 1,126 MW of wind generation capacity was added to the market, ending 2025 

with 35,934 MW of nameplate wind capacity. Wind generation capacity now accounts for 

34% of installed nameplate capacity in the SPP market. 

•  At the end of 2025, there was 987 MW of dispatchable demand response resources of 

installed nameplate capacity representing 130 resources. This is an increase of 19 MW 

nameplate capacity, and a net gain of nine resources. 

•  At the end of 2025, generation capacity from renewable, storage, and hybrid resources 

accounts for the vast majority of proposed generation interconnection queue, at nearly 

114 GW (76%) of the nearly 149 GW in the queue. These resource types accounted for 

89% of the queue at the end of 2024. An increase in gas generation interconnection 

requests was also experienced in 2025, rising from 14 GW at the end of 2024 to 35 GW 

at the end of 2025. A significant portion, almost 9 GW, of this increase in interconnection 

requests for gas resources can be attributed to submittals into the expedited resource 

adequacy study (ERAS). 

•  Wind generation represented the largest portion of total energy produced at 38% of the 

total. Coal generation was next highest at 25% of the total.  

•  SPP remained a net exporter for 2025 with an hourly average of 254 MW, up from 153 

MW in 2024.  

•  Net market-to-market payments from MISO to SPP were $86 million in 2025 compared 

to $112 million in 2024.  

•  Cleared virtual energy bids and offers as a percentage of load for 2025 was 32%, up from 

29% in 2024. 

•  Average profit per cleared virtual megawatt after fees was $1.29/MW in 2025, up from 

$1.25/MW in 2024.  

State of the Market 2025 

26 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

2.1  THE INTEGRATED MARKETPLACE 

SPP is a Regional Transmission Organization (RTO) authorized by the Federal Energy Regulatory 

Commission (FERC) to ensure reliable power supplies, adequate transmission infrastructure, and 

competitive wholesale electricity prices. FERC granted RTO status to SPP in 2004. SPP provides 

many services to its members, including reliability coordination, tariff administration, regional 

scheduling, reserve sharing, transmission expansion planning, wholesale electricity market 

operations, and training. This report focuses on the 2025 calendar year of the SPP wholesale 

electricity market referred to as the Integrated Marketplace, which started on March 1, 2014. 

The Integrated Marketplace has transmission congestion rights, a day-ahead market with virtual 

trading, a reliability unit commitment process, a real-time balancing market, and a price-based 

operating reserves market. SPP simultaneously put into operation a single balancing authority as 

part of the implementation of the Integrated Marketplace. The primary benefit of a day-ahead 

market is improved efficiency of daily resource commitments. Another benefit of this market 

includes the joint optimization of the available capacity for energy and operating reserves. 

SPP MARKET FOOTPRINT 

The SPP market footprint is located in the westernmost portion of the Eastern Interconnection, 

with Midcontinent ISO (MISO) to the east, Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT) to the 

south, and the Western Interconnection to the west. Figure 2–1 shows the current operating 

regions of the nine RTO/ISO markets in the United States and Canada, as well as a more detailed 

view of the SPP footprint. The SPP market also has connections with other non-RTO/ISO areas 

such as Saskatchewan Power Corporation, Associated Electric Cooperative, and Southwestern 
Power Administration.32  

32 Southwestern Power Administration belongs to the SPP RTO, Reliability Coordinator (RC), and Reserve 
Sharing Group (RSG) footprints. Associated Electric Cooperative belongs to the SPP RSG.

SPP market footprint and RTO/ISO operating regions 

  RTO EXPANSION 

While this report covers only 2025, it should be noted here that on April 1, 2026, the SPP 

Integrated Marketplace expanded into the western interconnection. The RTO expansion 

increases the footprint of the Integrated Marketplace with the addition of the Western Area 

Colorado Missouri (WACM) balancing authority, located primarily in Colorado and Wyoming, 

and the Western Area Power Administration Upper Great Plains West (WAUW) balancing 

authority in Montana. This addition will create the West Balancing Authority Area (West BAA) in 

the Integrated Marketplace and be separated from the existing Integrated Marketplace (East 

Balancing Authority Area, or East BAA) by DC Ties separating the eastern and western 

interconnections.  

Figure 2–2  RTO expansion map 

State of the Market 2025 

28 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

There are seven primary DC ties that form a “seam” between the Eastern and Western 

interconnections, allowing power transfer between the systems The RTO Expansion was 

expected to go live with Miles City, Stegall, and Sidney DC Ties, but due to a potential long-term 

outage, control of the Sidney Tie was not transferred to SPP at the time of expansion. 

Consequently, the market’s ability to move power between the East BAA and West BAA is 

limited. With only the Miles City and Stegall ties participating in the Integrated Marketplace, the 

maximum transfer capacity between the east and west is 310 MW. The next state of the market 

report will contain further information related to the expansion. 

  SPP MARKET PARTICIPANTS 

SPP market participants can be divided into several categories: regulated investor-owned 

utilities, electric cooperatives, municipal utilities, federal and state agencies, independent power 

producers, and financial-only market participants that do not own physical assets. Figure 2–3 

shows the distribution of the 110 market participants registered to take part in the Integrated 

Marketplace. 

Figure 2–3  Market participants by type 

The independent power producers account for 66% of all market participants because most 

variable energy producers are included in this category. The market participant in the agent 

category represents asset owners of more than one of the different types. 

Figure 2–4 shows generation nameplate capacity owned by the type of market participant. 

State of the Market 2025 

29 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–4  Nameplate capacity by market participant type 

Although investor-owned utilities represent only a small portion of the total number of market 

participants at 7%, they own the highest portion of the SPP generation capacity at 46%. This is in 

contrast to the “independent power producer” category, which has a large number of market 

participants (66%) representing a smaller portion (21%) of total nameplate capacity. 

2.2  ELECTRICITY DEMAND  

Several factors influence demand for electricity. In the short term, weather conditions, often 

measured in cooling degree days or heating degree days, influence the magnitude and duration 

of peak demand periods. In the long term, electrification of vehicles, heating, and other 

processes along with economic growth can cause average demand to increase. Like many 

regions, the SPP footprint faces potential load growth over the next decade that is uncertain but 

has a likelihood to be historically high due to a combination of factors, but most notably, due to 

the rapid development of infrastructure supporting artificial intelligence. 

The MMU monitors trends in demand over time. Key metrics include system peak demand, 

system energy consumption by market participant, and heating and cooling degree days. 

Together, these metrics can help identify drivers of load growth and identify possible 

changes in demand patterns in the footprint.  

  SYSTEM PEAK DEMAND 

One way to evaluate load is to review peak system demand statistics over an extended period. 

The market footprint has changed over time as participants have been added to or withdrawn 

from the market. The peak demand values reviewed in this section are coincident peaks, 

calculated out of total generation dispatch across the entire market footprint that occurred 

State of the Market 2025 

30 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

during a specific real-time market five-minute interval. The peak experienced during a particular 

year or season is affected by events such as unusually hot or cold weather, daily and seasonal 

load patterns, and economic growth and change. 

Figure 2–5 shows a month-by-month comparison of five-minute demand for the last three 

years. 

Figure 2–5  Monthly peak system demand 

In 2025, nine of twelve months exceeded the peak from 2024. The SPP system five-minute 

interval peak demand in 2025 was 54,850 MW, which occurred on July 29 at the five-minute 

interval beginning at 3:35 PM. This was below the all-time peak set in 2023 of 56,139 MW. 

Figure 2–6 shows the seasonal peak for five-minute demand for the last three years. Winter 

comprises January, February, and October through December, while summer comprises March 

through September. 

Figure 2–6  Seasonal peak system demand

Winter peak system demand for 2025 was 48,195 MW, which was 3% above 2024, while summer 

peak system demand for 2025 was 54,850 MW, 2.5% below 2023. The winter peak was set 

during the winter weather on February 20. 

  MARKET PARTICIPANT LOAD 

The amount of load participating in the day-ahead is shown in Figure 2–7. 

Figure 2–7  Cleared demand bids in day-ahead market 

The average monthly participation rates in the day-ahead market for load assets on an 

aggregate level were between 96% and 99% of the actual real-time load in 2025. Accurate 

reflection of demand in the day-ahead market economically incents generation to participate in 

the day-ahead market. Additionally, accurate reflection of the load helps to converge prices. 

After a slow decline over the past several years, load participation in the day-ahead market has 

climbed slightly in 2025, with averages in both on-peak and off-peak periods just under 98%.

In 2025, the maximum hourly average load was 52,124 MW, which was down 0.3% from 2024. 

The minimum hourly load for 2025 was 23,378 MW, which was 1.9% above 2024. Comparing 

annual load duration curves shows differentiation between cases of extreme loading events and 

more general increases in system demand. If only the extremes are higher or lower than the 

previous year, then short-term loading events are likely the reason. If the entire curve is higher 

across the entire range, this is more indicative of increased system demand. For 2025, the curve 

remains above 2024 for almost the entire curve, indicating an overall increase in system 

demand. 

  HEATING AND COOLING DEGREE DAYS 

Changes in weather patterns from year-to-year have a significant impact on electricity demand. 

One way to evaluate this impact is to calculate heating degree days (HDD) and cooling degree 

days (CDD). These values can then be used to estimate the impact of actual weather conditions 
on energy consumption, compared to normal weather patterns.33 Regression analysis has shown 

33 To determine heating degree days and cooling degree days for the SPP footprint, several representative 
locations are used in the calculation. These locations include Shreveport LA, Lubbock TX, Oklahoma City 
OK, Amarillo TX, Kansas City, MO, Hays, KS, Omaha NE, North Platte NE, Sioux Falls SD, Rapid City SD, 
Grand Forks ND, and Williston/Stanley ND. The base temperature separating heating and cooling periods 
is 65 degrees Fahrenheit. If the average temperature of a day at a location is 75 degrees Fahrenheit, there 
would be 10 (=75–65) cooling degree days at that location. If a day’s average temperature is 50 degrees 
Fahrenheit, there would be 15 (=65–50) heating degree days at that location. Using statistical tools, the 
daily estimated load impact of a single cooling degree day is just over four times higher than the impact of 
a single heating degree day. This is in part because more electric is used for cooling than electric heating. 
So, in order to show the actual impact of degree days, cooling degree days are multiplied by 4.2 in Figure 
2–11. 

State of the Market 2025 

35 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

that a cooling degree has about 4.2 times the impact of a heating degree on load, so cooling 

degree days are multiplied by 4.2 in the chart below. 

Figure 2–11 shows monthly heating and cooling degree days’ impact over the last three years 

compared to the average hourly load. 

Figure 2–11  Heating and cooling degree days 

As shown in the chart, cooling degree days are more prevalent in the higher load months of 

May through October, whereas heating degree days are more prevalent in the other months. 

Total degree day impact in 2025 was down 1% from 2024. Average hourly load was up 3% in 

2025 compared to 2024. The fact that average hourly load was up and degree day impact was 

down indicates that the increase in average hourly load can be primarily attributed to economic 

growth in 2024, as opposed to weather impacts. 

2.3  INSTALLED GENERATION CAPACITY 

Sufficient capacity to cover a region’s load obligation is a prerequisite for sustaining an efficient, 

competitive market by providing the necessary liquidity to support both market transactions 

and system reliability. A diverse generation portfolio is also key. Resources utilizing different 

generation technologies can provide different ancillary services such as regulation and voltage 

control. Additionally, resources of each fuel type have different vulnerabilities and thus a diverse 

portfolio provides lower correlation risk: wind output is limited to wind speed, gas supplies can 

be interrupted by pipeline issues, and so on. 

The MMU monitors the region’s generation portfolio, including the age, fuel types, and 

resources in the interconnection queue. These metrics not only serve as one way to assess the 

State of the Market 2025 

36 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

overall health of the current generation portfolio but shed light on potential risks and how 

the current resource mix may change in the future.

footprint.

 Total installed nameplate generation capacity in the SPP Integrated Marketplace was 106,291 
MW at the end of 2025, representing an increase of 3.3% (or 3,499 MW) from 2024.34  

Wind continues to have the highest share of total nameplate capacity at 34% of total nameplate 

generation. When both types of natural gas resources are combined, natural gas-fired installed 

generation capacity continues to represent the largest share of generation capacity in the SPP 

market at 36% (gas simple-cycle growing to 23% and gas combined-cycle at 13%) of nameplate 

capacity, with coal being the third largest type at 21%. 

As has been the case for the past two years, the amount of solar capacity more than doubled 

from 2024 to 2025. Continued growth in solar capacity also remains likely, as evidenced by the 

high amount of solar capacity that is in the generation interconnection queue seen in Figure 2-

18. With the continued growth of solar compared to other types of resources, it is likely that 

solar will continue to grow in market share as well.

While market storage resources didn’t have the same amount of gross capacity growth that 

solar did, it should be noted that the amount of capacity for this type of resource nearly tripled 

from 2024 to 2025. With battery and storage resources constituting a large amount of the 

generation interconnection queue, it should be expected that storage resources will continue to 

grow and play a larger role in SPP’s footprint. As the load within the RTO continues to grow, the 

versatility offered by market storage resources could play a large role in maintaining reliability. 

Figure 2–13 illustrates that certain segments of the SPP generation fleet are aging.  

Figure 2–13  Capacity by age of resource 

As of the end of 2025, 39% of SPP’s generation fleet is more than 30 years old. Notably, 85% of 

coal capacity and 41% of gas capacity is older than 30 years, though these values have mostly 

stayed the same from 2024, with only 5 MW of natural gas capacity retiring. According to the 

U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA), the national average age of natural gas generation 
that retired in 2025 was 42 years.35 While SPP certainly has a contingent of natural gas resources 

that are beyond this age, new gas generation is being built or planned in the interconnection 

generation queue to offset the eventual retirements of these older resources. Wind continues to 

be the largest in terms of capacity regarding newly developed resources, though natural gas 

also boasts a large amount of more recently developed capacity, with over 21,000 MW younger 

than 30 years old. 

Figure 2–14 shows the annual trend of capacity additions and retirements over the past three 

years.

Figure 2–14  Capacity additions and retirements by year 

Until this past year, majority of the capacity retired since 2016 have been coal and gas plants 

that started operation in the 1950s and 1960s. 2025 saw a dramatic shift in the type of resource 

retired though, with 513 MW of demand response resources retired. The only other retirements 

within SPP in 2025 were five MW of fuel oil. 

Total nameplate capacity additions were 4,048 MW in 2025. For capacity additions, wind 

generation has accounted for 44% of the additions over the last three years. 2025 saw 1,368 MW 

of wind generation added, increasing almost 300 MW from what was added the year prior, 

though wind did fall as proportion of overall additions for 2025. This year saw the addition of 

1,129 MW of solar resources, up from the 402 MW that were added in 2024. Also of note, an 

additional 510 MW of nameplate capacity for dispatchable demand response resources was 

added to the market in 2025, showing a net decrease for these types of resources over the past 

year. Additionally, 328 MW of nameplate capacity of market storage resources was added in 

2025, more than doubling the 130 MW added in 2024. This indicates that the solar and market 

storage resources in the interconnection process are slowly starting to work their way through 

and become closer to operational status, while demand response resources have hit a balance 

regarding new resource additions and resource retirements. Considering the 4,048 MW in 
capacity additions along with 518 MW of retirements, 3,530 MW36 of net generating capacity 

was added to the SPP market in 2025.

between both peak and average demand between 2024 and 2025, the 2024 lines for both are 

not visible on the chart above. Based on the monthly system energy consumption analysis in 

Section 3, the SPP market footprint had slightly higher energy consumption in summer month 

of 2025 compared to 2024, which resulted in higher average demand in the summer months as 

compared to the previous year.

in 2024 and 16.6 GW in 2025, respectively.

SPP is responsible for performing engineering studies to determine if the interconnection of 

new generation within the SPP footprint is feasible, and to identify any transmission 

development that would be necessary to facilitate the proposed generation. The generation

interconnection process involves a cluster study methodology allowing participants several

Figure 2–17 shows the capacity by generation technology type in all stages of development. 

Included in this figure are interconnection agreements in the process of being created; those 

under construction; those already completed, but not yet in commercial operation; and those in 

which work has been suspended as of year-end 2024.  

Figure 2–17  Active generation interconnection requests 

As shown above, generation capacity from renewable, storage, and hybrid40 resources accounts 

for the vast majority of proposed generation interconnection, at nearly 114 GW of the nearly 

149 GW in the generation interconnection queue. Wind generation in the queue at the end of 

2025 was over 30 GW; this has dropped to 25 GW in 2025, continuing a trend of decreased wind 

resources in the interconnection queue that peaked in 2023. Interconnection requests for solar 

generation similarly decreased, dropping from 44 GW at the end of 2024 to 37 GW at the end of 

2025. While renewable capacity decreased in the queue decreased in 2025, both batteries and 

hybrid resources increased by 6 GW to 31 GW and 25 GW, respectively. Interconnection requests 

for gas have also drastically increased this past year, rising from 14 GW at the end of 2024 to 

over 35 GW at the end of 2025. A significant portion, almost 9 GW, of this increase in 

interconnection requests for gas resources can be attributed to submittals into the expedited 

resource adequacy study (ERAS). This process, approved by FERC on May 6, 2025, is designed to 

allow large new proposed generation that would be quickly able to reach commercial operation 

in five years or less an alternative to the standard generation interconnection queue. The stated
