wind, the current largest resource type in SPP in terms of capacity, representing 12 GW of this 

generation. It is important to note that of the on-schedule projects, 40% are for solar resources, 

34% are for wind resources, and 16% are for battery/storage resources. However, when looking 

at the entire generation interconnection queue, 25% of the projects are for solar resources, 24% 

are for gas resources, 21% are for battery/storage resources, 17% for wind resources, and 13% 

for hybrid. While gas does constitute a large proportion of the overall queue, its lack of on-

schedule capacity indicates that as SPP quickly brings on more generation to combat the 

growing load, it is renewables and battery solutions that are overwhelmingly being completed 

to support the grid in the immediate future. This means that while gas and coal resources will 

remain vital to reliability within the footprint, market share will likely continue to grow for wind 

and solar powered resources. While generation can still be added or removed from the on-

schedule project list, even in the current year, there is more surety to the levels of generation 

that are listed to go into production closer to the current year, and additions and deletions to 

on-schedule projects are more typical in future years.

2.4  GENERATION

  GENERATION BY TECHNOLOGY 

An analysis of generation by technology type used in the SPP Integrated Marketplace is useful in 

understanding pricing and reliability, as well as the potential impact of environmental and 

additional regulatory requirements on resources in the SPP system. Information on fuel and 

technology types and fleet characteristics is also useful in understanding market dynamics 

regarding congestion management, price volatility, and overall market efficiency. 

To these ends, the MMU tracks real-time generation over time. We focus specifically on 

generation by fuel and technology type, including how often each type is the marginal 

resource.  

Figure 2–19 depicts annual generation percentages in the SPP real-time market by technology 

type for the years 2014 through 2025. Some of the annual fluctuations in generation by 

technology type shares are driven by the relative difference in primary fuel prices, namely 

natural gas versus coal. The long-term trend for coal-fired generation had been relatively flat 

prior to 2014 (not shown on chart above) but has been in a steady decline ever since. This is 

primarily due to low gas prices from 2015 to 2020, causing efficient gas generation to displace 

some coal generation, as can be seen in the higher generation from combined-cycle gas plants. 

Higher gas prices in 2021 and 2022 led to a temporary reversal of this trend. During that period, 

coal generation became more economic than many gas resources, resulting in an increase in the 

percentage of total generation by coal to 36% year-over-year. With gas prices returning to 

previous levels, coal’s share of total generation has declined to 29%. 

Figure 2–19  Generation by technology type, real-time, annual 

State of the Market 2025 

44 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Wind generation’s share fell to 36.7% in 2025, following the recent incline to 37.9% in 2024. The 

drop in wind generation in 2023 was primarily attributed to the increase in capacity, as shown in 

Figure 2–25. With the return of lower gas prices, generation from gas units such as gas turbines 

and gas steam turbines increased back to pre-2020 levels; gas, combined-cycle at 15% of total 

generation and gas, simple-cycle at 10%. 

Retirement of older coal generation, environmental limits, and competition from renewable and 

natural gas technologies are some of the factors that will continue to put pressure on coal 

generation levels. In addition, renewable generation is expected to continue to increase in the 

years ahead. 

Figure 2–20 depicts the 2025 monthly fluctuation in generation by technology type.  

Figure 2–20  Generation by technology type, real-time, monthly 

Wind generation as a percentage of total generation is generally lowest in the summer months 

at levels around 27%. The highest wind generation months are typically during spring and fall. 

During these seasons, monthly wind generation levels can approach, or even exceed, 50% of 

total generation. In 2025, wind reached a high of 49% in March. Additionally, wind generation 

outpaced coal in most months, excluding July, August, and September. 

One method commonly used to assess price trends and relative efficiency in electricity markets 

originating from non-fuel costs is the implied heat rate. The implied heat rate is calculated by 

dividing the electricity price, net of a representative value for variable operations and 
maintenance (VOM) costs, by the fuel (gas) price.41 For a gas generator, the implied heat rate 

41 For the implied heat rate calculation, natural gas units are assumed to be on the margin and accordingly, 
gas prices are taken as the relevant fuel cost. Emission costs are ignored in fuel cost as they rarely apply in 
the SPP market. 

State of the Market 2025 

45 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

serves as a “break-even” point for profitability such that a unit producing output with an 

operating (actual) heat rate below the implied heat rate would be earning profits, given market 

prices for electricity and gas. If the price of natural gas was $3/MMBtu, and the electricity price 

was $24/MWh, the implied heat rate would be (24/3) = 8 MMBtu/MWh (8,000 Btu/kWh). This 

implied heat rate shows the relative efficiency required of a generator to convert gas to 

electricity and cover the variable costs of production, given market prices. 

Figure 2–21 shows the monthly implied heat rate using real-time electricity prices for 2023 to 

2025, along with an annual average for those years.  

Figure 2–21 

Implied heat rate 

As shown above, the implied heat rate for 2025 was significantly below that of 2024 and most of 

2023, except for March and October. 2025 averaged of over 11,000 Btu/KWh, compared to a 

2024 average of over 14,400 Btu/KWh – a decrease of over 23%. For 2025, the peak implied heat 

rate took place in September at around 13,700 Btu/KWh – down from a peak in August 2023 

and 2024 of around 17,700 Btu/KWh. With actual heat rates for many gas resources above the 

levels of implied heat rates in 2025, certain gas resources would be unprofitable given electricity 

and gas prices during the year. 

  GENERATION ON THE MARGIN 

The system marginal price represents the price of the next increment of generation available to 

meet the next increment of total system demand. The locational marginal price at a particular 

pricing node is the system marginal energy price plus any marginal congestion charges and 

marginal loss charges associated with that pricing node. For a generator to set the marginal 

price, the resource must be: 1) dispatchable by the market; 2) not at the resource economic 

State of the Market 2025 

46 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

minimum or maximum; and 3) not ramp limited. In other words, it must be able to move to 

provide the next increment of generation. 

Day-ahead generation on the margin, shown in Figure 2–22, is different from real-time in that 

the day-ahead market includes virtual transactions. The real-time market does not include virtual 

transactions and must adjust to unforeseeable market conditions such as unexpected plant and 

transmission outages.  

Figure 2–22  Generation on the margin, day-ahead 

In 2025, coal was on the margin the highest percentage of hours, at 23% in the day-ahead 

market. Virtual transactions accounted for 23% of hours, while gas – simple-cycle and wind 

resources both accounted for 18% of hours, and lastly, followed by gas – combined-cycle at 

14%, and lastly, gas – combined-cycle at 14% of total hours. While marginal virtual offers occur 

at all types of settlement locations, 74% of marginal virtual offers are at resource settlement 

locations, with a significant amount of that activity at wind generation resource locations.  

Figure 2–23 illustrates the frequency with which different technology types were marginal and 

price setting in the real-time market.  

State of the Market 2025 

47 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Figure 2–23  Generation on the margin, real-time 

Load and resources 

After increasing to 37% in 2024, wind generation on the margin dropped to 33% in 2025. With 

the growing amount of dispatchable wind generation and an overall share of 34% of total 

nameplate capacity, wind generation had increasingly become the marginal technology a higher 

percentage of the time. However, with the drop in wind generation on the margin in 2025, the 

MMU will continue to observe if this is an ongoing trend. At the end of 2024, 98% of nameplate 

wind capacity was dispatchable, compared to 97% at the end of 2023. At the beginning of the 

Integrated Marketplace in March 2014, just 27% of nameplate wind capacity was modeled as 

dispatchable.  

The most significant difference between day-ahead and real-time fuel on the margin is the 

necessary absence of virtual offers in the real-time market. From the day-ahead to the real-time 

market, wind resources on the margin increased from 19% to 33% of all intervals (14 percentage 

points), while gas, simple-cycle and combined-cycle decreased by five and six percentage points, 

respectively. Coal resources on the margin increased by five percentage points from the day-

ahead to the real-time.  

On a monthly basis, intervals with coal generation on the margin are typically lower in the spring 

and fall months, offset by wind resources acting as base load units. This results in coal and gas-

fired units cycling more often. Increased wind generation also affects prices to some extent 

every month of the year. The higher wind generation on the margin values in the spring and fall 

are as expected given that these periods are the windiest times of the year, as well as the lowest 

demand periods in the SPP footprint.

48 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

2.5  GROWING IMPACT OF VARIABLE ENERGY RESOURCES 

The SPP region has a high potential for wind generation given wind patterns in many areas of 

the footprint. Federal incentives and state renewable portfolio standards and incentives have 

been additional factors that have resulted in significant investment of wind generation capacity 

in the SPP footprint during the last several years, The region has also seen rapid growth in solar 

over the last few years, a trend that is expected to continue. However, shifting federal policies 

are likely to stymie future growth of variable energy resources, particularly that of wind 

generation. 

The MMU tracks variable energy resource penetration in terms of both capacity and 

generation. Key metrics to understanding the impact of variable energy resources include 

production relative to load and curtailments.  

  WIND CAPACITY AND GENERATION 

One of the primary drivers for the amount of wind generation available in the market footprint 

is, not surprisingly, the amount of wind that is blowing at any point in time. Figure 2–24 shows 

the average monthly wind speed at three locations located in the portion of the footprint with 

an abundance of wind resources – Amarillo, TX; Lubbock, TX; and Hays, KS. 

Figure 2–24  Wind speed 

As shown above, the 2025 average wind speed at those locations was 11.6 mph, down just 

slightly from 11.7 mph in 2024. March and April had significantly higher wind speeds in 2025 

compared to 2024, while May, September, and October had much lower wind speeds in 2025 

compared to 2024. 

State of the Market 2025 

49 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–25 depicts nameplate capacity and average monthly generation of SPP wind facilities 

by year since 2014.  

Figure 2–25  Wind capacity and generation 

Total registered wind nameplate capacity at the end of 2025 was 35,934 MW, an increase of 3% 

(1,126 MW) from 2024. Annual wind generation dropped just slightly (0.2%) from 110,400 GWH 

in 2025 to 110,200 GWh in 2024.  

Consistent with previous years, wind generation fluctuated seasonally with summer being the 

low wind season and spring and fall being the high wind seasons. Wind also conformed to 

previous trends where production tended to be lower during on-peak hours than off-peak. 

Finally, higher levels of wind generation tended to coincide with the morning ramp periods. 

Figure 2–26 shows the wind capacity factor. Note that the wind capacity factor is reported for 
the entire month.42

The wind capacity factor in the day-market for 2025 was 26.3%, down slightly from 26.5% in 

2024. However, the real-time wind capacity factor for 2025 was 37.0%, up from 36.3% in 2024. 

The spread between the real-time and the day-ahead wind capacity factor, which historically has 

remained around a 10 percentage point difference, illustrates the disconnect in the amount of 

wind in the real-time market compared to the cleared wind in the day-ahead market.  

  WIND IMPACT ON THE SYSTEM 

Average annual wind generation as a percent of load continues to increase as shown in Figure 

2–27. The chart shows the trend for average and maximum wind generation as a percent of load 

since 2014. 

Figure 2–27  Wind generation as a percentage of load

Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Average wind generation as a percentage of load in the real-time dropped from 38.1% in 2024 

to 37.0% in 2025. After leveling off from 2017 to 2018, the growth of average wind generation 

as a percentage of load has climbed steadily from 2018 to 2022 but has leveled off, with a slight 

decreasing trend since then. Wind generation peaked at 26,344 MW in 2025 on a five-minute 

interval basis, up from 23,754 MW in 2024. Wind generation as a percentage of load for any 

five-minute interval reached a maximum value of 83% in 2025, which was up from 82% in 2024. 

Figure 2–28 shows wind production duration curves that represent wind generation as a percent 

of load by real-time (five-minute) interval for 2023 through 2025.  

Figure 2–28  Wind production curve 

The shift downward on the first half of the curve reflects an increase in total load in 2025. Wind 

generation served at least 40% of the total load during half of the year in 2024, with that figure 

decreasing to 38% in 2025.  

Figure 2–29 below shows average demand by hour of day, along with wind generation, and net 

demand (demand minus wind generation) for 2023.  

State of the Market 2025 

52 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–29  Average demand, net demand, and wind generation by hour of day 

Wind generation typically produces more energy when demand is lowest and less when demand 

is highest. Wind generation is at the highest levels in the overnight hours while demand is low. 

In the morning hours, wind generation decreases while load increases. To navigate these 

transitional hours, available rampable capacity is necessary. During these transitional hours, 
ramp scarcity is more likely.43 While the marginal costs of wind generation are very low and 

result in lower clearing prices, it is important to pair wind generation with capacity that is 

available to ramp up. 

While Figure 2–29 shows the yearly average demand, wind, and net demand, there are seasonal 

differences as well. For instance, in the summer, wind generation is lower than during other 

times of the year, and loads are higher. Thus, the effect on net demand is smaller. However, in 

spring and fall, loads are lower, and wind can have a significant effect on net demand. 

  VARIABLE ENERGY RESOURCE INTEGRATION 

Wind integration brings low marginal cost generation to the SPP region but does not count for 

much accredited capacity. There are a number of operational challenges in dealing with 

substantial wind capacity. For instance, wind energy output varies by season and time of day. 

This variability is estimated to be about four times more than load when measured on an hour-

to-hour basis. Moreover, wind is counter-cyclical to load. As load increases (both seasonally and 

daily), wind production typically declines. The increasing magnitude of wind capacity additions, 

along with the locational concentration, volatility, and variability of wind, can create challenges 

for grid operators with regard to managing transmission congestion and resolution of ramping 

43 This is discussed in Section 3.3.4.  

State of the Market 2025 

53 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

constraints. Wind forecasting errors can lead to price spikes while the under-clearing of wind in 

the day-ahead market is a leading cause of day-ahead and real-time price divergence.  

Figure 2–30 illustrates average hourly curtailments for solar and wind resources over the past 

three years. Since 2024, SPP has added over 1,500 MWs of solar capacity; however, solar 

curtailments average only 10 MW per hour and represent only 0.73% of total curtailments. 

Figure 2–30  Curtailments for variable energy resources 

From 2019 to 2024, total average hourly curtailments increased substantially from 137 MW to 

1,483 MW. However, 2025 represented the second year in the SPP market where curtailments 

decreased, falling to 1,382 MW from 1,483 MW in 2024. This occurred despite wind generation 

staying flat in 2025 relative to 2024, indicating that shifting congestion patterns played a role. 

Figure 2–31 displays a violin plot of the monthly distribution of hourly wind curtailments. For 

example, the wider parts of each shape represent where most of the data points exist, while the 

narrow parts illustrate where fewer data points are located. The line in the middle of each shape 

represents the median hourly value of wind curtailments for each month.

In 2025, curtailments were the largest and varied the most in the spring. This relationship exists 

since wind generation is higher in the spring relative to other seasons. 

  SOLAR CAPACITY AND GENERATION 

Although not having as high potential as wind, the SPP region has the capacity to add 

significant solar generation. As with wind, changes in policy at the federal level with regard to 

incentives for new renewables could potentially stymie the growth of solar generation going 

forward.

response resources in the SPP market until December 2019, when three dispatchable demand 

response resources, representing 0.3 MW of nameplate capacity became active in the market.

2.7  MARKET STORAGE RESOURCES 

At the end of 2025, the Integrated Marketplace had 14 registered market storage resources – six 

pumped storage and eight battery – and 728 MW of nameplate capacity. This is up from 11 

resources and 400 MW of nameplate capacity at the end of 2024. 

2.8  SEAMS 

The SPP Integrated Marketplace has greater than 6,000 MW of AC interties with MISO to the 

east, 720 MW of DC ties to ERCOT to the south, and over 1,000 MW of DC ties to the Western 

Interconnection to the west. Additionally, SPP has over 1,500 MW of interties with the 

Southwestern Power Administration (SPA) in Arkansas, Missouri, and Oklahoma, and over 5,000 

MW of AC interties with the Associated Electric Cooperative (AECI) in Oklahoma and Missouri. 

Efficiently and optimally utilizing these interties to import and export power is not only 

important economically but can be key for reliability. On the other hand, inefficiencies can create 

unnecessary costs and potentially harm reliability. 

The MMU monitors trends in net interchange to better understanding regional pricing 

dynamics between SPP and adjacent markets. The MMU also closely analyzes market-to-

market settlements as they demonstrate the ability of each market to relieve non-monitored 

constraints.

downward pressure on imports from SPP since 2022, until an uptick for the second half of 2025.

  MARKET-TO-MARKET 

Load and resources 

SPP began the market-to-market (M2M) process with MISO in March 2015 as part of a FERC 
requirement that also included regulation compensation and long-term congestion rights.45  

Each RTO is allocated property rights on market-to-market constraints. These are known as firm 

flow entitlements (FFE), and each RTO calculates its real-time usage, known as market flow. RTOs 

exchange money (market-to-market settlements) for redispatch based on the non-monitoring 
RTO’s46 market flow in relation to its firm flow entitlement. The non-monitoring RTO receives 

money from the monitoring RTO if its market flow is below its firm flow entitlement; it pays if 

above its firm flow entitlement. Figure 2–42 shows payments by month between SPP and MISO 

(positive is payment from MISO to SPP and negative is payment from SPP to MISO.) 

Figure 2–42  Market-to-market settlements 

All months in 2025 were net payments from MISO to SPP. Payments from MISO to SPP totaled 

$115 million in 2025 while payments from SPP to MISO totaled $29 million. This resulted in a net 

payment from MISO to SPP of $86 million in 2025 compared to $112 million in 2024, a 23% 

decrease.  

Figure 2–43 shows market-to-market payments (over $2 million from MISO to SPP and over $1 

million from SPP to MISO) by constraint for 2024. 

45 The market-to-market process, regulation compensation, and long-term congestion rights were required 
to be implemented one year after go-live of the SPP Integrated Marketplace. The market-to-market 
process under the joint operating agreement allows the monitoring RTO and non-monitoring RTO to 
efficiently manage market-to-market constraints by exchanging information (shadow prices, relief request, 
control indicators, etc.) and using the RTO with the more economic redispatch to relieve congestion. 
46 Essentially, the RTO which manages the limiting element of the constraint is the monitoring RTO. In most 
cases, the monitoring RTO has most of the impact and resources that provide the most effective relief of a 
congested constraint.  

State of the Market 2025 

62 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–43  Market-to-market settlements by flowgate 

In both 2024 and 2025, the TEMP39_26016 flowgate (Tekamah-Sub 1226 161 kV [OPPD] for the 

loss of Fort Calhoun-Raun 345 kV [OPPD-MEC], Fort Calhoun 345 kV [OPPD]) had the highest 

payments from MISO to SPP. Payments on this flowgate were $3.5 million higher than the next 

highest flowgate, RAUTEKRAUFTC (Raun-Tekamah 161kV [OPPD-MEC] for the loss of Raun-Fort 

Calhoun 345kV [OPPD-MEC]). Both of these flowgates are located in the Omaha area. The 

TEMP98_31502 flowgate (Couch-Lewisville 115 kV [EES] for the loss of Mt. Olive-Layfield 500 kV 

[CSWS-EES]) had the highest payments from SPP to MISO in 2025 at $3.5 million and is located 

in southwest Arkansas. 

2.9  VIRTUAL TRADING 

Market participants in SPP’s Integrated Marketplace may submit virtual energy offers and bids at 

any settlement location in the day-ahead market. Virtual offers represent energy sales to the 

day-ahead market that the participant needs to buy back in the real-time market. These are 

referred to as “increment offers,” which are like generation. Virtual bids represent energy 

purchases in the day-ahead market that the participant needs to sell back in the real-time 

market. These are referred to as “decrement bids,” which are like load. The value of virtual 

trading lies in its potential to converge day-ahead and real-time market prices and improve day-

ahead unit commitment decisions. 

The MMU tracks virtual trading in terms of quantity and settlement location type. We also 

measure profitability, before and after fees. Though not perfect, profit tends to indicate that 

virtuals helped converge the market. Fees provide a measure of the hurdle rate for virtual 

participation.   

State of the Market 2025 

63 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

For virtual transactions to converge prices, there must be sufficient competition in virtual 

trading; transparency in day-ahead market, reliability unit commitment, and real-time market 

operating practices; and predictability of market events. Since the market began in 2014, there 

have been increasing levels of virtual participation. Figure 2–44 displays the total volume of 

virtual transactions as a percentage of real-time market load along with wind output levels.  

Figure 2–44  Cleared virtual transactions as percent of real-time load 

As shown in the figure, virtual transactions averaged 32% of real-time market load in 2025, 

compared to 29% in 2024, and 26% in 2023. Cleared virtual offers as a percentage of load 

amounted to just over 19%, up from 17% in 2024, and 15% in 2023. Virtual cleared bids 

increased to 14% in 2025 from 12% in 2024, while averaging 11% in 2023. Virtual bids typically 

increase during high load hours.  

Most virtual transactions occurred at wind resources in 2025 – a trend that has been increasing 

since mid-2015. Figure 2–45 illustrates the settlement location types where virtual offers clear. 

State of the Market 2025 

64 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Figure 2–45  Cleared virtual offers by settlement location type 

In total, the monthly average of cleared virtual offers for 2025 was around 4,438 GW, up from 

4,035 GW in 2024. This figure shows that an average of just over 1,872 GW of virtual offers 
cleared at variable energy resources per month, during 2025.47 This is up from an average of 

nearly 1,731 GW per month in 2024. Virtual offers at wind locations remain the largest volume of 

any single location type. These large volumes highlight the possibility that market participants 

with registered wind resources may be missing financial opportunities by being under-cleared in 
the day-ahead market.48

Virtual trades profited $261 million before charges and fees in 2025, over a 25% increase from 

2024. Virtual bids can be charged distribution fees for day-ahead make-whole payments and 

virtual offers are susceptible to real-time make-whole payment distribution fees. In addition, 

both types of transactions can receive revenue neutrality uplift charge/credits and are assessed a 

per megawatt virtual fee. The average 2025 rates per megawatt for day-ahead make-whole 

payments, real-time make-whole payments, and real-time revenue neutrality uplift distributions 

are $0.14/MWh, $0.79/MWh, and $0.51/MWh, respectively. When factoring in these charges and 

credits, the net virtual trading profits for 2025 were $123.3 million. Net profits in 2025 increased 

by over 24% from $99.0 million in 2024.

monthly average profit per megawatt for a cleared virtual in 2023.

The chart shows that, when factoring in all fees, the average profit per megawatt in 2025 was

In 2025, 138 participants transacted virtuals – an increase of 13 from 2024. Figure 2–50 illustrates 

each virtual participant’s virtual portfolio for the year by both net megawatts cleared and profits 

before and after adjusting for fees.  

Figure 2–50  Virtual portfolio by market participant 

Eight participants accounted for just over 40% of the virtual profits after fees, which can also be 

referred to as net profits. These participants account for roughly 26.5% of the transactional 

volume in the market. Virtual trading generated profits before fees for 114 participants, and 

profits after fees for 96 participants. The total losses after fees amounted to roughly $12.1 

million, and five entities accounted for just over 69% of that loss.  

State of the Market 2025 

68 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Load and resources 

Additionally, Figure 2–50 highlights the disparity in the trading fees paid by each market 

participant. These fees totaled over $137.6 million in 2025; they include: virtual fees (3%), real-

time revenue neutrality uplift fees (33%), day-ahead make-whole payment fees (9%), and real-

time make-whole payment fees (55%). Virtual bids are subject to virtual fees, real-time revenue 

neutrality fees, and day-ahead make-whole payment fees. Virtual offers are subject to virtual 

fees, real-time revenue neutrality fees, and real-time make-whole payment fees. Nearly three-

quarters of the total fees assessed to virtual transactions are assessed only to virtual offers.  

The discrepancy in virtual fees relates to the quantity calculation associated with payers of real-

time make-whole payments – specifically, the real-time net settlement location deviation hourly 

amount. This determinant accounted for nearly 88% of the real-time make-whole payments in 

2025, or roughly $66 million. As the name implies, the quantity applied to applicable non-virtual 

transactions includes only the incremental deviations from day-ahead, however the quantities 

assessed to virtual offers include the full virtual offer quantity.  

This calculation methodology, when combined with the larger make-whole payments normally 

associated with real-time, generally leads to higher fees associated with virtual offers when 

compared to virtual bids. In 2025, the fees associated with virtual offers amounted to $2.58 per 

megawatt compared to $3.54 per megawatt for virtual bids. This calculation methodology and 

associated incentives could be part of the reason why virtual trading offsets only part of the 

under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market and should be considered as part of 

any analysis or evaluated as part of any potential solution to address price divergence. The 

market monitor will continue to evaluate these trends going forward.  

Cross-product market manipulation has been a concern in other RTO/ISO markets, and 

extensive monitoring is in place to detect potential cases in the SPP market. For example, a 

market participant may submit a money-losing virtual transaction intended to create congestion 

that benefits a transmission congestion right position for an overall net gain. Generally, this 

behavior shows up as a loss in one market, such as a virtual position, and a substantial 

associated benefit in another market, such as a transmission congestion right position. In the 

SPP market, only 8 market participants lost more than $100,000 in virtual transactions before 

fees, and 15 lost more than $100,000 in virtual transactions after fees in 2025. The market 

monitor reviews these outcomes and takes actions as needed. 

State of the Market 2025 

69 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

3  UNIT COMMITMENT AND DISPATCH PROCESSES  

This chapter covers unit commitment and dispatch and attempts to analyze the efficiency of the 

day-ahead and real-time markets. This covers not only physical energy, but the extent and 

means by which capacity is available to the market, the procurement and utilization of ancillary 

services, and responding to changes and deviations in real-time. Key highlights from this 

chapter include:  

•  Overall participation in the day-ahead market remains strong, though there are still 

gaps in participation, particularly among merchant-owned variable energy resources.  

•  The day-ahead market still shows several systemic inefficiencies. Self-commitments 

continue to make up a significant portion of started capacity, impacting price 

formation and efficient dispatch. Fast-start resources also continue to be started and 

committed in the day-ahead market, removing them from being available for quick 

start in real-time.  

•  The region had a large margin of available capacity compared to load obligation on 

average. However, there is a measurable amount of capacity that is either offered 

above economic maximum, in a reliability commitment status, or above emergency 

maximum. This capacity does not contribute to price formation and is either 

unavailable until a reliability event occurs or not at all. Fortunately, outages – the 

main cause of unavailable capacity – were down significantly in 2025. 

•  Accredited capacity had an average availability of 80% for winter and 89% for 

summer. While most resources offer in most of their accredited capacity, some 

capacity is either above economic maximum or above emergency maximum. This 

highlights the current lack of appropriate incentives for resources to remain available 

at their accreditation level during the resource adequacy season. 

•  Ancillary services scarcity remained largely flat, and most scarcity events only lasted 

one interval. Ramp was an exception; not only were there many more ramp-up 

scarcity events, but they also lasted longer than other product scarcity events, with 

only 35% of scarcity events lasting one interval. Ramp and uncertainty scarcity prices 

continued to increase year-over-year but are still very low.  

•  The MMU continues to have concerns with the ramp and uncertainty product 

designs. Much of the cleared ramp capacity came from resources that cannot deploy 

the needed ramp in real-time due to constraint limitations. The clearing of these 

non-deployable resources leads to artificially low market clearing prices since these 

State of the Market 2025 

70 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

resources usually do not have opportunity costs which are used to set the market 

clearing prices.  

3.1  COMMITMENT AND DISPATCH 

The Integrated Marketplace uses centralized unit commitment and dispatch. The goal of 

centralized unit commitment and dispatch is to efficiently schedule and dispatch generation 

resources to meet demand at the lowest system cost while respecting operational constraints 

and maintaining system stability and reliability.  

Currently, SPP employs five commitment processes. Figure 3-1 shows a timeline describing 

when SPP executes each process. 

Figure 3-1  Commitment process timeline 

Most commitments happen in the day-ahead market. The day-ahead market co-optimizes the 

clearing of energy and operating reserve products and attempts to commit sufficient offered 

capacity to meet the demand bid for each hour for the following operating day. Committing 

generation ahead of the operating day provides both economic and reliability benefits: it allows 

market participants to hedge against price volatility risk by locking in an hourly price and allows 

for a more efficient utilization of generation resources to manage system constraints, such as 

congestion.  

Some resources require longer lead times than afforded by a day-ahead commitment to be 

available to serve load on a given operating day. SPP utilizes multi-day reliability assessment 

(MDRA) for periods up to three days prior to an operating day to determine if any of these long 

lead-time generators will be needed for capacity or to address an emergency for a given 

operating day. Any generator committed from this process is treated as a “must commit” in the 

corresponding day-ahead market.  

As the day-ahead market works from expectations of the operating day including load forecasts, 

variable energy production forecasts, and resource availability, real-time conditions typically 

deviate from day-ahead and can require changes to resource commitments. Reliability unit 

commitment (RUC) processes and manual commitments can address deviations between the 

day-ahead market and real-time needs by committing additional capacity, or changing 

commitments after the day-ahead market has closed.  

State of the Market 2025 

71 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

The MMU monitors the commitment and subsequent dispatch of resources in the region over 

time as one measure of market health. Key indicators discussed in this section include the 

proportion of resource starts and commitments stemming from the day-ahead market, the 

proportion of capacity contributing to price formation, resource starts, and the utilization of 

fast-start resources. 

  COMMITMENT STATUS  

The effectiveness of the day-ahead market to secure adequate generation in advance of the 

operating day while minimizing total production costs relies on high participation rates in the 

among generation resources. Figure 3-2 shows the average day-ahead participation rate by 

resource type, owner type, and whether they are available for commitment or are not 

participating/on outage.

In 2025, the level of resource participation in the day-ahead market among load-serving entities 

was similar to levels seen in 2024. Load-serving entities, on average, offered 75% of non-variable 

energy resources into the day-ahead market while 24% had an outage status. Merchant 

generators continued to offer a higher share of their non-variable energy resources in market 

status (86% of capacity) compared to load-serving entities. However, merchant generators also 

placed a significant and growing portion of their variable energy resources (47%, compared to 

42% in 2024) in a not participating status whereas load-serving entities do not place any variable 

energy resource capacity in a not participating status.  

Part of the difference in participation rates between owner types stems from the Integrated 

Marketplace’s limited day-ahead must-offer provision. The must-provision was intended to 

incentivize load-serving entities with generation assets to participate in the day-ahead market. 

Market participants that are non-compliant are assessed a penalty based on the amount of 

capacity available in the day-ahead market relative to the market participant’s peak hourly real-

State of the Market 2025 

72 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

time load.50 In 2025, total must-offer penalties totaled $16,269. This is a notable decline from 

2024, when total penalties assessed were $79,205 assessed.  

While this provision does encourage available generation to be offered, participants are able to 

circumvent the requirement by registering load and generation under different asset owners. In 

addition, there are exceptions to the must-offer requirement; most notably, it exempts resources 

on outage. Finally, the day-ahead must-offer provision also does not tie into Attachment AA, 

which defines the resource adequacy requirement in the SPP tariff. The MMU continues to 

recommend updating the day-ahead must offer requirement. See further discussion in Chapter 

1.  

Resources offered in the market are available for commitment and dispatch to differing degrees. 

One component of availability is a resource’s commitment status. A resource that is participating 

in the day-ahead market that is not on outage must choose to either have a commitment status 

of market, self, or reliability. A market status tells SPP the resource is available for economic 

commitment. Capacity in a market status can be committed and dispatched up to its economic 

maximum. 

A self status communicates that a resource has committed itself and will be operating at its 

economic minimum unless dispatched up by the market clearing engine. These resources are 

price-takers up to their economic minimum. While there may be good reasons for self-

committing, the practice can distort prices, offer and bid behavior, market outcomes, and 

investment signals. This is particularly true if a large proportion of the region’s capacity 

frequently self-commits. Self-commitment can distort prices as the market clearing engine treats 

self-committed generators as price insensitive at their minimum. This shifts the supply curve to 

the right and leads to a corresponding decline in the marginal price of energy. 

Figure 3-3 shows day-ahead dispatch megawatts by fuel type by commitment type for each year 
of the study period.51 The volume of dispatched megawatts from self-committed resources 
remains a significant portion of the total dispatch megawatt volumes.52 For 2025, about a third 

of the energy produced was from self-committed resources and hence, was not economically

An alternative way to satisfy the provision is to offer all resource capacity not on outage.

Finally, a resource can choose a reliability commitment status. Reliability status communicates 

that the resource is offline and only available to be committed if there is an anticipated reliability 
issue.5455 These resources compete economically against other reliability status resources to fill 

in gaps after resources in self or market status have been committed.

grew to 9%. The “outage” commit status averaged 20%. The “self-commit” status averaged 

around 15% of total offered capacity, a figure that has remained roughly level since 2023. The

The day-ahead market is generally the preferred method of start-up for resources, particularly 

for those with longer lead times and less so for fast-start resources. However, a limiting factor 

on the number of day-ahead commitments is that the optimization algorithm is restricted to a 
48-hour window.61 Large base-load resources with long lead-times and long run times may not 

appear economic to the day-ahead market commitment algorithm. Some market participants 

choose to self-commit these resources at their economic minimum to have them online and 

available for incremental dispatch.  

In real-time, commitment flexibility is limited by resource start-up times. As the operating hour 

approaches, fewer resources are eligible to be started. The reliability unit commitment 
processes—day-ahead,62 intra-day, and short-term— together with manual commitments 

represented about 20% of the started capacity. Day-ahead RUC starts in 2025 were concentrated 

between hours starting 6 AM and 3 PM. The vast majority of intra-day RUC starts were spread 

across the hours between 5 AM and 4 PM, and short-term RUC starts peaked during the 

evening, which is when system load is peaking and is consistent with the pattern seen in prior 

years. The most common drivers of these commitments (and decommitments) result from

Figure 3-6 shows start-up instructions issued by fuel type. For gas resources, most start-ups

gas resources starts from the intra-day RUC has also decreased, falling 12% in 2022 to about 7% 

in 2025, while manual commitments and short-term RUC commitments have increased. 

  PHYSICAL PARAMETERS 

Physical parameter values also determine a resource’s availability and ability to be committed 

and dispatched. SPP allows participants to self-report the physical operating parameters of each 

resource. Per the market protocols, these physical operating parameters should reflect the 

underlying physical capabilities of a resource.  

A resource’s physical parameters determine how often and how quickly a resource can be 

started, how long a resource must run once online, and how quickly a resource can change its 

output (ramp), among other things. Resources with physical parameters that limit how quickly it 

can change its output and/or its availability for commitment or dispatch are, in general, less 

flexible. Moreover, a resource’s physical parameters can indicate what services, outside of 

State of the Market 2025 

77 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

energy, a resource can provide the market. This includes regulation, ramp, uncertainty, 

contingency, and quick-start capability. The growth in variable energy resources has increased 

the demand for ancillary services, such as regulation, ramp, and uncertainty, and requires a 

system with more flexibility to respond to sudden changes in output. Thus, to be effective and 

efficient, an accurate representation of the physical capabilities for resources continues to grow 

in importance. 

Figure 3-7 provides a summary of the average values for a subset of physical parameters across 

the fleet, both weighted by capacity and a straight average across all resources. Additionally, as 

resources with a common fuel frequently have relatively similar physical capabilities, the table 

also shows these values by fuel type.

The two nuclear units are the least flexible with almost no dispatchable range (economic 

maximum minus economic minimum). While they report relatively short minimum run and down 

times and start up times, these resources are exclusively self-committed, making run and startup 

times less relevant. Coal units, which are generally older steam turbines, making up roughly 21% 

of the region’s installed capacity, have long startup times, slow ramp rates, and long minimum 

run times. Natural gas resources, which consist of a combination of steam and combustion 

turbines, have much shorter startup times, faster ramp rates, and an average minimum run time 

of just shy of one day. These resources are much more flexible and account for roughly 35% of 

the region’s installed capacity. The most flexible resources are solar, water, and wind resources 

and, to a slightly lesser degree, oil resources. Though providing the most flexibility, most are 

State of the Market 2025 

78 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

variable energy resources which represent the highest uncertainty in available capacity and are 
the least controllable by SPP.63 Together, these resources make up 39% of the region’s installed 

capacity.  

The centralized unit commitment process is most efficient when physical parameters reflect the 

physical capabilities of a resource. However, because physical parameters are self-reported, 

opportunities exist to set physical parameters in such a way as to significantly influence or 

hedge against certain outcomes related to commitment and dispatch. Examples of this behavior 

that the MMU has observed include: 

•  Changing real-time economic minimum operating limits in the real-time balancing 

market to match day-ahead commitment and clearing. This allows resources to avoid 

being dispatched below their day-ahead clearing in real-time to serve a contract or 

hedge against buying back their day-ahead position at a loss while ramp-constrained.  

•  Depressing ramp-down rates to avoid becoming ramp constrained and missing price 

spikes. This allows inframarginal resources to avoid being ramped down during a 

transient downward price excursion, becoming ramp constrained and missing out on 

producer surplus or potentially buying back a lucrative day-ahead position at a loss, 

when prices rebound. Limiting ramp rates creates unnecessary inflexibility in the fleet of 

resources managed by SPP, and leads to an inefficient, more costly dispatch solution.  

•  Extending minimum runtimes beyond the day-ahead economic evaluation window (two 

days). The day-ahead market solution minimizes forecasted production costs, including 

uplift, across a two-day window to help normalize production costs across resource 

types. Resources with more expensive starts may be the best economic choice when 

evaluated over the two-day period. However, if the resource with a minimum runtime 

longer than two days is no longer economic after the evaluation period, it is treated as a 

“must run” for the remainder of its minimum runtime and is eligible to be made whole to 

its mitigated costs. The minimum runtime parameter can be used to ensure a resource 

meets contractual obligations or to burn its fuel as a hedge against a take or pay 

contract. 

Using physical parameters to effectively override what would otherwise be the outcome of the 

central commitment and dispatch process leads to sub-optimal, inefficient market outcomes. 

Moreover, this behavior can be a means to exert market power by changing the quantity and 

63 In February 2026, SPP opened SIR839 to conduct an analysis that aims in part to evaluate the impact of 
resource uncertainty on the need for ramp and uncertainty products. 

State of the Market 2025 

79 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

potentially the price of energy, but not to a degree that would fail SPP’s conduct and impact 

test.  

  FAST START RESOURCES 

In contrast to resources where start times can restrict a resource’s availability for commitment, 

fast-start resources are those that are dispatchable, can start, synchronize, reach its minimum 

operating limit within 10 minutes of SPP notification, and have a minimum run time of one hour 

or less. Market storage resources with a discharge time of 60 minutes or less also meet the fast-

start criteria.  

Fast-start resources can sit off-line near real-time and respond to ramp and uncertainty needs, 

but only if they are not committed in the day-ahead market. Historically, SPP has seen a large 

percentage of the fast-start resources clearing in the day-ahead market. Figure 3-8 shows the 

megawatts cleared by fast start resources in the day-ahead market compared to megawatts 

dispatched incremental to day-ahead in real-time.  

Figure 3-8 

Fast-start resources, day-ahead cleared vs. incremental real-time 

In 2025, 90% of megawatts coming from fast-start resources came from the day-ahead. This is 

lower than the previous two years, where the percentage of fast-start resources cleared in the 

day-ahead was 96% and 95% in 2023 and 2024, respectively. The above chart also highlights 

that in 2025, the total megawatts from resources meeting the fast-start criteria decreased 

notably compared to the prior two years. This decline is in part due to a general decline in 

generation from fast-start resources and fewer resources offering in a manner that make them 

qualified as fast-start compared to prior years. This occurs when a resource’s physical 

parameters no longer meet the fast-start requirements.

80 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

3.2  CAPACITY AVAILABILITY 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Whether and how a resource can be committed in the market is one element of commitment 

and dispatch. How much capacity is available and by what means ultimately determines whether 

the region can meet its load and reserve obligations. A primary determination of how much 

capacity is available is how market participants structure their resource offers. The more capacity 

that is above a resource’s economic maximum, above a resource’s emergency maximum, or that 

is on outage, the less capacity is available to the market clearing engine. Decreases in capacity 

available during the normal unit commitment and economic dispatch processes effects 

efficiency and reliability.  

The MMU tracks the availability of the region’s capacity to identify potential supply adequacy 

risks, offer behavior concerns, and inefficiencies between the resource adequacy processes 

and real-time operations. Key indicators discussed in this section include how resources offer 

capacity to the market, the availability of capacity compared to regional load obligation, and 

outages.  

  CAPACITY STATUS 

Whether and by how capacity translates into system reliability and economic efficiency depends 

on both the extent that is available and by what means. To the former, availability represents 

how much of the registered capacity is available to be committed and subsequently dispatched 

to serve load and ancillary service requirements. Regarding the latter, when and by what means 

capacity is available depends on how the capacity is offered to the region. Figure 3-9 provides a 

breakout of capacity available by how it is offered to the market. 

Figure 3-9  Capacity status by type 

State of the Market 2025 

81 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Capacity offered up to a resource’s economic maximum is available to the market clearing 

engine to serve the region’s load obligation under normal operating conditions. As economic 

capacity is readily available and transparently priced, reliability, liquidity, competition, and price 
formation are best supported when most registered capacity falls into this category.64 In 2025, 

average hourly capacity available economically to the market in real-time increased slightly to 

62 GW in 2025, up from 56.8 GW in 2024. Relative to load, available capacity represented 181% 

of the region’s load obligation on average, up from 170% in 2024. 

Capacity above a resource’s emergency maximum or submitted in reliability status is, by 

contrast, only available if SPP determines there is a reliability event. Capacity above economic 

maximum often either represents an operational level that may be riskier or that can only be 

sustained for short periods. Resources using a reliability commitment status tend to be those 

where, if called upon, may have limitations that prevent the resource from being available later. 

Thus, this capacity truly is less available than that offered up to a resource’s economic maximum. 

In 2025, roughly 4,600 MW were either offered in a reliability status or above a resource’s 

economic maximum on average. This is a material increase from 2024 where this value was 

closer to 4,000 MW.  

The rest of the capacity is unavailable to the region even in the case of a reliability event. 

Capacity that is above a resource’s emergency maximum but below its registered maximum is 

not offered. This missing capacity, averaging 7,800 MW, could be the result of outages and 

derates that did not meet the reporting thresholds, registered maximum values that overstated 

resources’ current capabilities, or physical withholding. There is also considerable capacity 

represented by the difference between the registered maximums of variable energy resources 

(VERs) and their collective potential output (forecast, excluding any curtailments). This capacity, 

averaging almost 22,000 MW, is the largest single source of unavailable capacity and is 

unavailable to the region due to lack of underlying fuel, such as wind or water. Curtailments 

represent the megawatts from VERs that were not utilized due to dispatch instructions. 

Curtailments can be driven by economics or physical transmission constraints. Curtailments 

average just over 1,300 MW and were lowest during the summer peak and highest during the 

spring shoulder season. Finally, outages and derates account for as little as 5,000 MW and as 

much as over 15,000 MW on average. The largest driver of higher average outage rates are 

planned and maintenance outages, which largely occur during the shoulder seasons when the 

region has greater available maintenance margin. 

64 It is important to note that capacity up to the economic minimum of a resources using a self-commit 
status is an exception. While it is available capacity, it is not dispatchable and, as discussed in the resource 
starts section, can distort price formation. 

State of the Market 2025 

82 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Aside from availability, the mix of resources underpinning the region’s capacity can impact 

system reliability and market efficiency. Particularly relevant to the SPP region is the growth in 

variable energy resources (VERs) as a proportion of the total registered capacity. Growth in the 

proportion of VERs increases the importance of fully controllable resources to fill in when VER 

production drops.  

Figure 3-10 compares average available internal conventional capacity (measured by emergency 

maximum) and load plus contingency reserves by day for 2025. The vast majority of days, the 

region had, on average, a margin of available conventional capacity versus load obligation of 

60% to 100%. 

Figure 3-10  Conventional capacity available versus load, 2025 

Metrics that reflect daily, monthly, or seasonal averages do not reflect the extremes of the 

period. Reliability requires planning for and mitigating those extremes. Figure 3-11 shows 

average load as a percentage of available capacity (as measured by economic maximum) along 

with the maximum percentage of capacity load reached by month. This shows that average 

monthly load stayed around 57% of available capacity across 2025 while maximum ranged from 

65% in March to 95% in August.

Figure 3-12  Average and minimum percent available capacity 

SPP enforces a resource adequacy requirement (RAR) during the summer and winter seasons. 

The RAR requires all load responsible entities to accredit enough capacity to cover their net 

peak load plus the planning reserve margin (PRM). For the resource adequacy requirement to be 

effective in ensuring reliability, the accreditation process should render an accredited capacity 

value that reflects true availability of a resource, particularly when it is most needed by the 

region. 

Figure 3-13 shows the average daily real-time availability of accredited capacity that is 

registered internally to SPP (and hence, whose performance is directly measurable) throughout 

State of the Market 2025 

84 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

winter 2024-2025 (December 1 through March 31) and summer 2025 (June 1 through 

September 30) resource adequacy seasons.  

Figure 3-13  Availability of accredited capacity 

As demonstrated by Figure 3-13, availability of accredited capacity differs significantly between 

the winter and summer seasons. On average, 70% of accredited capacity was offered 

economically during the winter season versus 80% during the summer season. Accounting for 

capacity offered via resources’ emergency maxes, these values increase to 77% and 87%, 

respectively. 

Availability was also significantly lower during the first and last 15 days of the seasons, which are 

exempted from the expectation of availability requirement for accredited capacity. During the 

first 15 days, maintenance outages from the shoulder season are typically ending; during the last 

15, new maintenance outages begin as the region enters a period of lower demand. This 

increase in the amount of capacity on maintenance outage explains most of the depressed 

availability at either end of the resource adequacy season. Examining availability during only the 

3 months during which the expectation of availability applies, average availability increases to 

80% for winter and 89% for summer using emergency maximums. These values are 74% and 

82% for winter and summer, respectively, using economic maximums.  

Currently, the tariff lacks appropriate incentives for resources to remain available at their 

accreditation level during the resource adequacy season. For conventional resources, the current 

accreditation process utilizes a capability test adjusted for a forced outage rate to determine 

accreditation. In real-time, there is no requirement for accredited resources to offer in this full 

capacity as there is no link between the real-time must offer requirement and accreditation 

value.  

State of the Market 2025 

85 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Additionally, a small but significant amount of accredited capacity comes from a range typically 

above resources’ economic maximum; another portion comes from resources using a reliability 

commitment status. The MMU remains concerned over the practice of accrediting resources up 

to a capacity amount that is generally only reflected in a resource’s emergency maximum and 

not defining a limited set of circumstances that accredited capacity may be offered in a 

reliability commitment status. This creates a potential reliability risk as while this capacity is 

technically available for reliability, its true availability and risk is less known. In some cases, 

increasing a unit’s output level to its emergency maximum can put it at an increased risk of 

tripping offline, leaving the region without access to the entire capacity of that accredited 

resource. Capacity coming from resources in a reliability commitment status is not generally 

online and the risk of a fail to start could limit the value of this capacity.  

Without a mechanism to incentivize or ensure availability, there is a disconnect between the 

capacity SPP accredits for resource adequacy and the actual capacity available to operate the 

system reliably. SPP and its stakeholders should continue to work on new mechanisms and 

evaluate the efficacy of current incentives that reward resources that perform more reliably and 

are available when needed by SPP operations. The MMU also continues to recommend that a 

resource adequacy requirement be applied to all four seasons. While forecasted demand tends 

to be lower in the shoulder seasons, the region continues to see more intervals where SPP must 

declare a Resource Advisory or above during these times.  

Overall, the MMU has the same four critical recommendations related to supply adequacy, with 

slight modifications based on lessons learned over the past three years: 

1)  If SPP is to rely on any resource to provide energy, then that resource should be 

available. The resource adequacy process should consider all outages to represent 

expected availability more accurately. It should also include an analysis of outage 

correlation between resources in similar areas using the same fuels and/or technologies 

and across all seasons. Deliverability should also be considered, like in the effective load 

carrying capability methodology.  

2)  There should be meaningful incentives related to reliability. There should be market, out-

of-market, and/or regulatory policy mechanisms to incentivize reliability attributes. These 

attributes include but are not limited to flexibility on outage timing and duration, dual-

fuel capability, and winterization. 

3)  A more frequent resource adequacy requirement, such as a seasonal requirement, should 

be developed. Our data demonstrate low availability levels during the shoulder seasons 

State of the Market 2025 

86 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

when most planned outages are taken. The MMU recommends exploring the feasibility 

of a four-season resource adequacy requirement.  

4)  SPP should plan for shocks to generator availability including adverse weather events, 

pipeline outages, wind turbine icing, and solar eclipses. Express attention should be paid 

to the pattern and correlation of outages between resource types and locations. SPP 

should implement mitigation measures to ensure the reliable operation of the grid under 

all circumstances. This may include incentivizing strategic investments in transmission to 

and development of energy storage resources, and expanding the market to handle 

price sensitive demand. 

  RESOURCE OUTAGES 

Resource outages are the main contributor to resource unavailability for both accredited and 

unaccredited capacity. Two major reasons for resource outages are generator maintenance and 

a forced event, such as an equipment failure. Maintenance outages are those submitted to SPP 

for study and approval ahead of time. As the reliability coordinator and balancing authority, SPP 

determines a maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity that can be approved for 

planned and maintenance generator outages at a given time without creating a reliability risk. 

SPP approves, denies, or reschedules these outages to ensure system reliability using the 
maintenance margin and following the outage coordination methodology.65 

Forced outages, by contrast, are generally unpredictable, resulting from sudden equipment 

failure, fuel supply disruptions, or extreme events such as icing or flooding. However, the MMU 

has also seen instances where market participants force a resource outage for economic 

reasons, such as not wanting to staff the resource or to take an outage that was not approved 

by SPP. Forced outages are not known or studied in advance by SPP operations and may pose a 

reliability risk, especially during periods of capacity scarcity. As such, minimizing the frequency 

and duration of forced outages is a critical piece of ensuring the reliable operation of the grid. 

This requires creating the right incentives for resources to mitigate exposure to potential shocks 

such as freezing and penalizing resources that use forced outages inappropriately. 

Figure 3-14 shows capacity derated and taken out-of-service by reason by resource fuel type. 

Overall, from 2024 to 2025, there was a decrease in total outaged capacity of roughly 16%, 

driven by a 26% decrease in forced outages. As a result, maintenance outages represented the 

largest portion of outage capacity. In previous years, forced outages accounted for a measurably 

larger portion as compared to maintenance outages. While it is unclear why this may be the 

65 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology.

case, the implementation of the new performance-based accreditation methodology for

shifted outage behavior. Resources could either be requesting more outages as planned or 

maintenance or may be delaying outages until they can be considered planned or maintenance 

rather than simply taking a forced outage when maintenance margin is unavailable.  

Figure 3-14  Resource outages and derates 

Coal provided 29% of total energy produced and accounted for about 25% of outaged capacity

Correlated output, including correlated outages, is a growing area of concern in SPP. In 2018, 

SPP and stakeholders recognized that maintaining reliability as the region sees a large increase 

in variable energy resources, whose output tends to be highly correlated, requires a new 

approach to accreditation for these resources. Following the industry trend, SPP filed a tariff 

revision with FERC in February 2024 that would adopt an effective load carrying capability 

State of the Market 2025 

89 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

methodology for accreditation of variable energy resources to explicitly account for the 

reliability risk associated with their output that is both highly correlated and not fully 

controllable. However, variable energy resources are not the only resources that pose reliability 

risks due to correlated output. In 2024, SPP also filed a winter season only, fuel assurance 

methodology with FERC that would act as an addition to the proposed accreditation process for 

conventional resources. The fuel assurance methodology further adjusts a resource’s 

accreditation for out of management control, fuel-related forced outages that were incremental 

to the forced outage baseline used as part of the loss of load expectation study. By design, this 

policy only captures one specific driver of correlated outages among conventional resources and 

only during the winter season. 

Figure 3-17 and Figure 3-18 show the distribution of the percentage of capacity on forced 

outage by fuel type during the summer season and winter season, respectively. While 

percentage of fleet is a good measure of correlation between outages, the reliability impact of 

these correlated outages also depends on how many resources are on outage and the amount 

of capacity on outage. If a small percentage of coal resources is on outage but this represents a 

large amount of capacity, this could present a greater reliability issue than a larger percentage 

of oil units on outage that represents a small amount of total capacity. Thus, the call outs 

identify the median amount of capacity and number of units on forced outage by fuel type.

Figure 3-18  Percent of fleet on concurrent forced outages, summer 

The region experiences outage correlation among resources of the same fuel type to a 

measurable degree in both winter and summer. Historically and in 2025, the region saw a 

greater median amount of capacity on outage at the same time in the winter as compared to 

summer. The expected number of outages occurring at the same time among resources of the 

same type (represented by the distributions in Figure 3-17 and Figure 3-18) varies to a greater 

degree in summer. This indicates that while winter may see more correlated outages, summer 

may experience greater unpredictability.  

Though wind resources typically had the lowest percent of their total capacity on forced outage 

at the same time, in 2025, hydropower resources had the lowest (3% in winter, 2% in summer). 

Coal resources once again had the highest proportion of their capacity on outage at the same 

time with 12% in the winter and 8% in the summer. Natural gas resources had the largest 

difference in the median amount of capacity on outage at the same time between winter and 

summer. For these resources, the median amount of capacity on outage at any given time is 

significantly higher in the winter (10%) versus the summer (4%). In terms of megawatts of 

capacity, the amount of capacity on outage simultaneously was higher in the winter for all fuel 

types. This is not surprising as the region sees the highest rates of forced outages during 

periods of prolonged cold and winter storms.  

Correlated forced outages can pose a challenge for procuring adequate reserves, managing 

congestion, reliability, or a combination of the three and should be accounted for in the supply 

adequacy process. The data above suggests that correlated outages among conventional 

resources do have a significant impact on availability in both the winter and summer and hence, 

should be considered in the accreditation process. The MMU recommends better integrating 

correlated outages into the accreditation methodology for conventional resources in place of 

the fuel assurance policy that is limited in scope and application.  

State of the Market 2025 

91 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

In addition to identifying correlated outages, having the ability to understand the causes allows 

for a better prediction of forced outages and to take steps to mitigate their causes. Currently, 

outage data are often incomplete or insufficient to determine driving causes behind forced 

outages. More accurate, complete, and standardized outage data would allow SPP and the 

MMU to identify common causes of forced outages. 

  OUTAGE COORDINATION 

To balance the need to perform routine and preventative maintenance and to maintain grid 

reliability, SPP uses a centralized outage coordination process. This process includes setting a 

maintenance margin, which is the amount of capacity that can safely be on planned or 

maintenance outage based on expected grid conditions at a given time. Numerous factors, 

including an aging fleet, generator retirements, and an increase in variable energy resource 

penetration have led to a narrowing maintenance margin over time. This means the accuracy 

and transparency of the outage scheduling process is ever more important to ensure outages 

are efficiently prioritized and approved.  

Contributing to the efficiency of the outage coordination process is outage submittal data. The 

outage coordination methodology requires a reason and planned end date for the outage at 
both the time of the outage submittal and at the submittal of each change.66 SPP uses these 

data to study each outage to determine the priority of the outage, if the outage as submitted 

will pose any reliability risks, and to ensure the total amount of capacity on outage does not 

exceed the maintenance margin during the proposed timeframe. Because outage submissions 

are intended to be used for assessing real-time and future reliability of the bulk electric system, 
it is critical that the outage information submitted to SPP is accurate.67  

The MMU has observed insufficient, omitted, delayed, and incorrect outage information. The 

MMU maintains material misstatements of outage information could be considered providing 
false information to the RTO and may result in referral to FERC.68 However, these data are hard if 

not impossible to validate after the fact. Instead, greater care should be taken at the time of 

submission to ensure that outages are not studied or approved until all necessary data are 

submitted and validated for accuracy.  

Another area of concern is outage extensions, particularly those that are greater than a week. An 

outage’s original scope of work cannot always be completed in the original scheduled 

66 A forced outage can be submitted with an unknown cause, but the cause and planned end date are 
required to be updated promptly as soon as more information is known. 
67 SPP Reliability Coordinator Outage Coordination Methodology, Section 1 
68 18 CFR § 35.41 

State of the Market 2025 

92 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

timeframe. The current outage coordination methodology allows an outage to be extended and 

gives extensions priority for approval, similar to forced outages. As such, extensions have the 

potential to be prioritized higher than new outages, resulting in denial of the new outages. In 

addition, outage extensions decrease the amount of available maintenance margin and, due to 

their unexpected nature, can adversely affect other processes such as the congestion hedging 

market. Figure 3-19 shows generator outage extensions that are greater than one week, by 

duration.  

Figure 3-19  Generator outage extension count by duration, greater than one week 

In 2025, there were 967 outages extended longer than one week, a four percent increase from 

2024. Of the five outage extension duration categories, one week to one month experienced the 

largest increase of any category, which experienced an increase of eight percent since 2024. The 

MMU continues to encourage SPP to implement processes and mechanisms to properly 

schedule, classify, report, and prioritize outage extensions.  

The MMU continues to remain concerned about the improper use of outage extensions, 

especially outage extensions with a long duration. The MMU has observed outages extended for 

reasons different than the original outage reason, resulting in misclassified and nontransparent 

outage extensions. For some performance-based resource adequacy calculations, misclassified 

outages could affect the amount of capacity accredited under SPP’s performance-based 

accreditation approach. 

Though there are legitimate reasons for resources to be on outage, proper incentives and 

procedures can promote efficiency, reliability, and proper outage behavior. To these ends, the 

MMU recommends SPP: 

•  Bolster the outage coordination methodology by requiring all necessary supporting 

information be submitted before an outage can be submitted for review and approval. In 

State of the Market 2025 

93 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

addition, SPP should set in place a process for verifying and validating the submitted 

data to ensure the proper outage priority and status are assigned.  

•  Validate that an outage extension is needed to finish the original outage’s scope of work. 

When this cannot be validated, SPP should treat the extension as a new outage request. 

•  Develop a clear link between the outage coordination and resource adequacy processes 

to ensure transparency and accountability. This will not only improve the accuracy of the 

accreditation process but will provide a proper incentive for resources to not misclassify 

or misuse outages.  

3.3  ANCILLARY SERVICES 

The physical constraints of generators and the resource grid along with uncertainty create the 

need not just for energy, but for certain attributes tied to the production of that energy. These 

ancillary services, such as regulation, spin, and ramp, help the grid react to sudden changes and 

keep voltage and frequency within allowable tolerances while avoiding load shed. This section 

looks at the region’s demand and utilization of ancillary services. 

The MMU measures and tracks trends in ancillary services. Of primary concern in this section 

is the clearing, availability, and pricing of these products. The MMU places special emphasis 

on analyzing pricing and procurement outcomes for ramp and uncertainty that could suggest 

design issues with these products. 

  REGULATION AND CONTINGENCY RESERVE 

When an insufficient amount of regulation-up service, regulation-down service, or contingency 

reserve is cleared, a scarcity price is set by a demand curve. The regulation demand curves, for 

both up and down, consist of six steps with a maximum price of $600/MW. The contingency 

reserve demand curve consists of three steps with a maximum price of $1,100/MW.  

The scarcity of ancillary service products can be caused by a lack of capacity or a lack of ramp.69 

Scarcities are due to capacity when there are insufficient resources at maximum output available 

to meet demand. Scarcities are due to ramp when sufficient capacity is available, but ramp rate 

limitations do not allow access to the full capacity. When multiple products compete for the 

same, limited capability of resources, the scarcity of one product can also raise the price of other 

products.  

69 The clearing engine does not record the reason for the scarcity, (i.e. whether it is caused by a lack of 
capacity or ramp.) The MMU suggests that SPP captures the appropriate information so that the reason for 
the scarcity will be transparent. 

State of the Market 2025 

94 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes

intervals in the real-time market and average prices for each product by month, along with an 

annual average of monthly values. We have not included a day-ahead chart as there was no 

day-ahead scarcity for regulation or contingency reserve in 2025. 

Figure 3-20  Scarcity intervals and prices, regulation and operating reserves, real-

The number of real-time regulation-up scarcity intervals was level with 2024, occurring an 

average of 63 intervals per month. Regulation-down and contingency reserve scarcity intervals 

were both reduced in 2025 at 1 and 9 intervals per month, respectively, compared to 4 and 10 in 

2024, respectively. The average number of real-time scarcity intervals per month held mostly flat 

in 2025 when compared to 2024 and 2023, with slight decreases in regulation-down and 

operating reserve scarcity each year.  

While the number of intervals with scarcity was an overall improvement, average real-time 

scarcity prices for these three products increased: the average real-time scarcity prices from

$215/MW for regulation-down (up from $159/MW in 2024 and $185/MW in 2023), and 

$546/MW for contingency reserve (up from $410/MW in 2024 and $450/MW in 2023).
