Operating reserve scarcity intervals peaked in September and October with roughly a quarter of 

scarce intervals occurring on just two days in September. 

  RAMP 

In the 2017 annual report, the market monitor recommended that SPP create a ramp capability 

product. Historically, SPP operators procured ramp through out-of-market mechanisms, often 

resulting in uplift payments. The ramp capability product was designed to allow for more 

economical and transparent management of the intermittent aspects of the system. The product 

optimizes the resources’ dispatch instructions over a ten-minute period to allocate any 

economically available ramp for the interval starting ten minutes in the future. Future ramp 

needs get met by pre-positioning online resources with available ramp if the cost of this action 

is less than the applicable ramp-scarcity demand curve price. The ramp requirement is set to 

State of the Market 2025 

97 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

procure enough ramp to meet forecasted net load changes plus an amount to cover unexpected 

net load changes based on historical needs and the market-clearing price is set by the 

opportunity cost of providing other products.  

Figure 3-23 and Figure 3-24 below show the day-ahead and real-time cleared ramp-up 

megawatt hours for each product by fuel type.  

Figure 3-23  Cleared ramp-up by resource type, day-ahead 

Figure 3-24  Cleared ramp-up by resource type, real-time 

As these two charts illustrate, a large percentage of ramp capability-up is cleared by wind and 

natural gas resources. These figures also show that wind resources clear a greater percentage in 

the day-ahead market than the real-time market. Forty-eight percent of the ramp capability-up 

cleared in the day-market was cleared by wind, as compared to only 34% cleared by wind 

resources in real time. This percentage gap was much larger in the previous two years, but the 

State of the Market 2025 

98

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

influx of new demand response, represented under “other”, and market storage resources in 

2025 has cleared some of the ramp-up previously cleared by wind.  

Figure 3-25 shows ramp-up capability scarcity in the day-ahead and real-time markets.70  

Figure 3-25  Ramp-up scarcity intervals and prices  

Real-time ramp capability-up scarcity intervals peaked in May 2025 at 995 intervals and were 

generally highest from April through June. The highest average monthly real-time scarcity price 

(averaging $26/MW) occurred in November, and average real-time scarcity prices were generally 

higher towards the end of the year, beginning in September, despite the number of real-time 

ramp capability up scarcity intervals being generally lower during this period. Overall, ramp 

capability-up had an average of 475 scarce intervals per month in 2025, down slightly from 489 

in 2024 and 626 in 2023. The overall average scarcity price for real-time ramp capability-up in 

2025 was $22/MW, a significant increase over the $17/MW of 2024 and 2023. During ramp-up 

shortages in real-time, the median ramp-up cleared was 86% of its requirement, and the median 

shadow price was $14/MW.  

In winter 2022, the MMU evaluated the effectiveness of the newly implemented ramp product.71 

The MMU was particularly concerned with the trend of day-ahead ramp capability up scarcity 

events being roughly two and a half times the frequency of real-time ramp capability up scarcity 

events. This is not a logical trend as there is far less uncertainty in the day-ahead market than 

the real-time market. In the day-ahead market, resources are considered to always stay on 

dispatch, forecasted outputs are assumed to be in line with forecasted quantities, and resources 

70 There has been no ramp-down capability scarcity since the introduction of the ramp product in March 
2022. 
71 The MMU identified several concerns which can be found in the MMU comments in Docket No. ER20-
1617. 

State of the Market 2025 

99 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

are not considered to trip. These assumptions do not hold true in the real-time market, so 

typically there are far more regulation and operating reserve shortages due to ramp limitations 

in real-time than day-ahead. 

The MMU discovered the disparity between day-ahead and real-time ramp shortages has to do 

with how ramp capability-up is accessed for clearing. Resources owners do not offer a cost for 

either ramp capability-up or -down. Instead, the products clear solely based off their 

opportunity costs for not clearing other products, such as energy. For instance, if a resource had 

an energy locational marginal price of $35/MWh and marginal energy cost of $30/MWh, the 

opportunity cost for ramp capability-up would be $5/MWh. This means that the price for ramp 

capability-up will need to be at least $5/MWh for this resource to clear the product. However, all 

dispatchable resource types with available ramp may participate in clearing this product, and 

congestion is not considered in the clearing. This means that a resource behind a constraint may 

have a -$65/MWh energy locational marginal price and marginal energy cost of $10/MWh. In 

this case, this resources opportunity cost would be -$55/MWh. The market-clearing engine 

considers all negative opportunity cost to have $0/MWh opportunity costs.  

This causes a disconnect between the assumptions used in the clearing of the ramp capability 

product and the actual deployment of the ramp. When the market clearing engine deploys the 

ramp cleared behind the constraint, it is often too expensive to meet the ramp needs in the 

market. This can make the market go short of products, when the cost of congestion relief is 

higher than the scarcity demand curves. In the example above, the -$55/MWh opportunity cost 

will likely get more negative as the resource gets dispatched up for ramp. When the costs to 

ramp-up the resource get higher than the scarcity or violation relaxation limits the market will 

go scarce the product and price it with the associated scarcity price.  

Figure 3-26 below shows the 2025 megawatts of ramp capability-up cleared on resources with 

shift factors on a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market.  

State of the Market 2025 

100 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3-26  Real-time cleared ramp-up effect on constraints 

The above graph illustrates that there is a large percentage of ramp capability-up being cleared 

behind a binding or breached constraint in the real-time market. The negative values represent 

resources with shift factors that increase congestion on a constraint, thus decreasing the ability 

to deliver ramp-up to the market. This is a leading contributor to the market not being able to 

use the ramp procured. SPP evaluated the effects of ramp-up clearing behind and the constraint 
and found that much of ramp cleared was behind binding constraints. 72 SPP said they intended 

to work on this issue. However, these efforts have been halted as SPP would like to address all 

ancillary service products clearing behind constraints.  

The MMU continues to express the following concerns with the performance of the ramp 

capability product: 

1.  Variable energy resources cannot produce regulation-up in the SPP market. However, 
48% of the day-ahead ramp-up and 34% of the 2025 real-time ramp-up product was 
cleared on variable energy resources. 

2.  The scarcity demand curve prices for the ramp capability up product may be set too low. 
There is a high frequency of ramp-up scarcity events, especially when taking in the fact 
that non-deployable ramp-up is being cleared to meet the requirement. 

3.  The real-time dispatch does not consider future intervals. It simply calculates one value: a 

dispatch instruction for the next interval. While the real-time balancing market considers 

a resource’s ramp capability for the purpose of calculating the dispatch instruction for 

the next interval, ramp is not considered for any interval after that. Prior to the ramp 

product’s implementation, ramp needs were not accounted for in terms of the 

72 Strategic Road Map Revision 424, currently closed.  

State of the Market 2025 

101 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

subsequent dispatch instructions even though ramp is the very capability that allows a 

resource to get to future dispatch instructions. 

Not having ramp capability considered for future intervals can cause an inability in the market 

clearing engine to procure enough energy to serve the load or provide sufficient operating 

reserves in those future intervals. Even when enough capacity is available, a lack of ramp renders 

that capacity temporarily unreachable. Moreover, sufficient ramp has typically been offered, by 

the market participants, but the clearing process has not left enough available for future use. 
When this occurs in the pricing run, it often leads to short-term transitory price spikes.73  

  UNCERTAINTY 

SPP implemented an uncertainty product on July 6, 2023. The product’s objective is similar to 

the ramp product in that it was designed to reorganize the market dispatch to efficiently ensure 

rampable capacity for future needs while effectively pricing and paying for the procured 

rampable capacity. There are two main differences between the uncertainty product and the 

ramp product, which was implemented in 2022. First, the ramp product has a 10-minute 

assessment horizon while the uncertainty product has a one-hour horizon, for which they can 

both procure ramp. Second, the uncertainty product allows clearing from offline and online 

resources, while the ramp product only clears from online resources. Online resources clear 

based off opportunity costs, as described in 3.3.2 for the ramp product, where offline resources 

provide competitive offers.  

Figure 3-27 illustrates the monthly gigawatt clearings for both day-ahead offline and online 

uncertainty, while Figure 3-28 illustrates the real-time cleared megawatts for both offline and 

online resources.  

73 This is essentially temporal, or time-based, congestion. 

State of the Market 2025 

102 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3-27  Uncertainty product cleared, day-ahead 

Figure 3-28  Uncertainty product cleared, real-time 

The average monthly day-ahead uncertainty cleared in 2025 was approximately 19 GWh, up 

from the 2024 and 2023 respective monthly averages of 5 GWh and 1.9 GWh. Real-time 

uncertainty clearing averaged 20 GWh per month in 2025, 5 GWh in 2024 and 2.2 GWh in 2023. 

The big jump in clearing can be attributed to a change in SPP’s method for setting the ramp 
product requirement. 74  The requirement for the uncertainty product prior to October 2024 was 

being set by looking at the forecasted net-load increase over the uncertainty horizon then 
adding the 97th percentile of the forecast errors from the prior twelve months. This derived 

amount is called the flexibility requirement. Once the flexibility requirement was set; the ramp 

capability-up, regulation-up, and contingency reserve requirements were backed out. Backing 

these other requirements out frequently led to the uncertainty product’s requirement to be set

at zero, which also set the price a zero dollars. The Market Working Group voted in May 2024 to 

update the requirement to only back out the ramp capability-up product’s requirement from the 
flexibility requirement. 75 This lead to an uptick in cleared megawatts and prices. These charts do 

not depict the megawatts coming from soon-to-start resources. 

Soon-to-start resources are resourbelow. .ted to start during the uncertainty horizon. These 

resources provide ramp to the market, but are not paid through settlements, nor are they able 

to set price as their opportunity costs is considered to be zero dollars. In 2025 6% of the total 

day-ahead uncertainty clearing was met with soon-to-start resources compared to 15% of the 

real-time clearing. The MMU made recommendations in the 2023 annual state of the market 

report that SPP review the use of soon-to-start resources in meeting the requirement for 

uncertainty, as soon-to-start are not used to meet any other SPP product requirements. SPP has 

opted to hold off on reviewing soon-to-start until the effects from the uncertainty requirement 

changes have had ample time to be observed.

  SCARCITY PATTERNS 

The distribution of scarcity intervals within any given hour vary by product. In 2025, about 15% 

of real-time regulation-up scarcities and about 41% of regulation-down scarcities occurred in 

the first interval of the hour. Regulation scarcity events typically happen at the beginning of the 

hour and have since the inception of the marketplace. Contingency reserve scarcities were over 

twice as likely to occur during the first half of hour than the second half, peaking at 16% in the 

fourth interval. Ramp capability-up scarcities tended to rise throughout the hour. Figure 3-30 

below illustrates the average count of scarcity events in the real-time market by the 12 intervals 

of each hour.  

Figure 3-30  Scarcity by product by interval, real-time 

One potential reason for regulation scarcities peaking at the beginning of the hour is that SPP 

does not pre-position regulation-providing resources to be within their regulating maximum 

and minimum limits prior to the period that the resource is cleared for regulation. Consider a 

resource that is currently dispatched to its minimum of 100 MW. If this resource clears 20 MW of 

regulation-down reserves in the next hour, it will need to move up to 120 MW to account for the 

cleared regulation-down and the 100 MWs of minimum output. If the resource’s ramp rate does 

not allow it to ramp up 20 MW in one interval, the resource cannot provide regulation-down in 

the first interval. If this causes a scarcity event, the resource may have to buy back a day-ahead 

position at scarcity prices. However, if the resource moves there prior to the hour, it will deviate 

77 SPP MMU 2023 Annual State of the Market report, page 109.  

State of the Market 2025 

105 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

from its current dispatch instruction, which has financial penalties.78 Consequently, resources 

often follow dispatch until the first interval of the regulation commitment, contributing to 

shortages in the first interval of the regulating commitment. 

The duration of scarcity events also varies by product. Figure 3-31 shows that a scarcity pricing 

event in real-time was most likely to occur for only one five-minute interval. Other than ramp-

up, comparably few scarcity pricing events last more than two intervals. This pattern has been 

consistent in recent years; however, the proportion of events lasting two or more intervals 

increased year-over-year, primarily driven by the increased proportion of events lasting six or 

more intervals for regulation-up, ramp-up, and operating reserve.

Interval length of short-term price spikes, percentage 

Of all the regulation-up scarcity events, about 55% lasted for only one interval and about 18% 

lasted for two intervals, showing a small shift towards regulation-up scarcity events lasting three 

or more intervals as compared to 2024. For regulation-down scarcity events, about 65% lasted 

for only one interval, and about 22% lasted for two intervals. Operating reserve scarcity event 

lengths were roughly 43% lasting for one interval and 15% lasting two intervals. For ramp-up 

scarcity events, 35% lasted one interval, and 15% lasted for two intervals. While ramp-up scarcity 

events decrease quickly as event length increases, there were many more ramp-up scarcity 

events, and they lasted longer than other product scarcity events. This is mostly due to the 

product’s low scarcity demand curve prices. Each of regulation-up, regulation-down, and

3.4  REAL-TIME BALANCING 

While SPP attempts to commit an amount of generation and ancillary services in the day-ahead 

market that approximates real-time need for the corresponding operating day, adjustments 

must be made in real-time to keep the system in balance as deviations occur. This is why the 

real-time market is considered an incremental or balancing market. Deviations between the day-

ahead and real-time markets are often small but can have significant impacts on efficiency and 

reliability. For example, because SPP cannot decommit resources in real-time, operations may 

need to bring a resource or resources down to their economic minimum during real-time if their 

capacity committed in day-ahead is no longer needed. Among other impacts, this can lead to 

make-whole payments when resources are not able to recover their costs while operating at 

economic minimum. 

The MMU tracks metrics related to real-time deviations from day-ahead commitment and 

dispatch. Key indicators discussed in this section include capacity deviations and headroom 

requirements. The MMU also tracks the market’s demand for energy attributes, such as ramp 

and uncertainty.  

  REAL-TIME DEVIATIONS 

While there are many reasons deviations between the day-ahead and real-time markets, there 

are three systemic drivers of particular concern. First, virtual supply (net of virtual demand), is a 

financial only product limited to the day-ahead market and will generally not match actual real-

time supply. Because the reliability unit commitment processes must ensure sufficient physical 

generation is on-line to meet energy demand, virtual transactions are not included in the 

reliability unit commitment processes used in day-ahead, intra-day, or short-term. 

Second, there are generally measurable differences in real-time wind generation compared to 

wind cleared in the day-ahead market. The mismatch is partly because some market participants 

with wind generation assets, recognizing the uncertainty of the wind forecast in day-ahead, offer 

a percent of the forecasted capacity or, in some cases, choose not to participate in the day-

ahead market at all. This may cause other generation to clear in the day-ahead market that will 

not be needed in real-time when the wind replaces it. Finally, real-time net exports often exceed 

day-head net exports. This is partly due to the release of firm transmission occurring after the 

day-ahead market close, opening up non-firm transmission for imports closer to real-time. 

State of the Market 2025 

107 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Import and export transaction data are updated to include the latest information available for 

the reliability unit commitment processes.  

These differences are typically responsible for most of the excess (or shortfall) in price-following, 

physical generation cleared in the day-ahead market as compared to real-time and explain why 

some generators produce much less in real-time or why additional commitments occur after the 

day-ahead market has cleared (see Figure 3-5). Figure 3-32 compares the average difference 

between cleared capacity in day-ahead versus the actual dispatched capacity in real-time market 

alongside average prices.  

Figure 3-32  Average hourly capacity increase from day-ahead to real-time 

The chart indicates that in 2025 there was, on average, around 2,337 MWs of additional 

dispatchable generation cleared incremental to the day-ahead market, an increase of 7% from 

2023 and a decrease of 6% compared to 2024.  

One of the reasons for reliability commitments is the need for ramp capability. The increase or 

decrease of the resource’s output to achieve the next dispatch instruction is called “ramp.” The 

number of megawatts a resource can ramp in one minute is the resource’s “ramp rate.” 

The instantaneous load capacity constraint may commit additional resources to ensure there is 

adequate ramping capacity to meet the instantaneous peak demand for any given hour. The 

instantaneous load capacity constraint is defined as the greater of the forecasted instantaneous 

peak load, or an SPP defined default value that is evaluated quarterly. Unfortunately, the default 

or minimum requirement, which is used frequently, is not based on market information. As a 

result, the instantaneous load capacity constraint can contribute to reliability commitments that 

State of the Market 2025 

108 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

are not based on current market information. The percent of hours at various upper bound 
requirements is shown in Figure 3-33.79.. 

Figure 3-33  Instantaneous load capacity upper bound requirements 

The most frequent observations were from 400 MW to 499 MW at around 41% of hours. This is 

a decrease from the past two years which saw headroom requirements most frequently fall in 

the 500 MW to 599 MW and more observations at higher values. However, these values are 

closer to 2022, which saw most observations of the head-room requirement fall between 300 

MW and 499 MW. While a market-based product is more appropriate for market efficiency 

improvement, keeping this requirement low may help reduce unnecessary make-whole 

payments. A continued shift upward in the default requirement may diminish any possible 

benefit of lowering unnecessary make-whole payments.  

Resources committed to provide ramp capability can affect real-time prices, whether because of 

applying the instantaneous load capacity constraint in a reliability unit commitment process or a 

manual process. Without the appropriate scarcity pricing rules that reflect the market value of 

capacity shortages due to ramp capability, the cost of bringing the resource on-line may not be 

fully reflected in the real-time prices. The resource keeping the market from being scarce may 

not be paid to provide the needed capability. Additionally, manual commitments made during 

conservative operations, while possibly needed for capacity, similarly suppress the price signals 

when they are needed most.  

79 The default requirements are hourly values as low as 200 MW 

State of the Market 2025 

109 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Reliability commitments, along with wind exceeding the day-ahead forecast, can dampen real-

time price signals, as is evidenced by 54% of real-time make-whole payments made for 
reliability unit commitments, as shown in (Figure 4-24).80  

In real-time, resources increase and decrease output to meet changes in both load and non-

dispatchable generation. These changes can be measured as changes in net load. Net load is net 

of non-dispatchable generation, net imports/exports, and parallel flows from other markets. 

Figure 3-34 shows the frequency and extent of net load changes from one real-time interval to 

the next.  

Figure 3-34  Frequency of net load change, real-time 

Of the net load changes between real-time intervals, 95% were between a decrease of about 

381 MW and an increase of about 358 MW. This is relatively in line with 2024. There was a slow 

but steady increase in net load swings for the first nine years of the SPP Integrated Marketplace. 

Net load swings must be balanced by resources with a flexible dispatch range, or ramp 

capability. Figure 3-35 below shows the volatility in net load change since 2017. 

80 This is the sum of the intra-day RUC, short-term RUC, and day-ahead RUC in Figure 4-29.

110 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3-35   Volatility of interval-to-interval net load change 

After steadily increasing for nearly a decade, net load volatility went down for the first time by 

three percent in 2024. However, it increased one percent again in 2025. This is a substantial 

slowdown considering the 27% increase from 2017 to 2023.  

As variable energy resources serve more load, volatility is expected to increase. Variable energy 

resources, primarily wind and solar, produced about 37% of total generation in 2025. Of all 

intervals where these resources were on-line and available for economic dispatch, forecasted 

variable energy resources were expected to follow dispatch in about eight % of resource-

intervals, compared to about 80% for non-forecasted energy resources. When these forecasted 

resources are not following dispatch, it is possible for them to reduce the ramp need when they 

move in the same direction as demand, as long as they don’t overshoot demand by too much. 

However, they can also increase the amount of ramp need when they move in the opposite 

direction as demand or overshoot demand by a large quantity. When they increase the system 

ramp need, flexible resources are needed to account for the additional ramp they cause.  

Figure 3-36 below shows how much additional ramp was needed in real-time intervals due to 

undispatched, forecasted variable energy resources with the ramp that would have been needed 

for demand alone. 

State of the Market 2025 

111 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

Figure 3-36   Additional ramp need due to undispatched forecasted resources, 95th 

percentile, real-time 

The 95th percentile increase in net ramp need averaged 511 MW for 2025 compared to 558 MW 

in 2024. The highest month was April, which averaged just over 728 MW. In contrast, 

undispatched forecasted resources decreased net ramp needs by only about 117 to 161 MWs, 

averaging 141 MW across the year. Because the precise timing of the fluctuations of forecasted 

resources is unknown, rampable capacity must be committed and available throughout the day. 

This is even more challenging when the resources’ real-time output does not follow the forecast. 

While forecasted variable energy resources provide inexpensive energy, they also create a 

greater need for more system ramp. 

  REAL-TIME DISPATCHING TO MINIMUM  

Changes in dispatch between day-ahead and real-time is one measure of how efficiently the 

region used its available capacity. One inefficiency is the inability for the market to decommit 

resources in real-time. When a resource is dispatched above their economic minimum in day-

ahead but is no longer needed or is no longer competitive in real-time, that resource will often 

be brought down to the economic minimum to make room for more economic generation. The 

capacity left online at minimum often represents a cost to the region as it crowds out more 

economic generation.  

Figure 3-37 shows, by month, the change between day-ahead and real-time dispatch MW for 

resources that received a day-ahead award above their economic minimum but were dispatched 

down to economic minimum in real-time. We only included coal and natural gas resources as 

other resource types represented less than 1% of the data. Also included in Figure 3-37 is the 

average amount of capacity operating at economic minimum after being dispatched down in 

State of the Market 2025 

112 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

real-time. This represents the amount of capacity that likely crowded out more economic 

generation.  

Figure 3-37  Capacity dispatched down to economic minimum in real-time 

On average, the region typically dispatched 8 resources down to their economic minimum in 

real-time, representing a change in dispatch MW of 500 MW between day-ahead and real-time. 

Roughly 5% resulted from resources increasing their economic minimum in real-time. These 

figures were highest in June, July, and November and lowest in August, September, and 

October. Compared to 2023 and 2024, the change in day-ahead to real-time dispatch awards for 

those resources brought down to economic minimum was 11% greater and 4% greater in 2025, 

respectively. The total amount of capacity operating at economic minimum as a result of being 

dispatched down in real-time also increased between 2023 and 2025, from 650 MW on average 

to 725 MW on average, respectively. This increase potentially represents a greater amount of 

economic capacity crowded out in real-time. As this value grows, it is likely that the region is 

moving further away from the optimal total production cost in the real-time market. 

The MMU is concerned by trends that indicate worsening efficiency over time and encourages 

SPP and stakeholders to investigate potential causes. There could be several explanations for the 

increase in the metrics above. For example, resources may have increased the value of their 

economic minimum over time, leading to a greater total capacity operating at economic 

minimum after being dispatched down in real-time. Alternatively, greater uncertainty from 

variable energy resource production may have led to a greater change in these resources’ 

awards in day-ahead and real-time. The MMU would encourage SPP and its stakeholders to 

study potential causes of the increase in instances where capacity is brought down to economic 

minimum in real-time and the increase in total capacity operating at economic minimum in real-

time as a result of these changes in dispatch.  We would also encourage SPP and stakeholders 

State of the Market 2025 

113 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Unit commitment and dispatch processes 

to examine potential operational or policy changes that minimize how much capacity the region 

has to dispatch down to economic minimum in real-time.

4  MARKET PRICES AND COSTS 

This chapter covers market prices and costs in the SPP market, along with related metrics on 

negative prices, make-whole payments, and long-run price signals for investment. Highlights of 

this chapter include:  

•  Average gas price for 2025 at the Panhandle Eastern hub was $2.97/MMBtu, up 50% 

from $1.98/MMBtu in 2024. 

•  The average hourly day-ahead price increased by 13% from $30.43/MWh in 2024 to 

$34.40/MWh in 2025 and the real-time price increased by 16% from $28.34/MWh in 

2024 to $32.78/MWh in 2025.  

• 

In 2025, 7.6% of all asset owner intervals in the day-ahead market had prices below zero; 

this is down from 9.9% of all intervals in 2024. In the real-time market, the frequency of 

negative price intervals was 12.7% of all intervals in 2025, down from 15.2% in 2024. 

•  For 2025, day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment make-whole payments 

combined totaled $257 million, the same as was reported in 2024. Reliability unit 

commitment make-whole payments constituted 54% of the total make-whole payments 

in 2025. Day-ahead make-whole payments were over $119 million in both 2025 and 

2024, up from $79 million in 2023, an increase of 51%. Reliability unit commitment 

make-whole payments in 2025 totaled $138 million, up just over $200,000 from the value 

in 2024, representing a 0.16% increase. 

• 

In 2025, 68 resources received over $1 million in total make-whole payments, the same 

amount that occurred in 2024. The highest payment to a single resource in 2025 was just 

over $11 million, though it was the only resource to receive over $10 million.  

•  On a market participant level, ten market participants each received annual make-whole 

payments greater than $10 million. These ten market participants accounted for 80% of 

the total make-whole payments paid out in 2025. 

•  Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2025 was $290 million, up 78% from $163 

million in 2024. The real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality 

uplift was $86 million in 2025, a decrease of 24% from $113 million in 2024. The real-

time congestion and revenue inadequacy components of revenue neutrality uplift 

totaled $360 million in 2025, an increase of 40% from $257 million in 2024. 

•  Historically, market revenues alone have been insufficient to support the cost of new 

entry for all major generation technologies since the start of the Integrated Marketplace. 

State of the Market 2025 

115 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Exceptions to this have been in 2021, due to increased revenue due to the February 

winter storm and in 2022 for certain wind resources. In 2025, this broader trend largely 

continued. However, combined-cycle and combustion turbine generation in the SPS 

region were able to recover their cost of new entry due to a glut of wet gas production 

at the Waha trading hub that resulted in natural gas prices averaging $0.64/MMBtu for 

the year. 

4.1  MARKET PRICES AND COSTS 

Proper price formation is crucial for efficient markets in both the short and long term. Prices 

should signal underlying market conditions, including relative scarcity, changes in fuel prices, 

and congestion. Distortions arising from behaviors such as resources self-scheduling and 

operator intervention, or policies such as production subsidies, can lead to inefficient outcomes. 

This can include too much or too little producer surplus, deviations between the day-ahead and 

real-time markets, and improper signals for long-term investments.  

This section reviews market prices and costs by focusing on the energy market and fuel 

prices, price volatility, negative prices, operating reserve prices, and ancillary service prices. 

The MMU pays particular attention to the level of price convergence between the day-ahead 

and real-time market as a potential indicator of systemic drivers of inefficiency.  

ENERGY MARKET PRICES AND FUEL PRICES

Historically, on-peak prices tend to average about $10-$15/MWh higher than off-peak prices,

after 2022 (not shown) had a spread of nearly $22/MWh. The differences between on-peak and 

off-peak prices can be mostly attributed to lower loads and a higher percentage of lower cost 

wind generation in off-peak hours. 

Changes in gas prices have historically had the highest impact on electricity prices compared to 

other fuels. This is because the short-run marginal costs of coal-fired generation historically 

have been cheaper than natural gas-fired generation; hence, gas units are most frequently 

marginal. The energy pricing continued to trend with the gas prices throughout the year. Figure

SPP has two pricing hubs: the SPP North hub and the SPP South hub. The SPP North hub 

represents a portion of pricing nodes in the northern part of the SPP footprint, generally in 

Nebraska. The SPP South hub represents a portion of pricing nodes in the south-central portion 

of the footprint, generally in central Oklahoma. Figure 4–6 shows the average day-ahead prices 

and real-time prices at the two SPP market hubs. 

Figure 4–6  Hub prices, day-ahead and real-time 

The general pattern of higher prices in the south and lower in the north is primarily due to fuel 

mix and congestion. Coal, nuclear, and wind are the dominant fuels in the north and west. The 

south and east have a much larger share of gas generation, hence, more exposure to changes in 

fuel prices. However, six months in the day-ahead market and eight months in the real-time 

market during 2025 saw South hub prices below North hub prices. This can primarily be 

attributed to congestion around North hub settlement locations. The spread between the North 

and South hubs, in both the day-ahead and real-time markets, was around the $9/MWh range. 

In 2025, the spread decreased to around $1/MWh.

Average on-peak day-ahead prices increased at the SPP North hub but decreased at the SPP 

South hub. The increase at the North hub can mostly be attributed to congestion patterns 

during the year. Price increases in each region can primarily be attributed to higher natural gas 

prices relative to prior years. ERCOT’s higher prices in 2023 were the result of additional reserve 

procurement that abated in 2024 and 2025.  

  ENERGY PRICE VOLATILITY  

Price volatility87 in the SPP market is shown in Figure 4–8 below. As expected, day-ahead prices 

are much less volatile than those in real-time. The day-ahead market does not experience the 

actual (unexpected) congestion and changes in load or generation found in the real-time 

market. Real-time volatility tends to peak in the spring and fall, roughly corresponding with 

times of higher wind and lower load but can also peak during the summer months because of 

congestion and peak load conditions. 

87 Volatility is calculated as the standard deviation for prices received by load-serving entities in the SPP 
market. The standard deviation is calculated using hourly price in the day-ahead market and interval (five 
minute) price in the real-time market.  

State of the Market 2025

Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Figure 4–8  System price volatility 

Market prices and costs 

In 2025, volatility in the day-ahead market decreased by 21% from 2024, while volatility in the 

real-time market increased by 31% from 2024. The most volatile months were April and October. 

  DAY-AHEAD AND REAL-TIME PRICE CONVERGENCE 

Price convergence between day-ahead and real-time prices is important, because the more day-

ahead prices reflect real-time prices, the better unit commitment and positioning of resources 

occurs for real-time operations. 

While average prices in the day-ahead and real-time markets have been close over the past 

several years, average prices can mask real-time volatility and underlying price differences. The 

averaging of price spikes, and in particular, high prices during periods of scarcity, drove real-

time average prices up, closer to day-ahead prices. These short-term, transient price spikes can 
be attributed to limitations in ramping capability.88 

In this section, underlying differences in prices after controlling for scarcity events are 

highlighted. This analysis shows that a significant volume of generation, particularly from wind 

resources, that is not cleared in the day-ahead market drives down real-time prices. 

Many factors cause prices to diverge between the day-ahead and real-time markets. Some of 

these factors may include, but are not limited to: 

•  Day-ahead offers typically include premiums to account for uncertainty in real-time fuel 

prices or forced outage risk. 

•  Differences in real-time market results, caused by load and wind forecast errors.  

88 For further information on ramping issues, see Section 3.3.2. 

State of the Market 2025 

123 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

•  Participants not bidding in all load or offering all generation in the day-ahead market.  

•  Modeling differences, including transmission outages between the two markets.  

•  Generation outages or derates that were different in real-time than was anticipated in 

the day-ahead.  

•  Unanticipated impacts from other RTOs which affect SPP’s real-time market. 

•  Changes in imports and exports from other systems in the real-time markets. 

•  Unanticipated weather changes. 

Price divergence89 between the day-ahead and real-time markets at the system level is shown in 

Figure 4–9 below. Market participants may be willing to pay a premium for price certainty in the 

day-ahead market. This can result in higher prices in the day-ahead market, which is appropriate 

in a well-functioning market. A large divergence between the day-ahead and real-time prices 

may indicate that actual conditions in the market do not match the expected conditions. An 

extended period of a large variance between day-ahead and real-time prices may indicate a 

structural or design deficiency in the market. 

Figure 4–9  Price divergence 

Average absolute divergence for 2025 was $15.43/MWh, up from $13.82/MWh in 2024, and 

from $12.45/MWh in 2023. Historically, absolute divergence has averaged around $10 per 

month up through 2020. However, the 2021 winter storm caused the absolute divergence to 

spike to $45/MWh in 2021, then absolute divergence dropped to $20/MWh in 2022, before 

89 Price divergence is calculated as the difference between day-ahead and real-time prices, using system 
prices for each five-minute (real-time) or hour (day-ahead) interval. The absolute divergence is calculated 
by taking the absolute value of the divergence for each interval. 

State of the Market 2025 

124 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

dropping to $12/MWh in 2023, then climbing gradually to 2025. The under-clearing of 

renewable resources and short-term ramping limitations contribute to increased price 

divergence. It was hoped that implementation of the ramping product in 2022 would also have a 

positive effect on this divergence; however, the impact has been negligible to date. The MMU 

recommends that additional work be done to improve price divergence related to under-
clearing of renewable generation in the day-ahead market.90

follow the same monthly trend as gas prices.

reflecting an overall hourly average decrease in additional energy of over 330 MW. Self-

While SPP is a net exporter in both the day-ahead and real-time markets on average, it sees an

Figure 4–13 shows the difference between the day-ahead offered wind generation and the day-

forecast, which is created one day prior to the operating day. 

Figure 4–13  Day-ahead wind offered versus cleared 

In 2025, 86% of the wind offered in the day-ahead cleared, returning to the percentage level 

seen in 2023 after falling to 83% in 2024. In 2025, 89% of forecasted wind was offered into the 

day-ahead, which is well below the 92% in 2023, but an improvement from 88% in 2024. 

However, even though wind resources are generally offering in close to full forecasted capacity 

in the day-ahead, a portion of this is at offer levels that exceed prevailing prices and thus does 

not clear the market. Typically, wind will clear all the megawatts that are physically offered into 

the day-ahead market if the economic offers are consistent with real-time offers. The MMU 

observed that almost all wind megawatts offered into the day-ahead market and not cleared 

had higher economic offers in the day-ahead market than real-time. 

State of the Market 2025 

127 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Systematic under-clearing of wind resources in the day-ahead market can contribute to 

distorted price signals, suppressed real-time prices, and insufficient revenue adequacy. Variable 

energy resources are generally able to produce close to a forecasted amount, in aggregate. 

Therefore, the MMU continues to recommend that SPP and its stakeholders address this issue 

through market incentives and rule changes that focus on market inefficiencies associated with 

under-clearing of variable energy resources in the day-ahead market based on forecasted 

supply. These rule changes could focus on changing incentives for wind resources or 

alternatively reducing barriers for virtual transactions. 

  NEGATIVE PRICES 

While negative prices are a legitimate market outcome, they can make it more difficult for 

generators to earn net revenue in the long run. Negative price intervals can be caused by many 

different factors including high amounts of wind generation, self-commitment of resources in 

the day-ahead market, negative natural gas prices, and external impacts. Negative price intervals 

for the day-ahead market are shown in Figure 4–14. 

Figure 4–14  Negative price intervals, day-ahead, monthly 

In 2025, 7.6% of all asset owner intervals92 in the day-ahead market had prices below zero, as 

shown in Figure 4–14. This is down from 9.9% of all intervals in 2024. April again had the highest 

percentage of negative intervals in the day-ahead market at 15.9%, despite coming in 

considerably lower than April 2024 at 25.6%. 

92 Asset owner intervals are calculated as the number of asset owners serving load that are active in an 
interval. For example, if there 60 asset owners active in one five-minute interval, throughout an entire 30-
day month, the total asset owner intervals would be 518,400 for the month (60 asset owners * 288 intervals 
per day * 30 days).

Historically, negative price intervals in the real-time market occur around two times more 

frequently than in the day-ahead market. Negative price intervals for the real-time market are 

shown in Figure 4–15. 

Figure 4–15  Negative price intervals, real-time, monthly 

The frequency of negative price intervals in the real-time market was 12.7% of all intervals in 

2025, down from 15.2% in 2024, a 16.5% decrease overall.  

Negative prices in the real-time market were mostly between −$0.01/MWh and −$25/MWh in 

2025, with 79% of real-time intervals with negative prices having values between −$0.01/MWh 

and −$25/MWh, 18% of intervals with negative prices having values between −$25/MWh and -

$50/MWh, and 2.4% of intervals with values below -$50/MWh. This contrasts with day-ahead 

negatively priced intervals, which show more stability with 94.5% of all intervals with negative 

pricing between −$0.01/MWh and −$25/MWh and only 5% of all intervals with negative pricing 

between −$25/MWh and -$50/MWh. 

Additionally, occurrences of negative prices are most prevalent in the overnight, low-load hours 

as shown in Figure 4–16 for the day-ahead market.

Figure 4–18  Negative price intervals, real-time, by asset owner 

In 2025, 25 of 71 asset owners (35.2%) with load experienced negative prices in more than 15% 

of total intervals. This is a decrease from 2024, where 44% of asset owners experienced negative 

prices more than 15% of total intervals. The asset owner with negative prices in the highest 

percentage of intervals in 2025, had 29.6% of intervals with negative prices – up from 2024 when 

the highest percentage was 34% of all intervals. 

The MMU remains vigilant regarding the frequency of negative price intervals. Negative prices 

may not be a problem in and of themselves, however, they do indicate an increase in surplus 

energy on the system. This is exacerbated by the practice of self and manually committing 

resources for capacity. In the SPP market, where there is an abundance of total system capacity 

and significant levels of renewable resources, negative prices can occur. In this instance, 

renewable resources will need to be backed down for conventional resources to meet their 

minimums. Moreover, unit commitment differences, due to under-clearing of wind resources in 

the day-ahead market and then producing more in the real-time market, can create differences 

in the frequency of negative price intervals between the day-ahead and real-time markets. This 

disparity between the markets negatively impacts the efficient commitment of resources.  

Negative price intervals in the day-ahead highlight the need for changes in market rules to 

address self-committing of resources in the day-ahead market and the differences in supply 

between day-ahead and real time. These issues are discussed further in Chapter 7. 

State of the Market 2025 

131 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

  OPERATING RESERVE MARKET PRICES  

Operating reserve is made up of four products: (1) regulation-up, (2) regulation-down, (3) 

spinning reserve, and (4) supplemental reserve. The regulation products are used to ensure the 

amount of generation matches load on a subinterval basis. Generators can respond to 

regulation instructions within cycles. Spinning and supplemental products are reserved for 

contingency situations and can respond to instructions within 10 minutes. 

In addition, regulating units are compensated for mileage costs incurred when moving from one 

set point instruction to another. Each month, the market calculates a mileage factor for both 

products (regulation-up mileage and regulation-down mileage), which represents the 

percentage a unit is expected to be deployed compared to what it cleared. If a unit is deployed 

more than the expected percentage, the unit will be entitled to reimbursement for the excess at 

the regulation mileage marginal price. If the unit is deployed less, it must buy back its position 

at the real-time mileage clearing price. 

Also of note is the sharp drop in regulation mileage prices starting in May 2024. The MMU had 

identified that increased regulation mileage prices are related, in part, to issues related to an 
outstanding MMU recommendation related to regulation mileage.93 The goal of Revision 

Request 504 (RR 504) was to help reduce the occurrence of high mileage offers setting price. RR 

504 did two things: 

1.  Mileage factor was calculated using the instructed mileage, which overstates the amount 

of regulation mileage that is seen on the system. RR 504 proposed to change the 

calculation for the mileage factor to be the actual regulation mileage divided by the 

cleared regulation, instead of the instructed mileage divided by the cleared regulation. 

2.  The mileage market clearing price (MCP) was set as the highest mileage price offered for 

resources that have cleared regulation. RR 504 proposed to change the way the mileage 

market clearing price is set – from the highest offered mileage price of cleared resource 

to the resource projected to provide the last mile, based on the mileage factor. 

The change was filed with FERC94 and was approved on June 14, 2023, with implementation 

scheduled for June 4, 2024. While this change was unintentionally implemented a month earlier 

than the FERC approved date, the intended effect of reducing high mileage offers setting price 

appears to have been successful, with regulation mileage prices dropping into the $2/MW to 

$4/MW range since implementation. 

93 SPP MMU 2021 Annual State of the Market report, page 273. 
94 FERC Docket No. ER23-1430 

State of the Market 2025 

132 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Generally, regulation-up and regulation-down usually have the highest market clearing prices. 

Supplemental reserves always have the lowest average prices of the operating reserve products, 

with prices typically averaging less than two dollars on an annual basis.

4.2  UPLIFT 

Market prices and costs 

The Integrated Marketplace provides make-whole payments to generators to ensure that the 

market provides sufficient revenue to cover the cleared offers providing energy and operating 

reserves for the period in which the resource was committed. To preserve the incentive for a 

resource to meet its market commitment and dispatch instruction, market payments should 

cover the sum of the incremental energy cost, start-up cost, no-load cost, transition cost, and 

cost of operating reserve products. The make-whole payment provides additional market 

payments in cases where revenue is below a resource’s offer to make the resource whole to its 

offer. Sometimes make-whole payments result from an economically efficient choice when it is 

less costly for the market to issue a make-whole payment to a resource to keep it online than to 

shut it down and incur start-up and no-load costs from another resource over a short period 

later. However, they can also result from inefficiencies including physical and economic 

constraints, forecast inaccuracies, reliability needs, or any other reason that would lead the 

region to commit or continue to run a resource when its total operational costs are not covered 

by current market prices.   

The MMU tracks make-whole payment quantities over time from multiple angles including by 

resource type, fuel type, and commitment process. We also monitor how make-whole 

payments are allocated across payment type and by resource. These metrics can indicate 

broader market trends or point to inefficiencies, modeling issues, or other systemic problems 

that should be addressed. 

  MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENTS 

For the resources that are not combined-cycle, settlements separately evaluate: (1) day-ahead 

market commitments based on day-ahead market prices and cleared offers; and (2) reliability 

unit commitments based on real-time market prices and cleared operating reserve offers. 

Combined-cycle resources that are registered as multi-configuration resources are unique in 

that they can be cleared in both the day-ahead and real-time markets at the same time. As a 

result, settlements must evaluate the revenues and cost of both real-time and day-ahead 

commitments when calculating real-time make-whole payments for combined-cycles.  

Figure 4–23 shows monthly and annual day-ahead make-whole payment totals by technology 

type. Figure 4–24 shows the same make-whole payment information for reliability unit 

commitment.  

State of the Market 2025 

135 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

Figure 4–23  Make-whole payments by technology type, day-ahead 

Figure 4–24  Make-whole payments by technology type, reliability unit commitment 

For 2025, day-ahead market and reliability unit commitment make-whole payments combined 

totaled $257 million. Much like 2024, 2025’s reliability unit commitment make-whole payments 

constituted 54% of the total make-whole payments. In total, these values remained stable and 

did not deviate much from the values seen in 2024, with the combined change being a decrease 

of less than $200,000 from 2024 to 2025. 

Day-ahead make-whole payments were nearly $119 million in 2025, only slightly lower than the 

amount in 2024 – a decrease of 0.35%. Conversely, reliability unit commitment make-whole 

payments in 2025 were almost $138 million, up just over $200,000 from the value in 2024, 

representing a 0.16% increase. 

2025 saw a notable increase in make-whole payments to the day-ahead market, particularly for 

simple-cycle gas resources for the month of January. This is due to colder temperatures driving 

State of the Market 2025 

136 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

up the price of gas during extreme winter events seen around the footprint during this time 

frame, causing increased make-whole payments to supplement the fuel costs. This phenomenon 

was also observed in 2024 and will likely continue to be seen in any year where major winter 

storm events affect the footprint. 

Some causes of make-whole payments included but are not limited to: local reliability 

commitments, congestion not accurately modeled in the day-ahead market, under-cleared 

generation (primarily wind) in the day-ahead market, inflexibility of resources to move in 

economic ranges or go offline between on-peak and off-peak hours, and excessive transmission 

congestion not being solved by physical resources. 

Total make-whole payments per megawatt generated averaged $0.85/MWh for 2025, down 

from $0.88/MWh for 2024, but still higher than the $0.83/MWh seen in 2022. Self-committed 

resources are not eligible for make-whole payment reimbursements. Primarily, resources 

committed under reliability or market status are eligible for cost reimbursement.

eligible for cost reimbursement, or in other words, those megawatts generated under either 

market or reliability status. These include manual commitments made by operators.

Day-ahead and real-time values for 2025 were smaller than those seen in 2024. This decrease

Day-ahead values for 2025 only had a minor decrease compared to 2024, though, it remains

Operators often commit resources when the available ramp capacity needs in future intervals 

are perceived to be short. These actions often reduce the occurrence of scarcity events. 

However, this has the effect of potentially suppressing the price signal that would indicate a 

problem, as capacity is brought on to meet the perceived ramp shortage. Manual commitments 

can and sometimes do lead to make-whole payments. Commitments that come from the 

security-constrained economic unit commitment are preferred. Additionally, capacity committed 

manually can often lead to price suppression. Another way to view the real-time make-whole 

State of the Market 2025 

137 

 
  
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

payments is that on average, $24.40/MWh was paid to eligible generators to avoid reliability 

problems that could not be addressed directly by the real-time market. 

In addition, most scarcity events last less than two intervals while most resources have start-

times that are longer and minimum-run times that are much longer than this period. Meaning, 

that even if these resources can capture one to two intervals with high prices, they may have to 

run an hour or two longer with less economic price levels, leading to the need for cost 

reimbursement.  

The MMU believed that the ramp capability product95 (see Section 3.3.2), which went into 
production in March 2022, the uncertainty product96 (see Section 3.3.3) implemented in July 

2023, and fast-start resource pricing logic should help provide the appropriate pricing and 
compensation mechanisms for meeting ramp capacity needs in the market97. However, both the 

ramp capability product and the uncertainty product need to be enhanced and improved to 

provide maximum benefit to the market. This includes reducing make-whole payments and 

bringing transparency to the market. Enhancements to these products will also improve price 

formation and better compensate resources that provide the much-needed ramping flexibility, 

as well as reduce the need for manual commitments for capacity.

In recent years, there has been a large increase in make-whole payments occurring during 

periods that resources are manually committed for capacity. In fact, nearly 31% of the real-time

from 28% in 2024 and 23% in 2023. After only a slight increase in make-whole payments for 

transmission in the real-time market from 2023 to 2024, this past year saw a large jump from 

19% in 2024 to 27.4% at the end of 2025. 

Make-whole payments associated with voltage support commitments, which have grown 

dramatically over the past few years, do not follow the same uplift process outlined in Section 

4.3.1. Instead, the cost of these make-whole payments is distributed to the settlement areas that 

benefited from the commitment by way of a load ratio share. Figure 4–27 illustrates the level of 

make-whole payments associated with voltage support commitments.

saw a significant increase in eight months of year when compared to make-whole payments for 

voltage support in 2024. While most months increased last year, October had the largest 

increase, which was mainly driven by a large sum of make-whole payments totaling nearly $3 

million to a single coal plant.

market participants received in 2024, but very similar in both number of market participants and

Market prices and costs 

portion of make-whole payments that went to those that went to these top receivers of make-

whole payments. 

  MAKE-WHOLE PAYMENT ALLOCATION 

The allocation of both day-ahead and real-time make-whole payments has important 

consequences for the market. In principle, for market efficiency purposes, uplift cost allocation 

should be directed to those members that contributed to the need for the make-whole 

payments (i.e., cost causation). 

For the day-ahead market, make-whole payment costs are distributed to both physical and 

virtual withdrawals on a per-MWh rate. The per-MWh rate is derived by dividing the sum of all 

day-ahead make-whole payments for an operating day by the sum of all cleared day-ahead 

market load megawatts, export megawatts, and virtual bids for the operating day. The average 

per-MWh rate for withdrawing locations in the day-ahead market was $0.31/MWh for 2025; 

down slightly from the 2024 rate of $0.34/MWh, but still up from the 2023 rate of $0.26/MWh.  

For the real-time market, make-whole payment costs are distributed through a per-MWh rate 

that is assigned to all megawatt-hours of deviation in the real-time market. The 2025 per-MWh 

rate was $1.62/MWh, which is down from both the $1.76/MWh seen in 2024 and the 2023 rate 

of $2.35/MWh. There are eight categories of deviation, and each category receives an equal 

amount per megawatt – which can vary by operating day when the cost of make-whole 

payments is applied.

Even though each category of deviation is applied the same rate for deviation, approximately 

87% (settlement location deviation in the table above) of the real-time make-whole payment 

costs were paid by entities withdrawing (physical or virtual) more megawatts in the real-time 

market than the day-ahead market.  

Transactions susceptible to this charge are virtual offer megawatts; real-time load megawatts 

different from the day-ahead megawatts cleared; import, export, and through megawatts in 

real-time different from the megawatts cleared in the day-ahead market; and units pulling 

substation power different from any megawatts produced by the unit. However, virtual offers 

are the most susceptible as 100% of their megawatts are considered incremental. Due to this, 

virtual offers alone paid 58% of all real-time make-whole payments in 2025, up from 53% in 

2024. Historically, virtual transactions have paid around 50% of real-time make-whole payments 

annually. 

Cost causation has been an area of concern in the SPP working groups. Participants raised 

concerns that the market is not properly allocating the market cost back to those responsible for 

causing those costs. With virtual offers bearing such a heavy burden on these costs, it reduces 

the incentives for behavior changes among those that are causing the cost, and it adds a 

premium to virtual transactions. This should be considered as part of the evaluation of under-

State of the Market 2025 

142 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

clearing of wind in the day-ahead as these incentives likely contribute to the lack of price 

convergence between day-ahead and real-time. 

  DISTRIBUTION OF MARGINAL LOSSES (OVER -COLLECTED 

LOSS REVENUE) 

Both the congestion and loss components of prices create excess revenues for SPP that must be 

distributed to market participants in an economically efficient manner. In the case of marginal 

loss revenues, this requires that the distribution does not alter market incentives.  

The current design consolidates the distributions of day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss 
rebates into one distribution.98 Both day-ahead and real-time over-collected loss rebates are 

distributed on only real-time withdrawing megawatts. This includes loads, substation power, 
exports, wheel-throughs, pseudo-ties, and bilateral settlement schedules (BSS).99  

Over-collected losses for the past three years are shown in Figure 4–31.  

Figure 4–31  Over-collected losses, real-time 

A total of nearly $167 million was paid out in over-collected losses rebates during 2025, with 

almost $157 million (94%) going to load. This is up from the $140 million in over-collected 

losses rebates paid out in 2024.  

98 Prior years over-collected loss designs are described in the 2018 Annual State of the Market report under 
Section 4.2.3.  
99 The only exception is that both day-ahead and real-time BSS are entitled to the rebate, if the underlying 
megawatts associated with the BSS are not less than the megawatts of the BSS.

  REVENUE NEUTRALITY UPLIFT 

Revenue neutrality uplift (RNU), shown in Figure 4–32, ensures settlement payments and 

receipts for each hourly settlement interval equal zero. Positive revenue neutrality uplift 

indicates that SPP receives insufficient revenue and collects from market participants. Inversely, a 

negative revenue-neutrality uplift would indicate that SPP received excess revenue that must be 

credited back to market participants. 

On October 16, 2024, Revision Request 596100 was implemented, which removed the real-time 

inadvertent energy amount and real-time congestion amount and included them with the real-

time revenue inadequacy amount. Prior to this revision request, the inadvertent energy amount 

and the congestion amount were removed from the overall real-time revenue inadequacy 

amount. The reasoning behind this revision request is that it will remove redundant settlement 

calculations from revenue neutrality uplift that are not needed in the financial settlement 

process.  

Figure 4–32  Revenue neutrality uplift 

Total monthly revenue neutrality uplift for 2025 was $290 million, up 78% from $163 million in 

2024. The main components of revenue neutrality uplift are real-time congestion which is folded 

into revenue inadequacy, and real-time joint operating agreement (also known as market-to-

market). The joint operating agreement component generally acts to lessen revenue neutrality 

uplift, while the revenue inadequacy components generally act to increase revenue neutrality 

uplift. Real-time joint operating agreement portion of revenue neutrality uplift was $86 million in 

2025, a decrease of 24% from $113 million in 2024. The revenue inadequacy components of 

100 https://www.spp.org/Documents/70440/RR596.zip

COSTS 

Figure 4–33 plots the average all-in price of energy and the cost of natural gas, measured at the 

Panhandle Eastern (PEPL) hub. The average all-in price includes the costs of energy (using the 

real-time MEC), day-ahead and real-time reliability unit commitment make-whole payments, 
operating reserves,101 reserve sharing group costs, and payments to demand response 

resources. 

Figure 4–33  All-in price of electricity and natural gas cost 

The figure shows that the majority of costs are from day-ahead and real-time energy market 
payments as opposed to reserve payments or make-whole payments.102 It also shows that the 

market cost of operating reserves, make-whole payments, and demand response constituted 

approximately 3% of the $33.85/MWh all-in price for 2025, while energy accounted for 97%. 

Since the start of the Integrated Marketplace, historically energy has made up between 95% and 

97% of the all-in price – apart from the unusually high day-ahead make-whole payments during 

the February 2021 winter storm. The 2025 all-in price was 14% higher than the 2024 average, 

which can be primarily attributed to an increase in natural gas prices in 2025.

Production cost “is defined as the settlement cost for the market … for all resources.”103 

Production cost, in this case, is the sum of four components: 

•  energy: cleared megawatts multiplied by locational marginal prices; 

•  ancillary service: cleared operating reserves multiplied by market clearing prices; 

• 

start-up: “… the out of pocket cost that a Market Participant incurs in starting up a 
generating unit from an off-line state …;”104 and 

•  no-load: “… the hourly fee for operating a synchronized Resource at zero … output.”105

Figure 4–34  Production cost, daily average, day-ahead 

The day-ahead daily average production cost increased 24% from 2024 to 2025. The increase in 

production cost was almost fully attributed to the energy component, which is the most 

sensitive to changes in gas prices. The energy component is sensitive to numerous inputs, which 

include fuel cost, amount of renewable energy, operating reserve scarcity, and load levels. In 

2025, daily day-ahead production costs ranged between $1.4 million on April 12 and $88.1 

million on February 20. The large top-end day-ahead production cost values are driven by 

operating days with total day-ahead production costs at $77.1 million (February 19), $88.1 

million (February 20), and $64.6 million (February 21). The next highest value was $68.3 million 

(January 20).

4.4  LONG-RUN PRICE SIGNALS FOR INVESTMENT 

In the long term, market prices provide signals for investment in new transmission and 

generation, as well as ongoing maintenance of existing generation and transmission assets to 

meet load. As SPP market prices have continued to remain relatively low since the beginning of 

the market, the MMU remains steadfast in its position that SPP market prices alone do not 

support new entry of generation investment. While the SPP day-ahead and real-time markets 

have historically offered low incentives for new generation, some reasons for new generation 

investments include SPP’s increasing planning reserve margins, expansion of corporate 

renewable goals, bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, SPP market protocol 

requirements, federal and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, emerging 

technologies, and emission reduction plans. 

To determine the extent market prices could support new investments, the MMU analyzed 

the fixed costs, including annual fixed operating and maintenance costs, of five generation 
technologies relative to their potential net revenues,106 at SPP market prices. The generator 

types include scrubbed coal, natural gas combined-cycle, industrial frame natural gas 

106 Net revenue is equal to revenues minus estimated marginal cost.

147

averages and assumptions to estimate profitability of constructing new generation and 

supporting the on-going cost of a new generator. It does not reflect the profitability of any 

specific existing generator. Many costs, such as supply adequacy penalties and individual 

contracts, are not included in this analysis. 

This is a very high-level analysis that uses several assumptions and averages. Daily averages at a 

hub were used for gas prices. A single gas hub was assumed for each region. These gas prices 

did not account for bilateral trades, hedges or any additional charges not represented by the 

hub price. Energy prices used were daily unweighted averages across the footprint. Figure 4–36 

provides the cost assumptions for a new generator. A capital recovery factor of 12.6% was used 

in the annual fixed operating and maintenance cost component.

Overwhelmingly, the conclusions do not vary geographically, despite differing energy prices and 

fuel costs. Historically, the SPS region has had higher net revenues than other regions for gas 

resources. This is likely because combined-cycle plants and combustion turbines in the Permian 

Basin (West Texas) region consistently experience below average natural gas prices. In 2025, 

these gas prices were around $0.64/MMBtu. This resulted in net revenue covering the cost of 

new entry for SPS. 

Based on these results, the MMU expects the market to continue to signal the retirement of 

some coal generation while also not signaling the long-term investment of other types of new 

generation. Whether or not coal generation actually retires is subject to a wide range of factors. 

If there were a decrease in overall available capacity—along with observed higher outages—it 

could present challenges for reliability. 

External economic decisions can provide additional impetus needed for new generation 

investments, such as increases in SPP’s planning reserve margin, the expansion of corporate 

State of the Market 2025 

150 

 
 
 
 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Market prices and costs 

renewable goals, SPP out-of-market payments, bilateral contracts, purchase power agreements, 

SPP market protocol requirements, federal and/or state incentives, state-regulated investments, 

emerging technologies, and/or emission reduction plans. However, market prices, by 

themselves, have mostly not signaled new generation entry since the inception of the Integrated 

Marketplace. Other revenue streams for value added could change this conclusion. For instance, 

resources could be paid to ensure their generation is highly dependable, or resources could be 

paid to remain available for specific performance requirements in the short to medium 

timeframe. As the market is currently designed, it does not directly incentivize new entry for 

energy capacity.  

Out-of-market actions by SPP operators, and the resultant make-whole payments, for reliability 

(manual) commitments reflect some of the symptomatic issues. If these make-whole payments 

were represented in a market price for energy or ancillary services, then the out-of-market 

payments would likely decrease, and market-based prices would likely increase. The increase in 

market prices could support sufficient net revenue for all capacity resources with marginal costs 
below that of the marginal unit[s] setting price in any interval.
