Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

5  CONGESTION AND TRANSMISSION CONGESTION 

RIGHTS MARKET 

This chapter reviews transmission congestion in the SPP market footprint, as well as the 

transmission congestion rights market in the Integrated Marketplace. Key points from this 

chapter include: 

•  The areas that experienced the highest congestion costs in 2025 were located in 

Northern and Southern Oklahoma, where annual costs were primarily driven by specific 

periods during the year with high congestion values. These specific periods included 

October and November 2025 for some constraints with the highest shadow price values, 

along with January and February 2025 for others. Overall, high congestion values were 

less geographically dispersed in 2025 relative to 2024, with seven of the ten highest-

valued constraints by average shadow price located in Oklahoma. Constraints outside of 

Oklahoma included in the top ten list of average constraints by shadow price include the 

Edwardsville transformer constraint in Kansas City, the FORTIEHANWAH market to 

market constraint in southeast North Dakota, and the Franklin transformer constraint in 

central Iowa. Constraints from previous years in northwest North Dakota and southeast 

New Mexico had lower average shadow price values in 2025. 

•  Fewer intervals in the day-ahead market experienced a breached constraint in 2025 

relative to 2024, with 0.5% of day-ahead intervals experiencing a breached constraint in 

2025 and 2.1% of day ahead intervals experiencing a breached constraint in 2024. In the 

real-time market, 2025 had more uncongested intervals than 2024, with 5.7% of intervals 

having no congestion in 2025 and 3.5% having no congestion in 2024. 

•  The 2024 Frequently Constrained Area (FCA) analysis identified Oklahoma City, Tulsa, 

Lubbock, and Kansas City as FCAs. The Williston, North Dakota area was recommended 

for removal as an FCA. The period covered by the 2024 FCA analysis is March 1, 2024, 

through February 28. 2025.  

• 

In aggregate, load-serving entities were over-hedged with TCR and ARR funding at 158% 

of their total congestion cost. Non-load-serving entities only hedged 55% of their total 

congestion cost. 

•  Total congestion rent for 2025 was over $1.6 billion, down from $1.9 billion in 2024 but 

up from $1.4 billion in 2023. 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

•  Transmission congestion rights (TCR) funding in 2025 once again fell outside the target 

range of 90% to 100%, coming in at 85%, up from the 83% seen in 2024 but down from 

the 88% seen in 2023. The total TCR shortfall for 2025 was $358 million, a decrease of 

20% from the $449 million net shortfall seen in 2024. 

•  Auction revenue right (ARR) funding increased to 128%, up from 125% in 2024 and 118% 

in 2023. Relatedly, the ARR surplus increased by just over $58 million year over year. 

5.1  TRANSMISSION CONGESTION  

The locational marginal price (LMP) for approximately 1,300 settlement locations in the SPP 

market reflects the sum of three components: 

1)   marginal energy component (MEC) - system-wide marginal cost of the energy 

required to serve the market, 

2)   marginal congestion component (MCC) - the marginal cost of any increase or 

decrease in energy at a location with respect to transmission constraints, and 

3)   marginal loss component (MLC) - the marginal cost of any increase or decrease in 

energy to minimize system transmission losses. 

𝐿𝑀𝑃 = 𝑀𝐸𝐶 + 𝑀𝐶𝐶 + 𝑀𝐿𝐶 

LMPs are a key feature of electricity markets that ensure the efficient scheduling, commitment, 

and dispatch of generation given the system load and reliability constraints. LMPs also provide 

price signals to efficiently incentivize future generation and transmission investment and to help 

guide retirement decisions. 

This section focuses on the congestion and loss components of price and related items 

including: 

•  geographic pattern of congestion and losses, 

•  changes in the transmission system that alter congestion patterns, 

•  congestion impacts on local market power, 

• 

load-serving entities hedging congestion costs in the transmission congestion rights 

market, and 

•  distribution of marginal congestion and loss amounts. 

State of the Market 2025 

153 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

  PRICING PATTERNS AND CONGESTION  

Figure 5–1 shows price contour maps representing the day-ahead and real-time average prices 

in 2025. 

Figure 5–1  Price map, day-ahead and real-time market 

Annual average day-ahead market prices ranged from around $11/MWh in western Kansas and 

western Oklahoma up to $80/MWh and above in eastern New Mexico, eastern Oklahoma, 

southwest Missouri, and eastern New Mexico. Lower average day-ahead prices tend to coincide 

with pricing nodes associated with wind generators in locations such as those to the west of 

Oklahoma City and in central Kansas. Just over 39% of settlement locations had an annual 

average day-ahead market price between $20/MWh and $30/MWh. 

In the day-ahead market, settlement location average prices ranged from $12/MWh to 

$130/MWh, and in the real-time market, settlement location average prices ranged from 

$5/MWh to $127/MWh. Transmission buildout has allowed higher levels of low-cost wind 

State of the Market 2025 

154 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

generation in the western parts of the SPP footprint to serve load centers located in the eastern 

portions of SPP.  

  CONGESTION BY GEOGRAPHIC LOCATION  

The major drivers of congestion patterns in SPP are the physical characteristics of the 

transmission grid and associated transfer capability, the geographic distribution of load, the 

geographic differences in fuel costs, and external flows from neighboring areas. The eastern side 

of the SPP footprint, with a higher concentration of load, also has a higher concentration of 

high-voltage (345 kV) transmission lines. Historically, high-voltage connections between the 

west and east have been limited; transmission buildout has resulted in most congestion 

occurring on the southeastern edge of the SPP footprint.  

The cost of coal-fired generation increases as transportation costs rise. For example, 

transportation cost increases with distance from the Wyoming Powder River Basin near the 

northwest corner of SPP’s footprint. This is important because even though it is declining over 

time, coal still accounted for 21% of SPP’s installed capacity and 29% of energy generation in 

2025. 

Natural gas-fired generation, SPP’s largest fuel type by installed capacity (36% in 2025), resides 

predominantly in the southern portions of SPP. Wind-powered generation generally lies in the 

western half of the footprint, and nuclear generation resides near the center, while the majority 

of hydro is in the north. 

Figure 5–2 depicts the average marginal congestion component for the day-ahead market 

across the SPP footprint in 2025. 

State of the Market 2025 

155 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

Figure 5–2  Marginal congestion cost map, day-ahead market 

The lowest average day-ahead marginal congestion costs occurred around western Kansas, 

western Oklahoma, and Nebraska. These regions averaged around −$25/MWh. The highest 

average day-ahead marginal congestion costs exist in two spots in Oklahoma, one near the 

Kansas border and one near the Texas border, averaging nearly $50/MWh. Other areas with high 

congestion costs include southeast North Dakota, eastern New Mexico, and southwest Missouri. 

Congestion remains on the southeastern edge of SPP extending from northern Missouri to 

southern Oklahoma which signifies the boundary between locations of renewable and 

conventional generation. This creates a general northwest to southeast split in prices. This 

general northwest to southeast pricing split does exist with congestion in eastern Oklahoma. 

However, some constraints located in eastern North Dakota, the Nebraska panhandle, and the 

Omaha area create congestion pockets that act as exceptions to this trend. Additionally, 

constraints in eastern New Mexico created a large congestion pocket in 2024 and 2025 that was 

not present previously.

  TRANSMISSION CONSTRAINTS 

Market congestion reflects the economic dispatch cost of honoring transmission constraints. 

SPP uses these constraints to manage the flow of energy across the physical bottlenecks of the 

grid in the least costly manner while ensuring reliability. In doing so, SPP calculates a shadow 

price for each constraint, which shows the potential reduction in the total market production 

costs through re-dispatching the system when incrementally relaxing the transmission line limit.

  GEOGRAPHY AND MARGINAL LOSSES 

Variable transmission line losses decrease with increased line voltage or decreased line length 

for the same amount of power moved. In the SPP footprint, much of the low-cost generation 

resides at a distance from the load and with limited high-voltage interconnection. The average 

variable losses on the SPP system for 2025 were 2.7% in the day-ahead market. This is down 

State of the Market 2025 

166 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

slightly from 2.8% in 2023 and 2.9% in 2023. The marginal loss component of the price captures 

the change in the total cost system of losses with an additional increment of load at a particular 

location relative to the reference bus. 

Figure 5–17 maps the annual average day-ahead market marginal loss components for 2025.

2025 Average 

Day-ahead MLC 

The average day-ahead marginal loss component ranges from about −$4.24/MWh in western 

Nebraska into Wyoming to $5.22/MWh in Oklahoma. Negative values reduce prices through the 

marginal loss component relative to the marginal energy cost. Positive values increase prices as 

generation from these locations are more beneficial from a marginal loss perspective. The 

$9.47/MWh spread between geographic prices in 2025 is wider than the $7.38/MWh spread 

from 2024.

  FREQUENTLY CONSTRAINED AREAS AND LOCAL MARKET 

POWER 

Congestion in the market creates geographic areas where only a limited number of suppliers 

can provide the energy to serve load in an area without overloading a constrained transmission 

element. Under these circumstances, the pivotal suppliers have local market power and the 

ability to raise prices above competitive levels or reduce output below optimal levels. SPP’s tariff 

provides provisions for mitigating the impact of local market power on prices, and the 

effectiveness of market power mitigation is described in Section 6.2.2. Local market power can 

be either transitory, as is frequently the case with an outage, or persistent, when a particular area 

is frequently import-constrained. 

Since the SPP tariff calls for more stringent market power mitigation for frequently constrained 

areas, the MMU analyzes market data at least annually to assess the appropriateness of the 
frequently constrained area designations. The 2024 study112 period was March 1, 2024, through 

February 28, 2025, and identified a total of four areas as frequently constrained. The four 

frequently constrained areas identified by the MMU are Oklahoma City, Oklahoma, Tulsa, 

Oklahoma, Lubbock, Texas, and Kansas City, Kansas. One previous frequently constrained area, 

Williston, North Dakota, was recommended for removal. The number of binding hours, pivotal 

suppliers, and congestion increased from the 2023 study to the 2024 study. The MMU is 

currently running the 2025 Frequently Constrained Area study. 

  MARKET CONGESTION MANAGEMENT 

In optimizing the flow of energy to serve the load at the least cost, the SPP market makes 

extensive use of the available transmission up to constraint limits. When constraints reach their 

limits, they are considered binding. The market occasionally allows transmission lines to exceed 
their rating if the redispatch cost to correct the overload becomes too high.113 This is considered 

a breached constraint. Figure 5–18 highlights day-ahead market binding, breached, and 

uncongested intervals.

  CONGESTION PAYMENTS AND UPLIFTS  

Market participants in the energy market incur congestion costs and receive congestion 

payments based on their marginal impact on total market congestion cost through the marginal 

congestion component of price. Most SPP market participants owning physical assets are 

vertically integrated, so their net congestion cost depends on two things. The first is whether 

they are a net buyer or seller of energy. The second is the relative marginal cost component at 

their generation and load. For financial market participants, congestion costs reflect the impact 

of virtual positions on a binding or breached constraint in the day-ahead and real-time markets. 

114 Excluding market-to-market constraints

5.2  CONGESTION HEDGING MARKET 

In the Integrated Marketplace, the locational marginal prices assessed to load are generally 

higher than the locational marginal prices assessed to generators. The largest portion of this 

price difference is almost always attributed to congestion. This is an expected outcome and 

central to the design of nodal electricity markets. The difference between what SPP collects and 

pays out from virtuals, import/exports, loads and generators is often referred to as congestion 

rent. SPP remains revenue neutral in all Integrated Marketplace transactions and therefore must 
allocate the congestion rent back to the market participants.118 The congestion hedging market 
is the mechanism used to allocate day-ahead congestion over-collections.119  

  MARKET DESIGN 

Market participants participate in the congestion hedging market by obtaining auction revenue 

rights and/or transmission congestion rights. Auction revenue rights begin as entitlements 

associated with long-term, firm transmission service reservations. These transmission service 

reservations are a revenue source for transmission owners and an expense for transmission 

customers. More specifically, transmission owners receive revenues from transmission customers 

for building and maintaining the transmission lines, and transmission customers pay the 

transmission owners for the use of the lines by way of the charges associated with transmission 
service reservations.120 

Auction revenue rights link the transmission service, which provides physical rights, to the 

Integrated Marketplace by converting these to financial rights. SPP verifies transmission service 

entitlements, which become candidate auction revenue rights. To obtain auction revenue rights, 

market participants nominate candidate auction revenue rights in the annual auction which 

awards revenue rights from June to May. If the nominations pass the allocation’s simultaneous 

feasibility test, the candidate auction revenue rights become auction revenue rights. The 

simultaneous feasibility test ensures that the market’s aggregate nomination of auction revenue 
rights does not violate thermal constraint limits under a single contingency.121 The test 

incorporates information from the network model, which aids SPP in aligning the supply of 

auction revenue rights with the capacity of the underlying transmission system. The 

simultaneous feasibility test aims to ensure the revenues generated from the congestion right 

auction will sufficiently fund the quantity of auction revenue rights nominated. If a candidate 

118 Schedule 1A fees are the exception. They are not redistributed through the market.  
119 With respect to day-ahead congestion rent only. 
120 These charges are assessed through transmission settlements and include various tariff schedules. 
121 SPP Open Access Transmission Tariff, Section 7.2.2, Simultaneous Feasibility. 

State of the Market 2025 

173 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

auction revenue right nomination fails the simultaneous feasibility test, this reduces the quantity 

of auction revenue rights successfully converted from candidate rights. 

Once market participants have successfully nominated their candidates into auction revenue 

rights, they must choose to either hold their auction revenue right or convert it into a 
transmission congestion right through a process known as self-conversion.122 If a market 

participant holds their auction revenue right, they will receive, or pay, a stream of unchanged 

cash flows over the life of the product. The size and direction of the cash flow depends on the 

market’s collective assessment of the congestion rent along the auction revenue right path as 

assessed by prices during the transmission congestion right auction. If a market participant 

believes that the auction prices will undervalue the congestion rent associated with their auction 

revenue right, the market participant will likely self-convert. When a market participant self-

converts, their auction revenue right becomes a transmission congestion right, which means 

their cash flow is subject to fluctuations in day-ahead market congestion rents.  

Financial-only123 market participants participate alongside traditional utilities in the transmission 

congestion right auctions to provide additional liquidity and price discovery. All participants 

compete on price for the residual network capacity. The auction software attempts to maximize 

auction revenue without violating the simultaneous feasibility test. This test helps align the 

supply of transmission congestion rights with the residual capacity of the underlying 

transmission system. If a transmission congestion right bid fails the simultaneous feasibility test, 

the quantity of transmission congestion rights successfully obtained will be reduced to the point 

where the bid no longer violates the test. Once a market participant obtains a transmission 

congestion right, they can hold it through to settlement, offer it for sale in a subsequent auction, 

or transact on the bulletin board outside of the auction cycle. The overwhelming majority of 

positions are held through to settlement. 

In addition to auction revenue rights, SPP may also award long-term transmission congestion 

rights. Much like auction revenue rights, these are awarded based on transmission ownership. 

However, long-term transmission congestion rights require the ownership of transmission with 

roll-over rights, and the ownership must span the entire TCR year. Long-term transmission 

congestion rights must also pass a simultaneous feasibility study, similar to auction revenue 

rights. Awarded long-term transmission congestion rights are automatically converted to 

transmission congestion rights for the entire year and settled in the same manner as 

conventional transmission congestion rights. These long-term congestion rights, once originally 

awarded, stay in effect until either the underlying transmission in relinquished or the owner of

the long-term transmission congestion rights removes the nomination. SPP limits the total 

amount of long-term transmission congestion rights awarded each auction to 50% of the 

available transmission capacity.

Most load-serving entities awarded TCR megawatts are awarded through long-term congestion 

rights and the self-conversion of auction revenue rights. This means these TCRs are acquired 

without direct cost. This is the main reason for the disparity in load-serving entities and non-

load serving entities profit per megawatt. In addition, ARR holders are entitled to excess auction 

revenue paid in by entities bidding into the TCR market. This also inflates the profits for load-

serving entities when comparing them to non-load serving entities. In 2025, load-serving entities 

profited $7.00 per awarded TCR awarded megawatt, while non-load serving entities only 
profited $0.18 per megawatt.125  This is a decrease from the $7.24 and $0.38 per megawatt seen 

in 2024 respectively by load-serving and non-load serving entities.  

The TCR revenue per megawatt represents the revenues that the TCR holder received on a per 

megawatt basis. As can be seen in the chart, load-serving entities received $5.30/TCRMW in 

2025 compared to non-load serving entities that only received $1.32/TCRMW. This is down from 

2024 where load-serving entities received $5.97/TCRMW and non-load serving entities received 

$1.60/TCRMW.  The disparity in the revenues per TCR megawatt between load-serving entities 

and non-load serving entities is likely attributed to the fact that load-serving entities acquire the

more congested paths through the purchase of firm transmission service rights, leaving the less 

congested paths available for non-load serving entities to bid on.  

  Bidding behaviors 

As noted above, in aggregate, load-serving entities received slightly more revenue from their 

congestion hedges than they paid in day-ahead and real-time congestion cost. However, on the 

individual participant level, some load-serving entities under-hedged while others over-hedged.  

Figure 5–25 shows, by load-serving market participant, the day-ahead congestion exposure 

along with the value of auction revenue rights and transmission congestion rights as well as the 

net overall position.  

Figure 5–25  Net congestion revenue by market participant 

The figure highlights that the majority of load-serving participants received positive net 

revenues, while 20% of participants held portfolios that did not cover their day-ahead 

congestion costs. For instance, the bottom five participants paid $52 million more in congestion 

costs than was offset by their auction revenue right and transmission congestion right positions. 

This is much less than the $181 million paid by the bottom five participants in the 2024 calendar 

year, but not much less than the $79 million paid by the bottom five in 2023. 

The range of participant outcomes is influenced by three main factors: hedging need, individual 

participant bidding behavior, and the market’s collective bidding behavior. With respect to 

hedging needs, each participant experiences varying levels of congestion exposure mostly 

related to geographic location and type of physical interconnection. The various levels of 

congestion exposure leads to different hedging needs among market participants. 

State of the Market 2025 

177 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

The bidding behavior of the individual participant affects the auction revenue rights they receive 

through the allocation. Participants can, and do, employ numerous strategies with varying 

degrees of success.  

The bidding behavior of the other market participants, as a whole, affects the ability of each and 

every other market participant to obtain hedges through the auction revenue right allocation. 

More specifically, if a transaction is physically feasible with respect to transmission service, and 

by extension the day-ahead market, it does not necessarily mean the transaction will also be 

feasible in the auction revenue right allocation. The issue arises because the transmission 
system’s capacity, represented by transmission service requests,126 includes both prevailing flow 

and counter-flow transactions. However, participants often choose not to nominate their 

counter-flow candidate auction revenue rights in the allocation process, in part, because these 

positions tend to carry negative cash flows. These incentives motivate individual participants to 

abstain from counter-flow positions.  By not nominating all candidate auction revenue rights, 

the capacity in the allocation will not match the capacity of the physical system. Because the 

basis of the auction revenue right is transmission service, if the two capacities do not align, a 

participant’s auction revenue right may not perfectly hedge their transmission service and their 

related day-ahead market activity.  

Differences between outages modeled in the auction processes and day-ahead market can also 

affect market participants’ ability to obtain hedges. Details on outage modeling are discussed 

below. 

  Transmission outage modeling 

When there are outages in the day-ahead market that were not in the transmission congestion 

rights auction, there is a reduction in day-ahead system capacity, which can cause underfunding. 

Figure 5–26 shows transmission outages by reported lead time. 

126 Long-term, firm, transmission service requests 

State of the Market 2025 

178 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

Figure 5–26  Transmission outages by reporting lead time 

SPP models only transmission outages that were reported at least 45 days prior to the first of 
the month in the transmission congestion rights auction.127 Prior to August 2024, SPP only 

required transmission owners to submit planned outages 14 days in advance. Now outages 

greater than 100 kV with durations over five days must submit planned outages 45 days before 
the calendar month in which the outage occurs.128 Late outages are still permitted, but this rule 

change still seems to have resulted in some behavior changes. Since August 2024, about 68% of 

planned outages with 5-day or greater duration were submitted at least 45 days in advance. This 

is an increase compared to the period of January 2023 to July 2024, when only 58% were 

submitted in that timeframe. The MMU will continue to monitor this trend and make future 

recommendations as needed. The above figure shows that the majority of outages in 2025 

would not have been considered in the transmission congestion rights markets based solely on 

the submission lead time.  

Figure 5–27 shows the duration in days for the different types of transmission outages.  

127 Market Participant Guide: SPP 2026 Congestion Hedging available at

128 SPP Outage Coordination Methodology, Section 2.3

179

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

Figure 5–27  Transmission outages by duration 

Outages shorter than five days are excluded from auction revenue right/transmission congestion 

right processes. The end result is that the majority of outages were excluded from the 

transmission congestion rights models because they are less than five days or were not reported 

in the time allowed to be included in the transmission congestion rights models. Other markets 

use different outage selection criteria; it may be advisable in the future to consider adopting 

some of those changes (such as always including outages of 345kV or above, regardless of 

duration) in order to improve congestion funding percentages. 

Figure 5–29 shows outages by real-time, day-ahead, and transmission congestion right markets.  

Figure 5–28  Transmission outages by market 

While the number of outages in the day-ahead and real-time are similar, the outages 

represented in the transmission congestion rights market are only a fraction of the total number 

of outages. The transmission congestion market only includes outages that are longer than five 

State of the Market 2025 

180 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

days and are submitted at least 45 days in advance of the first of the month. This represented 

only a small share of the total outages in the day-ahead market. These differences in outages 

can create underfunding of transmission congestion rights. 

Ideally, outages in the transmission congestion rights markets would be perfectly aligned with 

the day-ahead market. However, the MMU understands the challenges associated with 

accounting for outages in the transmission congestion rights market and recognizes that there 

can never be an exact match among the markets. Even so, improving how outages are handled 

and accounted for in the auction processes could help to reduce underfunding. The MMU has 

made recommendations for SPP to improve how outages are accounted for in the transmission 
congestion right auction process to improve the congestion hedging market results.129

revenue right closeout increased to 128% in 2025. This is up from 125% in 2024 and 118% in 

2023. The overfunding of auction revenue rights is primarily a function of the sales of TCRs on 

non-ARR paths.

INTRA-AUCTION SALES AND BULLETIN BOARD 

TRANSACTIONS 

Intra-auction sales refer to the sale of a previously acquired transmission congestion right 

position in a subsequent auction. Bulletin board transactions refer to trades where a market 

participant buys or sells a position outside of the auction cycle through the SPP bulletin board. 

Overall, both inter-auction sales and bulletin board transactions remain low. 

Figure 5–33 shows the transaction volume by type as a percentage of all transmission 

congestion right purchase volume.  

State of the Market 2025 

184 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

Figure 5–33  Intra-auction sales and bulletin board transactions 

No bulletin board transactions cleared in 2025, just like 2024 and 2018 through 2021. A small 

number cleared in 2022 and 2023. In both of those years the bulletin board transactions were 

less than 0.05% of all congestion right transactions. Intra-auction sales remained low at 7% of 

the total transmission congestion right volume. This is in line with 2024, but an increase from 

2023.  

Outside of their relationship to the auction cycle, these transactions also differ from each other 

in another material way. Bulletin board transactions are similar to the secondary equity market, 

where a share of stock is offered for sale and that same share is later purchased by another 

market participant. As such, the bulletin board transactions do not affect total supply; they only 

affect ownership of the existing supply.  

However, intra-auction sales can affect supply in addition to ownership. When market 

participants offer their prevailing flow positions for sale intra-auction, the capacity of those 

positions once used is available to the market again. But the newly available system capacity can 

be taken up by any path, not just the path sold intra-auction. Furthermore, to sell counter-flow 

positions intra-auction, unclaimed prevailing flow capacity must exist for the transaction to 
clear.133 This is because counter-flow intra-auction sales reduce the total capacity available. The 

counter-flow now offered for sale previously facilitated other prevailing flow positions. If the 

counter-flow sale were to clear without considering supply, the prevailing flow once facilitated 

by this counter-flow would no longer be feasible. So, in order for these existing prevailing flow 

transactions to remain feasible, additional prevailing flow capacity must exist. Practically, the 

additional prevailing flow capacity required plays the same role once played by the counter-flow 

133 The additional capacity could also be provided by another counter-flow intra-auction bid.  

State of the Market 2025 

185 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Congestion and transmission congestion rights market 

being offered for sale. These circumstances likely also incentivize market participant abstentions 

from counter-flow. Generally, if a market participant holds a counter-flow position, it could be 

very difficult to sell the position intra-auction, which is the main source of intra-marketplace 
liquidity.134 

134 Intra-market refers to inside the SPP Integrated Marketplace.

Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment 

6  COMPETITIVE ASSESSMENT 

Chapter 6 of this report provides a competitive assessment of the SPP market. Key points from 

this chapter include: 

•  Structural and behavioral metrics indicate that the SPP markets have been competitive 

over the last several years. 

• 

In 19.5% of the hours in 2025, the market share of the largest on-line supplier in terms of 

real time energy output exceeded 20%, breaking the declining trend observed since the 

June 2018 formation of Evergy, Inc. as SPP’s largest market participant. 

•  The Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI) market concentration analysis shows that 2% of 

hours were considered moderately concentrated in 2025, the same amount as in 2024. 

The SPP market remained mostly unconcentrated—with 90% of all intervals since 2019 

considered unconcentrated. No intervals were considered highly concentrated in 2025, 

and, like 2024, the percentage of intervals considered unconcentrated (98%) remain at its 

highest levels since 2017. 

•  The results of the pivotal supplier analyses indicate that the percent of hours with a 

pivotal supplier is the highest in two zones—New Mexico/West Texas and 

Iowa/Dakotas/Montana—with nearly all intervals possessing pivotal suppliers regardless 

of demand. This has been the case for the last several years for the New Mexico and 

West Texas region.  

•  Off-peak and on-peak annual average markups for marginal resources continued to be 

in negative territory in 2025. The lowest markups occurred in off-peak hours of the 

shoulder months when wind generation was generally the highest. 

•  There were slightly higher markups in 2025 than in 2024 in on-peak and off-peak hours. 

Compared with 2024, average offer markups in 2025 had some increasing trends at both 

off-peak and on-peak average annual markups, but they were still at negative levels at 

around −$5.94/MWh and −$3.15/MWh, respectively. 

•  Positive markups were observed for coal at slightly increasing levels in 2025 relative to 

the positive markups seen in 2024. Negative on-peak and off-peak markups were 

observed most summer months for natural gas resources in 2025, with a clear correlation 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment 

to the prevailing price of gas within the SPP footprint.  All months saw negative markups 

for wind resources, with markup levels continuing to stay low throughout the years.  

• 

Incremental energy offer mitigation in 2025 slightly increased in frequency in the real-

time market however, incremental, energy offer mitigation continues to remain very rare, 

at 0.04% and 0.05% of resource hours in the day-ahead and real-time markets, 

respectively. Overall, mitigation frequency levels remain very low at less than 0.75% 

across energy, no-load, and operating reserve intervals. 

•  Behavioral metrics suggest that attempts to actually exercise market power by 

manipulating the price (economic withholding) or quantity (physical withholding) of 

generation are rare. The output gap, an inference of economically withheld generation, 

dropped by about 0.1 percentage point in 2025.  

The SPP Integrated Marketplace provides sufficient market incentives to produce competitive 

market outcomes in regions and periods lacking local market power. The MMU’s competitive 

assessment provides evidence that market outcomes were workably competitive in 2025, 

requiring only infrequent mitigation of local market power. 

The market power analysis in this report considers both structural and behavioral aspects of 

market power concerns. Structural aspects are examined with techniques such as market share 

analysis, market-wide concentration indices, and pivotal supplier analysis. These structural 

indicators illuminate the potential for market power without regard to the actual exercise of 

market power. Behavioral analyses, on the other hand, look for the exercise of market power by 

assessing the actual offer or bid conduct of market participants, and the impact of that conduct 

on market prices. These analyses examined offer price markup, economic withholding, the 

frequency of automated mitigation, uneconomic production, and physical withholding. 

This chapter evaluates the SPP market’s competitive environment by establishing the level of 

structural market power and then examining market prices for indications of the exercise of that 

power. Structural market power is assessed both at the SPP footprint level through supplier 

concentration indices and market share levels, and at the local (transmission-constrained) level 

through pivotal supplier analysis. In the SPP markets, mitigation of economic withholding is 

accomplished ex-ante through automatic market power mitigation processes that limit the 

ability of generators with market power to raise prices above competitive levels. The mitigation 

program is monitored and evaluated to ensure it is efficient and effective. Accordingly, the 

following subsections examine the significance of market power and the effectiveness of local 

market power mitigation in the SPP markets. 

State of the Market 2025 

188 

 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment 

6.1  STRUCTURAL ASPECTS OF MARKET POWER 

Three core metrics of structural market power are the market share analysis, the Herfindahl-

Hirschman Index (HHI), and pivotal supplier analysis. The first two of these indicators measure 

market-wide concentration, ignoring local constraints. Pivotal supplier analysis, on the other 

hand, accounts for the dynamics of power markets and considers changing demand conditions 

and locational transmission constraints in assessing potential market power. 

Figure 6–1 

 displays the market share of the largest on-line supplier in terms of energy 

dispatch in the real-time market by hour for 2025.  

Figure 6–1  Market share of largest supplier 

The market share ranged from 9% to 26%, exceeding the 20% threshold135 in 1,706 hours (19%) 

for the year. In 2024, market shares were also in a similar range from 9% to 25%, with market 

share exceeding the 20% threshold in 14% of all intervals. This disrupts a trend of decreasing 

concentration in previous years. Evergy remains the largest on-line supplier in over 97% of 

intervals. Note that although a mere increase in market share does not itself pose a threat to the 

structural competitiveness of the SPP market, other relevant market data including pivotal 

supplier hours and local market power mitigation must also be closely evaluated for competitive 

assessment (see below). 

135 The 20% threshold is a historically accepted standard for identifying structural market power. Note, 
however, that neither market share nor the HHI metric alone would be sufficient for the assessment of 
market power particularly in today’s spot electricity markets where load pockets formed by transmission 
congestion may lead to market power with much smaller market shares and/or HHI values. 

State of the Market 2025 

189 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment

concentration in the market. It is calculated by using the sum of the squares of the market 

shares of all suppliers in a market as follows:

As shown above, the market remained mostly unconcentrated from the addition of the 
Integrated System to the date of the Great Plains/Westar merger that created Evergy.138,139 Even 

though moderately concentrated hours increased following the creation of Evergy, only 3%, 2%, 

and 2% of hours fell into this category in 2023, 2024, and 2025, respectively. In contrast, in 2014, 

just over 50% of hours were considered moderately concentrated, and in 2015, prior to the 

addition of the Integrated System, nearly 40% of hours were moderately concentrated. Later 

metrics in this report will more closely examine specific market behavior by market participants 
in terms of the exercise of local market power.140 

The MMU’s market share analysis and HHI metrics both indicate a moderate potential for 

general structural market power in SPP markets outside of frequently constrained areas. 

Structural market power is also assessed at a more localized level and in the context of 

locational transmission constraints by reevaluating frequently constrained areas periodically and 

(re)defining them, accordingly, as discussed in Section 5.1.7. 

Pivotal supplier analysis considers the dynamic nature of the power market, particularly variable 

demand conditions, and evaluates the potential for market power in the presence of pivotal 

suppliers. A supplier is pivotal when market demand cannot be met without some or all its 

generation. There may be one or more pivotal suppliers in a particular market defined by 

transmission constraints and load conditions, and a supplier’s pivotal status may vary between 

time periods irrespective of its size. 

The following metric identifies the frequency with which at least one supplier was pivotal in the 
five different reserve zones141 (regions) of the SPP footprint in 2025.142 The frequency with which 

a supplier is pivotal is an indication of their potential to raise prices above competitive levels. 

While the mere size of a supplier does not itself render the supplier pivotal, suppliers which are

almost identical in 2025. 

6.2  BEHAVIORAL ASPECTS OF MARKET POWER 

  OFFER PRICE MARKUP 

In a competitive market, prices should reflect the short-run marginal cost of producing the 

marginal unit. In SPP’s Integrated Marketplace, market participants submit hourly mitigated 

energy offer curves that represent their short-run marginal cost of energy. Market participants 

also submit their market-based offers, which may differ from their mitigated offers. To assess 

market performance, a comparison is made between the market offer and the mitigated offer 

for the marginal resources for each real-time market interval.  

Figure 6–6 provides the average marginal resource offer price markups143 by month for on-peak 

and off-peak periods. Similar to 2024, the MMU observed negative markups on average in 2025.  

143 Offer price markup is calculated as the difference between the market-based offer and the mitigated 
offer where the market-based offer may or may not be equal to the mitigated offer. The markups are 
weighted based on cleared megawatts to reflect each marginal resource’s proportional impact on price.  

State of the Market 2025 

193 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment 

Figure 6–6  Average offer price markup of marginal resource, monthly 

In 2025, average monthly markups ranged from −$14.54 /MWh to $2.31/MWh for off-peak 

periods and from −$15.29/MWh to $3.20/MWh for on-peak periods. The lowest markups 

occurred in off-peak hours of the shoulder months when wind generation was generally the 

highest. We also observed lower markups in off-peak hours than in on-peak hours for most of 

months in 2025, except for August. There was one marginal wind resource that offered −$500 

for their real-time offers and $0 for their mitigated offers, which caused more negative markup 

in both on-peak and off-peak group. This resource was on the margin more in the on-peak 

hours than off-peak hours in August, which led to more negative markups in on-peak hours 

than off-peak hours in August.

hours, but they are still negative.

Figure 6–7  Average marginal resource offer price markup, annual  

In competitive markets, markups should maintain levels around $0/MWh. The MMU monitors 

for markup levels that are either abnormally high or abnormally low. Compared with 2024, 

average offer markups in 2025 had some increasing trends at both off-peak and on-peak 

average annual markups, but they were still at negative levels at around −$5.94/MWh and 

−$3.15/MWh, respectively. 

Although a lower offer price markup level in itself would indicate competitive pressure on 

suppliers in the SPP market, this continuous and intensifying downward trend may raise 

questions about the continued commercial viability of generating units as well as inappropriate 

behavior.144 Despite this trend, SPP continues to add new generation. More renewable 

generation is anticipated to be added over the next few years, which is expected to outweigh 

planned retirements. As such the trend in negative offer-price markups will likely continue, 

which may pressure the economic viability of older generating resources.

Positive markups were observed for coal at slightly increasing levels relative to the positive 

markups seen in 2024. The annual average coal markup levels were $5.81 in 2025, $4.29 in 2024, 

and $6.88 in 2023. Negative on-peak and off-peak markups were observed in most of summer 

months for gas in 2025, with a clear correlation to the prevailing price of gas within the SPP 

footprint.  

Nonetheless, all months saw negative markups for wind resources, with markup levels 

continuing to stay low throughout the years. The average markups for wind resources were 

−$37.81 in 2025, −$37.99 in 2024, and −$41.50 in 2023, respectively.  

There was also a large drop in markup for wind resources in August which was due to one 

marginal wind resource in August that offered −$500 for their real-time offers and $0 for their 

mitigated offers. This led to a −$500 markup on the intervals when the wind resources were on 

the margin. 

Negative markups indicate that many market participants’ real-time market offers were below 
their mitigated offers.145 This could occur when generators decide to offer below their short-run 

marginal cost to: 

•  meet their day-ahead positions in order to avoid financial risk associated with buying 

back energy in the real-time market, 

145 Wind units may have negative mitigated offers as a result of the federal production tax credit (PTC) for 
renewable energy.

•  maintain commitments or reflect temporary negative fuel prices, as in the case of natural 

gas units,146  

•  meet the terms of their bilateral agreements, or  

•  ensure eligibility for production tax credits (PTC), as in the case of wind resources that 

constitute a sizeable share of the total resource portfolio. 

For context, wind resources were on the margin in the real-time market in over 30% of all 

resource intervals over the last three years (Figure 2–23).  

  MITIGATION PERFORMANCE AND FREQUENCY 

SPP resources’ incremental energy, start-up, no-load, and operating reserve offers are subject to 

mitigation for economic withholding when the following three circumstances occur 

simultaneously in a market solution: 

1)  The resource has local market power; 

2)  The offer has failed the conduct test. Resources submit two offers for each product: a 

mitigated offer representing the competitive baseline costs that must adhere to the 

mitigated offer development guidelines147 and a second offer generally referred to as a 

market offer, which often includes risk-based and strategy-based adjustments. An offer is 

considered for mitigation when the market offer exceeds the mitigated offer by more 

than the allowed threshold; and 

3)  The resource either: 

a)  Is manually committed by SPP for capacity, transmission constraint, or voltage 

support; or by a local transmission operator for local transmission problems or 

voltage support; or  

b)  The application of mitigation impacts market prices or make-whole payments by 

more than the allowed $25/MWh threshold. 

146 Tariff rules only allow for submitting monotonically non-decreasing offer curves by market participants, 
and this may result in market offers by natural gas units below their mitigated offers during periods with 
negative prevailing gas prices. Additionally, real-time market offers may be below mitigated offers when 
natural gas units’ mitigated offers are indexed to hub prices when in fact the cost of gas received could be 
below that hub price. Some SPP market participants have experienced negative natural gas prices in the 
past, but it remains a rare occurrence.  
147 As indicated in Appendix G of SPP’s Integrated Market Protocols.

  OUTPUT GAP (MEASURE FOR POTENTIAL ECONOMIC 

WITHHOLDING) 

Economic withholding is defined as submitting a resource offer that is artificially high, such that 

either the resource will not be scheduled or dispatched, or—if scheduled or dispatched—the 

offer will set a higher than competitive market clearing price. Accordingly, the output gap metric 

aims to measure the economic (or competitive) amount of output withheld from the market 

through the submission of offers in excess of competitive levels. The output gap is the amount 

of generation not produced as a result of offers exceeding the mitigated offer above an 

appropriate conduct threshold. The conduct threshold is employed to compensate for any 

inaccuracies or uncertainties in estimating the cost, similar to the one used in economic 

withholding mitigation. In this report, the output gap is calculated as the difference between a 

resource’s economic level of output at the prevailing market clearing price and the greater of 

actual offered MWs and actual amount of production. The economic level of output is produced 
by a generator between its minimum and maximum economic capacity.148  

The MMU employs a 17.5% conduct threshold for the frequently constrained areas and a 25% 

conduct threshold for the rest of the footprint to reflect the actual thresholds used in the 
clearing process’s automatic economic withholding mitigation.149 In order to account for the 

discrepancy between a resource’s offered capacity and the dispatched amount (due to possible 

limitations in real-time market conditions such as transmission constraints, operator actions or 

ramp limitations, and virtual participants), an upward adjustment is made by taking the greater 

of the day-ahead scheduled or the real-time dispatched amount to reflect the actual amount of 

production.  

Note that certain market conditions such as congestion (supplier location), supplier size, or high 

demand can create the potential for exercising market power and can facilitate economic 

withholding behavior. For this reason, the output gap is calculated as percentages of total 

economic output withheld compared to total reference capacity for the SPP footprint. In 

addition, the output gap is calculated for the largest three suppliers (market participant 

portfolios) in each frequently constrained area, and if so designated, comparing levels to those 
of the remaining suppliers. Similar to last year’s report, annual calculations were run for all days 

148 The MMU calculates this metric by including all resources’ total (reference level) capacity when 
calculating output gap percentages. 
149 The 2025 FCA analysis recommended Williston frequently constrained area to be inactivated on October 
1, 2025, and the Oklahoma City and Tulsa FCAs stayed activated in 2025 FCA analysis. (Note that the SPP 
did not deactivate the Williston frequently constrained area until February 2026.) Lubbock, TX and Kansas 
City were added based on the 2025 FCA analysis, Therefore, five frequently constrained areas are shown in 
this 2025 report. 

State of the Market 2025 

200 

 
 
Southwest Power Pool

Competitive assessment 

and evaluated at varying levels of demand as a potential market condition that can affect 

withholding outcomes.

2023 to 2025.  

Figure 6–12  Output gap, monthly 

Compared to the previous year, 2025’s output gap dropped to 0.05% from 0.14% in 2024. 

Overall, the output gap remains at very low levels, averaging less than 0.1% in all months of 

2025 and reflects a high level of participation in the market overall.  

Figure 6–13 displays the output gap calculated by demand level and participant size for the 

entire SPP market footprint, Figure 6–14 shows the output gap for the five frequently 

constrained areas for 2025. There were no observed output gaps in the Williston frequently

Figure 6–14  Output gap, frequently constrained areas 

Though output gaps continue to remain at low levels within the SPP market footprint, higher 

output gap levels were generally observed for the top three largest suppliers at all demand 

periods in 2025. The results indicate a very low level of economic output withheld in the SPP 

footprint. There was a similar pattern observed in the frequently constrained areas with a higher 

level of output gap for the three top suppliers. These outcomes are generally consistent with 

expectations of competitive market conduct. In general, output is more likely to be withheld at 

higher demand levels or by larger suppliers. However, at times, output may also be withheld in 

low load periods as prices are often negative during the lowest 20% of load hours.  

  UNOFFERED GENERATION CAPACITY (MEASURE FOR 

POTENTIAL PHYSICAL WITHHOLDING)  

As part of the competitive assessment, the MMU reviewed potential physical withholding 

behaviors by generators 2023 through 2025. Physical withholding refers to conduct where a 

supplier improperly derates a resource or otherwise does not offer it into the market. Physical 

withholding may include intentionally not following dispatch instructions, declaring false derates 

or outages, refusing to provide offers, or providing inaccurate resource parameters such as 

capability limitations. Any economic generation capacity that is not made available to the 
market through a derate, outage, or by other means is considered for this analysis.150,151 

150 This analysis, in part, draws on “Assessment of the Market Monitoring Metrics for the SPP Energy 
Imbalance Service (EIS) Market,” Potomac Economics, December 2010 and “2016 State of the Market 
Report for the New York ISO Markets,” Potomac Economics, May 2017. 
151 Economic offered capacity is determined in a similar way as in the output gap analysis in Section 6.2.3 
by comparing resource’s (cost-based) mitigated offer to the prevailing locational price.  

State of the Market 2025 

202 

 
 
 
Southwest Power Pool 
Market Monitoring Unit 

Competitive assessment 

Total economic capacity that was derated from respective reference levels was classified by 

reason and duration. Derates can be reflected as planned or forced outages submitted through 
SPP’s outage scheduling system or any undesignated unoffered capacity.152 Any derates from 

reference levels are considered in this analysis. 

Derates were classified as either short-term or long-term. Those of less than seven days’ 

duration were classified as short-term and the rest as long-term. This is because the economic 

capacity that was not offered short-term has more potential for physical withholding relative to 

long-term derates as it would be less costly—because of loss of sales—for a supplier to withhold 

capacity for a short duration of time.  

As in the case for economic withholding, the potential for physical withholding is also affected 

by various market conditions at the time offers are made including location (congestion), 

supplier size, or demand levels. Larger suppliers would be in a more advantageous position to 

exercise market power. During tight market conditions, suppliers have more incentive and 

opportunity to physically withhold capacity for strategic reasons. In addition, scheduling 

maintenance outages in high demand periods may indicate a strategic behavior to create 

artificial shortages.  

The MMU assessed derated and unoffered economic capacity both in the day-ahead and real-

time markets. Similar to the output gap analysis, the MMU modeled commitments based on 
day-ahead market outcomes for all units.153 The unoffered capacity is calculated as the 
difference between the unit’s economic capacity154 and its offered maximum economic capacity 

operating limit during intervals when the unit was deemed economic (i.e., covering its costs 

given the clearing price).  

The following figures show unoffered economic capacity as percent of total resource reference 

levels by month, frequently constrained area, and by supplier (participant) size against varying 
load levels for the SPP footprint.155  

152 The planned maintenance outages by nuclear generation and unoffered capacity by hydro, wind, and 
solar is excluded in this analysis. 
153 Due to the new fast-start logic implemented in 2022. 
154 Bounded by a resource’s reference level. 
155 Unoffered capacity percentages are calculated out of the total reference levels of the corresponding 
market area.

of maintenance outages in 2025. When short- and long-term outages were excluded from the

through 2025, respectively. From an overall market perspective, the results generally indicate
